P R E F A C E 
 
This Report for the year ended March 2021 has been prepared for 
submission to the Governor of Madhya Pradesh  under the 
Comptroller and Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and 
Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. 
The Report contains significant results of the audit of Panchayat i 
Raj Institutions  and Urban Local Bodies in the State  including 
the Departments concerned. 
The issues noticed in the course of test audit for the period 2019-
21 as well as those issues which came to notice in ear lier years 
but could not be dealt with in  the previous Reports  have also 
been included, wherever necessary. 
The audit has been conducted in conformity with auditing 
standards issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of 
India. 

  
 
OVERVIEW 
 

 

 
OVERVIEW 
This Report contains five chapters. The first Chapter provides an overview of the 
functioning, accountability mechanism and financial reporting issues  of 
Panchayati Raj Institutions (PRIs)  and fourth chapter provides an overview of 
functioning o f Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) . The second Chapter contains two 
Performance Audit report s on PRIs. The third and fifth Chapter contain four 
Compliance Audit reports, one  on PRIs  and three on ULBs . A summary of 
important findings is presented in this overview.  
Chapter I: An Overview of the Functioning , Accountability Mechanism and 
Financial Reporting Issues of the Panchayati Raj Institutions 
• Annual Audit Plans of Local Fund Auditor (LFA) for the year 2019-21 were 
forwarded with delay ranged between one  and five months to Principal 
Accountant General (Audit-I) Madhya Pradesh 1. Further, LFA acted only on 
36 IRs Inspection Reports ( IRs), out of 102 IRs of LFA  sent and  vetted in 
office of Principal Accountant General (Audit -I) Madhya Pradesh  during 
2019-21. 
(Paragraph 1.4.1) 
• Despite letters  regarding submission of compliance/ remedial action taken 
against outstanding audit paragraphs  issued to ZPs/JPs, 11,726 paragraphs of 
2,693 IRs of Principal Accountant General  (Audit) were outstanding for 
settlement as of 31st March 2021.  
(Paragraph 1.5) 
• Required number of Social audits were not conducted  (only 36,995 out of 
required 91,236) in GPs  during 2019 -21. Further, 2286 Social Audit  issues  
(` 1.11 crore) of 17 districts remained for settlement. 
(Paragraph 1.6) 
• As per accepted recommendation of 3rd SFC by State Government , Finance 
Department did not devolve  State Finance Commission  (SFC) grants of  
` 2440.73 crore to PRIs during 2019-21.   
(Paragraph 1.7.2) 
• PRIs could not utilise funds allotted by the State Government a nd the saving 
was between 5 and 24 per cent during 2019-21. 
        (Paragraph 1.7.3)

2.1   Implementation of 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act 
• None of the functions, funds and functionaries of test -checked four 
departments were devolved. Furthe r, four departments (other than test -
checked departments) took back devolved functions, fund s and functionaries 
from PRIs during 1998-2014. 
(Paragraph 2.1.7.1 and 2.1.7.2) 
• GoMP constituted the 3rd, 4th and 5th SFC with a delay of seven months, one 
and half year,  and one month 23  days respectively. We also noted that the  
3rd and 4th SFC submitted their reports with a delay of two and half years and 
four years respectively. 
(Paragraph 2.1.8.1) 
• Despite availability of adequate funds,  trainings were not imparted to  
70 per cent  of Elected Representatives (ERs) and 97 per cent  of officials, 
against the target of training of 3.92 lakh ERs and 28,150 officials of PRIs.  
(Paragraph 2.1.8.3)                                                                                                                     
• 35 per cent (71 out of required 200) of DPC meetings were held in selected 
districts during 2016 -21. None of the DPCs of selected districts prep ared 
District Development Plan by consolidating the  development plans of 
panchayats and urban local bodies.                                                                                                                                        
(Paragraph 2.1.8.4)                                                                                                                            
• Out of total receipt of ` 20,985.04 crore, the PRIs could generate only two per 
cent and the remaining receipts were grant from the SFC and CFC.                                                                                                                 
(Paragraph 2.1.9.1)                                                                                                                                
• GoMP created Grant No. 53 for providing budget to PRIs. Finance 
Department through Grant No. 53 allotted funds  of ` 79,693.06 crore to 
PRDD/PRIs and ` 43542.26 crore to BCOs of other depa rtments, for 
operating 33 to 42 and 32 to 57 schemes related to 29 functions as mentioned 
in 11th schedule of the constitution during 2016-21, leading to inadequate and 
ineffective functions and funds devolution to PRIs.  
(Paragraph 2.1.9.2)                                                                                                                                
• Except 2016-17, GoMP did not allot four  per cent of divisible tax revenue to 
PRIs and the allotment steadily declined from three to less than  
one per cent during 2017-21. 
(Paragraph 2.1.9.5)

imposed by the Department for t aking up new works until circulation of new

through treasury system were kept in the bank accounts along with the funds 
of other schemes, resultantly, interest earned on the scheme fund c ould not be 
bifurcated due to non -maintenance of separate ledger and account for SFC 
grants by ZPs/JPs. 
(Paragraphs 3.3) 
•   SFC grants were released without ensuring their utilization. Only five ZPs/JPs 
out of 14 selected ZPs/JPs submitted total 12 UCs out of the required 42 UCs 
for the year 2017-2020.  
(Paragraphs 3.3.1) 
•   Out of 163 selected infrastructure works , only 31 works  were included i n 
GPDP and administrative sa nctions issued f or remaining 132 works by the 
concerned ZPs/JPs were against the scheme guidelines. 
(Paragraphs 3.4.1) 
•   Only 1 4 works ( 8.6 per cent ) out of sele cted 163  works, were completed 
within prescribed time period.  Delay occurred in completion of 68 works 
could not be ascertained due to non - mentioning of completion period in 
Administrative Sanction and completion certificate of the works as well, led 
to no control of department over the delays in the completion of works.  
(Paragraphs 3.4.2) 
• Quality tests wer e not conducted in 88 per cent  (143 out of 163) works 
amounting to ` 9.12 crore. Detailed measurement of 79 works was not found 
recorded in Measurement Book. 
(Paragraphs 3.4.3 & 3.4.4)

Chapter IV:   An overview of the functioning of Urban Local Bodies  
• Urban Local Bodies are to generate perform ance repo rts on  Service Level 
Benchmarks periodically  and institutionalise systems for perf ormance 
management. However, Audit found that 312  (77.81 per cent ) out of 401 
Urban Local Bodies were yet to meet the target of achieving 100 per cent  
water connections. 151 Urban Local Bodies (37.66 per cent) failed t o me et 
the target set for achieving Co verage of toilets.  248 (61.85 per cent) and 207 
(51.62 per cent ) Urban Local Bodies failed to meet the targets set for 
segregation of solid waste and obtaining reuse d/recycled material from solid 
waste respectively. 
(Paragraph 4.3.2) 
• During the period 2016 -21, State Government received  ` 3,226.33 crore and  
` 1,768.50 crore as 14th (2015-20) and 15th (2020-2026) Finance Commission 
grant respectively from Government  of India till March 2021. However, the 
State Government delayed the release of grants to Urban Local Bodies  and 
the interest of ` 13.77 crore thereon was not released to Urban Local Bodies  
along with instalments as recommended by 14 th and 15 th Finance 
Commission.  
(Paragraph 4.7) 
• There was short release of ` 602.55 crore as Performance Grant to U rban 
Local Bodies of the State possibly due to lack of compliance to performance 
related recommendations by the 14th Finance Commission.  
(Paragraph 4.7) 

 
Overview 
 
xv 
 
• Third State Finance Commission (3rd SFC) recommended (accepted by State 
Government in February 2010) t hat one per cent of divisible tax revenue of 
the State Government should be devolved to Urban Local Bodies.  However, 
there was a short devolution of ` 226.50 crore to Urban Local Bodies during 
2016-21.  
(Paragraph 4.8) 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
 
5.1   Compliance Audit of Smart City Companies of Madhya Pradesh 
• Addition of non- Information and communication technology (ICT) projects 
as a Pan -city initiative are not  permissible. Smart City Compan ies, Bhopal 
and Indore incur red a total expenditure of ` 91.33 cr ore towards creating 
‘smart’ assets under Pan -city model. However, the smart assets created did 
not have Information and communication technology (ICT) components. 
(Paragraph 5.1.5.3) 
• The Smart City Comp anies, Bhopal and Indore took up renovation work of 
` 29.11 crore under Pan -city development in violation of Scheme 
guidelines.  
(Paragraph 5.1.5.3) 
• The Smart City Company, Indore, awarded the wor k to a contracto r who 
was not competent to complete the work adopting a defi cient tendering 
process. Consequently, the contractor could not complete the work and the 
contract was terminated (July 2021). This resulted in infructuous 
expenditure amounting to ` 23.38 crore. Further, the Smart City Company 
has not penalised the contra ctor for failure to complete the wor k by 
encashing the Bank Guarantee amounting to ` 2.03 crore (submitted by the 
contractor before execution of work).   
(Paragraph 5.1.6.4) 
• The Smart City Com pany, Indore floated an NIT for the work  of 
Development of Integ rated Smart Road Network Phase -V (Ju ne 2018) 
having estimated cost of ` 48.51 crore. Only one contractor responded to the 
bid. In violation of GoMP instructions , the con tract w as awarded 
(September 2018) at ` 60.58 crore, which was 24.90 per cent  above the  
Schedule of Rates.    
(Paragraph 5.1.6.5) 
• In 18 works of Smart City Compan ies, Bhopal and Indore , it was observed 
that the contractors executed construction works u sing various quantity o f 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
xvi 
 
minor minerals, for which neither royalty had been deducted properly by the 
Companies nor was the ‘No  Dues Certificate of royalty’ obtained from the 
contractors as required bef ore making final payments. This led to loss of  
` 10.62 crore to the Government. 
(Paragraph 5.1.6.11) 
5.2 Receipts (Property Tax and Rent) in Urban Local Bodies 
•   The Commissioner or Chief Municipal Officers of 12 out of 16 test checked 
Urban Local Bodies had not prepared and submitted the draft resolution to 
the Council/ Corporation during the period 2018-19 to 2020-21 and adopted 
old rates for determ ination of A nnual Letting Value/ Taxable Property 
Value.  
(Paragraph 5.2.5.1) 
•   In 15 out of 16 test -checked Urban Local Bodies, the Department had not 
recovered ` 347.98 crore (as of J anuary 2022) as arrears of Property Tax 
due from 1,14,030 property  owners. In these cases, the arrears were  more 
than ` 5,000 in each property.  
(Paragraph 5.2.5.3) 
•   In six ou t of 16 test checked Urban Local Bodies , an amount of  ` 12.10 
crore as premium against auctioned 94 shops were outstanding.  
(Paragraph 5.2.6.1) 
•   In 1 2 ou t of 16 test checked Urban Local  Bodies, an amount of ` 18.60 
crore on account of rent of 3,396 sho ps had remained outstanding for 
recovery. In these cases, the arrears were more than ` 10,000 in each case.  
(Paragraph 5.2.6.2) 
5.3   Water Supply Management in Urban Local Bodies 
• Against the recommended per capita water supply of 135 LPCD, the actual 
water supplied to the population during the period 2018 -19 and  2020-21, 
was betw een 32.11 and 35.22 LPCD in  Gwalior Municipal Corporation ; 
between 95.93 and 98 .60 LPCD in Jabalpur Municipal Corpo ration; and 
between 96.33 and 100.68 LPCD in Vidisha Municipal Council.   
(Paragraph 5.3.7.3) 
• There was no laid do wn pr ocedure for detect ing visible and non -visible 
leakage in all the test-checked Urban Local Bodies. 
(Paragraph 5.3.7.6) 

 
Overview 
 
xvii 
 
• As per the handbook  of Service Level Benchmark of the Ministry of Urban 
Development, all the households are to be brought into the  water connection 
network. 1.70 lakh households (53.97 per cent ) in Gwalior Municipal 
Corporation, 1.24 lakh households (44.93 per cent ) in Jabalpur Municipal 
Corporation and 8,837 households (26.98 per cent ) in Vidisha Municipal 
Council were out of water connection network.   
(Paragraph 5.3.7.7) 
• In all the test-checked Urban Local Bodies , bore wells water was supplied 
without any quality testing.  
(Paragraph 5.3.8.2) 
• The provisions of CPHEEO O& M Manual of Water Supply System, 2005 
for cleaning of O HTs were not followed i n any of the test -checked Urban 
Local Bodies. 
(Paragraph 5.3.8.4) 
• Compliance to the guideline  and gazette notification in respect of recovery 
of user charges for supply of wa ter was not being done by the test -checked 
Urban Local Bodies, and user charges for use of water was levied at the ad -
hoc rate of ` 150 per month by Gwalior Municipal Corporat ion, ` 170 per 
month by Jabalpur Municipal Corporation and ` 100 per month by Vidisha 
Municipal Council.  
(Paragraph 5.3.9.1) 
• The am ounts outstanding for r ecovery on account of water charges were  
` 16.93 crore in Gwalior Municipal Corporation, ` 126.20 crore in Jabalpur 
Municipal Corporation, and ` 18.81 crore in Vidisha Municipal Council as 
of March 2021. Action for recovery of dues as per provisions were not taken 
by the test-checked Urban Local Bodies. 
(Paragraph 5.3.9.2)

Chapter I: An overview of the functioning, accountability mechanism and financial 
reporting issues of Panchayati Raj Institutions 
 
An overview of the functioning of the Panchayati Raj Institutions in the State 
 
1.1   Introduction  
The Constitution (73rdAmendment) Act, 1992 conferred Constitutional status to Panchayats 
and provided inter alia for the creation of Panchayats at village, intermediate and district 
level with, fixed tenure and regular election, devolution of powers and responsibilities given 
to the Panchayats by the State Legislative Assembly for establishing local self-Government 
system. Consequently, Madhya Pradesh State Legislature enacted the Madhya Prades h 
Panchayat Raj evam Gram Swaraj Adhiniyam , 1993  (called PRI Act hereinafter) to 
establish a three-tier system viz.  Gram Panchayat, Janpad Panchayat and Zila Panchayat at 
Village, Block and District level respectively. There are 51 Zila Panchayats  (ZPs), 3 13 
Janpad Panchayats (JPs) and 22809 Gram Panchayats (GPs) in the State as of March 2021.  
The demography of Madhya Pradesh state vis-a-vis National average data is given below:  
Particulars Unit Madhya Pradesh All India 
Population Crore 7.26 121.08 
Rural population Crore 5.26 83.37 
Share of rural population per cent 72.45 68.85 
Rural Literacy rate per cent 63.94 67.80 
Rural Sex ratio (females per thousand males) Ratio 936/1000 949/1000 
(Source: Census data 2011) 
1.2   Organizational set up of PRIs 
The Panchayat and Rural Development Department is administrative  and regulatory 
authority to control Panchayat Raj Institutions (PRIs). 
                Organisational Chart of Panchayati Raj Institutions 
Additional Chief Secretary, Panchayat and Rural Development Department (ACS, PRDD) 
                                                            ↓ 
Commissioner, Panchayat Raj Directorate (PRD)

1.3   Functioning of PRIs 
Article 243G of the Constitution of India provides that the Legislature of a State may, by 
law, endow Panchayats with such powers and authority as may be necessary to enable 
them to functi on as institutions of self -government and such law may contain provisions 
for the devolution of powers and responsibilities upon Panchayats.  
1.3.1   In terms of PRI Act, every ZP shall consist of elected members, who are 
empowered to elect a President and  a Vice -President. The President is responsible for 
acting on the resolutions of the ZP, and following the directions of the State Government 
for ensuring proper maintenance of records and registers, authorizing payments, issuing of 
cheques and sanctioning  of refunds etc. The PRI Act also provides for a CEO and one or 
more Additional CEO at ZP level appointed by the State Government.  
ZPs are responsible to prepare annual plans for economic development and social justice of 
the district and ensure the coordinated implementation of such plans. It shall ensure overall 
supervision, co-ordination and consolidation of the plans prepared by JPs and reallocate to 
JPs and GPs the funds made available by Central or State Government. 
1.3.2   Janpad Panchayats (JPs) are the intermediate tier of PRIs at Block level .  Every JP 
has elected members, President and a Vice -President. President of JP has similar power 
and responsibility as President of ZP enjoys. As per s ection 69(2) o f PRI Act, State 
Government shall appoint C EO and  one or more  Additional CEOs for JPs . CEO of  JP 
controls and supervises the administration of community development block and tribal 
development block within its jurisdiction. The function and schemes assigned to such 
block by the State Government are implemented under the superintendence, directions, and 
control of JP in accordance with the instructions issued by the State Government from time 
to time. 
Further, under section 50 of PRI Act, JPs are also responsible to consider and consolidate 
the annu al plan  of GPs  in respect of economic development and social justice and to 
submit the consolidated plan to ZP. 
1.3.3   Gram Panchayats (GPs) the last tier of PRIs at the grassroot level of PRIs, consist of 
elected Panch and Sarpanch who implements the res olutions of the GP and follow the 
directions of the State Government. GPs  are responsible to ensure execution of schemes, 
works, projects entrusted  to them by any law and those assigned to them by Central or 
State Government or ZP or JP. According to Secti on 49-A of PRI Act, it shall be the duty 
Standing Committees of GPs 
a. General Administration Committee 
b. Construction and Development 
Committee 
c. Education, Health and Social  
    Welfare Committee 
   Standing Committees of ZPs and JPs 
a.  General Administration Committee 
b.  Agriculture Committee 
c.  Education Committee 
d.  Communication and Works Committee 
e.  Cooperation and Industries Committee

of GP to prepare annual plan  of the Panchayat in respect of economic development and 
social justice and submit it to the JP for integration with the Plan of JP.     
1.4   Audit Arrangement 
1.4.1   Primary Auditor 
The State Government appointed  (November 2001) Director, Local Fund Audit (DLFA), 
as primary auditor for the audit of account s of three tier PRIs, who work under the 
Technical Guidance and Support (TGS) of the Comptroller and Auditor General (C&AG) 
of India. As per the standard terms and conditions of TGS, C&AG of India has the right to 
conduct such test check of the accounts and to comment  on and supplement the report of 
the Statutory Auditor, as he may deem fit. Further, the C&AG of India or his representative 
has right to report to State Legislature the results of the audit at his discretion. 
Technical Guidance and Support to DLFA 
Section 152 and 120 of Regulation on Audit and Account 2007  and Regulation on Audit 
and Account 2020 respectively,  provide the following arrangements regarding Technical 
Guidance and Support to PRIs: 
• The Local Fund Auditor will  prepare by the end of March every year, an annual 
audit plan for audit of PRIs and forward it to the Principal Accountant General 
(Audit) of the State. 
• The audit methodology and procedure for audit of PRIs by the Local Fund 
Auditor would be as per various Acts and Statutes enacted by the State 
Government and guidelines prescribed by the C&AG. 
• Copies of inspection reports in respect of selected local bodie s shall be forwarded 
by the Local Fund Auditor to the Principal Accountant General (Audit) for advice 
on system improvement. 
• The Local Fund Auditor will furnish return in such format as may be prescribed 
by C&AG for advice and monitoring. 
• The Local Fund Au ditor shall develop  a system of internal control in his 
organization in consultation with Principal Accountant General (Audit). 
• The Principal Accountant General (Audit)  shall also undertake training and 
capacity building of the local fund audit staff. 
Local F und Auditor (LFA)  forwarded Annual Audit Plan (AAP)  with delay ranged 
between one and five months to Principal Accountant General (Audit -I) Madhya Pradesh  
for the year 2019-21. 
DLFA sent 1 02 Inspection Reports (IRs)  to Principal Accountant General (Audi t-I) 
Madhya Pradesh for vetting during 2019 -21. However, DLFA acted on 36 IRs, out of 102 
vetted IRs. 
Local Fund Auditor established Internal Control Cell (ICC) in eight offices 1 for quality 
improvement of IRs. ICC issued advisory on 64 IRs of ZPs and JPs during 2019 -20, 
however, no advisory was issued during 2020-21. 
                                                 
1  one at directorate and seven at regional offices.

Further, DLFA acted  against the cases of defalcation, serious financial irregularities and 
non-production of records by issuing of 29 and 67 letters during 2019-20 and 2020-21. 
Training and capacity building of 440 local fund audit staff was undertaken by organizing 
28 training and capacity building programme during 2019-21. 
1.4.2   Audit Report on Local Bodies 
Para 10.121 of recommendations of Thirteenth Finance Commission envisages that Annual 
Technical Inspection Report (ATIR) of C&AG as well as the Annual Report of DLFA 
should be placed before the State Legislature. Accordingly, Section 129 of PRI Act was 
amended in July 2011, which lays down that the Annual Audit R eport of DLFA on 
Panchayats along with the ATIR of the C&AG of India shall be submitted to the Governor, 
who shall cause the report to be laid on the table of the Legislative Assembly. 
Madhya Pradesh State Legislative Assembly constituted  (July 2015 ) Local Bodies and 
Panchayati Raj  Accounts Committee (LBPAC), to examine the annual audit reports of 
DLFA as well as the reports of C&AG  of India on Local Bodies to be laid on the table of 
the Legislative Assembly and to check the utilisation of grants-in-aid and loans provided to 
local bodies by the State Government for specified purpose.  
The Report of C&AG  of India on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 st March 2016, w as 
tabled in the State legislative Assembly in July 2017. Further, the ATIR on Local Bodies in 
terms of TGS of C&AG of India for the year ended 31st March 2014 was tabled in the State 
Legislative Assembly in June 2018.  Annual report of DLFA for the year ended 31 st March 
2017 and 31st March 2018  were tabled on 21st September 2020  in the State Legislative 
Assembly. Annual Report for the year ended 31st March 2019 was tabled on 11 th March 
2021 in State Legislative Assembly. Action taken o n the  Reports of C&AG of India  on 
Local Bodies and ATIR is awaited as of November 2022.

1.6   Social Audit 
Mahatma Gandhi National Rur al Employment Guarantee  (MGNREG) Audit of Schemes 
Rules, 2011 stipulates that State Government shall facilitate conduct of social audit of the 
works taken up under MGNREG Act in every Gram Panchayat at least once in six months. 
Subsequently, Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) established (January 2013) Social 
Audit Unit "M.P. State Samajik  Sampariksha Samiti" (MPSSSS) to enable Gram Sabha to 
perform Social Audit process effectively. 
Against required 91,236 social audit, only 36,995 (41 per cent) social audit was conducted

Panchayat and Rural Development Department 
 
2.1   Implementation of 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act 
 
Executive Summary 
Government of India (GoI) enacted the 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act (73rd CAA), 
1992 (came int o effect from April 1993) to strengthen the local self -government at 
Panchayats. The main features of 73rd CAA are to establish three-tier structure panchayat 
at village, block and district level; constitution of State Election Commissi on for regular 
election in panchayats at every five years; proportionate seat reservation for SC, ST and 
women; constitution of State Finance Commission, Gram Sabha at Gram Panchayat level. 
In addition, constitution of District Planning Committee (DPC) for  comprehensive 
planning of the district.  
Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) enacted the Madhya Pradesh Panchayat Raj 
Evam Gram Swaraj Adhiniyam, 1993 (called hereinafter PRI Act) came into existence 
from January 1994 to accord constitutional status to th e Panchayati Raj Ins titutions 
(PRIs), provides constitution of Standing Committee at each PRI level for the preparation 
of budget, annual accounts and development plans; supervisory power to Zila Panchayat 
(ZP) and Janpad Panchayat (JP) and execution al power to Gram Panchayat  (GP) for 
various centrally and state sponsored schemes.  
Further, in view of devolution of 29 functions mentioned in the 11th schedule of Article 
243G of the Constitution to Panchayats, GoMP issued (January 1997) guiding principles 
to the departments for devolution of programme, activities and responsibilities to 
Panchayats.  
The Performance Audit (PA), cover ing the period 2016-21 was conducted by test -check 
of the records of Directorate of four selected departments (apart from Panchaya t Raj 
Directorate) and its district units, 10 ZPs (one ZP from each division), 10 JPs (one JP from 
each selected ZP) and 20 GPs (two GPs from each selected JP) revealed the following:  
• None of the functions, funds and functionaries of test-checked four departments were 
devolved. Further, four departments (other than test -checked departments) took back 
devolved functions, funds and functionaries from PRIs during 1998-2014. 
(Paragraph 2.1.7.1 and 2.1.7.2) 
• GoMP constituted the 3rd, 4th and 5th SFC with a delay of seven months, one and half 
year, and one month 23 days respectively. We also noted that the 3 rd and 4 th SFC 
submitted their reports with a delay of two and half years and four years respectively. 
(Paragraph 2.1.8.1) 
• Despite availability of adequate funds, trainings were not imparted to 70 per cent of 
Elected Representatives (ERs) and 97 per cent of officials, against the target of training 
of 3.92 lakh ERs and 28,150 officials of PRIs.  
(Paragraph 2.1.8.3)                                                                                                                     

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021

• 35 per cent (71 out of required 200) of DPC meetings were held in selected districts 
during 2016-21. None of the DPCs of selected districts prepared District Development 
Plan by consolidating the development plans of panchayats and urban local bodies.                                                                                                                                        
(Paragraph 2.1.8.4)                                                                                                                            
• Out of total receipt of ` 20,985.04 crore, the PRIs could generate only two per cent 
and the remaining receipts were grant from the SFC and CFC.                                                                                                              
(Paragraph 2.1.9.1)                                                                                                                                
• GoMP created Grant No. 53 for providing budg et to PRIs. Finance Department 
through Grant No. 53 allotted funds  of ` 79,693.06 crore to PRDD/PRIs and                        
` 43542.26 crore to BCOs of other departme nts, for operating 33 to 42 and 32 to 57 
schemes r elated to 29 functions as mentioned in 11th schedule of the constitution 
during 2016-21, leading to inadequate and ineffective functions and funds devolution 
to PRIs.  
(Paragraph 2.1.9.2)                                                                                                                                
• Except 2016-17, GoMP did not allot four per cent of divisible tax revenue to PRIs and 
the allotment steadily declined from three to less than one per cent during 2017-21. 
(Paragraph 2.1.9.5)                                                                                                                               
• Due to delay in release of CFC grant to PRIs during 2017 -21, there was an avoidable 
payment of interest of `19.83 crore by GoMP. 
(Paragraph 2.1.9.6)

of Gram Sabha were held without the required quorum in selected GPs  during          
2016-21. 
(Paragraph 2.1.10.2)

recommended by C&AG on Panchayat Darpan portal leading to non-certification of 
PRIs accounts by Director of Local Fund Audit (DLFA). 
(Paragraph 2.1.10.5(a) & 2.1.10.5(b)i) 
• GoMP did not create Panchayat cadre as en visaged in GoI guidelines . Further, acute 
shortage of staff by 6 0 and 45 per cent  was noticed at selected ZPs and JPs 
respectively.  
(Paragraph 2.1.11.1 & 2.1.11.2)                                                                                               
• Non preparation of Inspection Roaster/Report by 75 per cent (15 out of 20) selected 
ZPs and JPs leading to inefficient monitoring mechanism. 
(Paragraph 2.1.12.1)                                                                                     
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

 
Chapter II: Performance Audit 
 
9 
 
2.1.1   Introduction 
To strengthen the local self -government, the Government of India (GoI) enacted the 73rd 
Constitutional Amendment Act, 1992 (73rd CAA) which came into effect from April 1993. 
In view of Article 243G of the Constitution, the Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) 
enacted the 73 rd CAA ( June 1994) and the Madhya Pradesh Panchayat Raj Evam Gram 
Swaraj Adhiniyam, 1993 ( known as PRI Act) came into existence from January 1994  to 
accord constitutional status to the Panchayat i Raj Institutions (PRIs) and made provisions 
for devolution of powers and responsibilities. The 73rd CAA empowers the state Government 
to devolve 29 functions relating 20 departments (Appendix-2.1.1) mentioned in the 11 th 
schedule of Article 243G  in Madhya Pradesh. The various provisions of the 73 rd CAA are 
summarised below: 
• Constitution of Gram Sabha in villages.  
• Constitution of three-tier Panchayat at village, block and district levels.  
• Direct election to all seats of Panchayats. 
• Minimum age for contesting elections to the PRIs be twenty-one years. 
• Reservation of seats for Schedule Castes and Schedule Tribes. 
• Reservation of one-third of the total seats at every level of PRIs for women. 
• Fixing of tenure of five years for Panchayats. 
• Giving representation to the Members of the Parliament, M embers of Legislative 
Assembly (MLAs) and Members of Legislative Council ( MLCs) at the intermediate  
(Block) and district level with full voting rights. 
The PRI Act, 1993 envisages provisions for smooth functioning of Zila Panchayats (ZPs), 
Janpad Panchayats (JPs) and Gram Panchayats (GPs).  At the district level, ZP is entrusted 
with supervisory powers to monitor Janpad Panchayats and Gram Panchayats. The functions 
of JP at Block level are to ensure the execution of schemes, works and projects entrusted to 
it. The functions of GPs at village level a re to prepare socio -economic annual plan and to 
execute various schemes sponsored by GoI and GoMP.  
Besides this, there are provisions for constitution of District Planning Committee (DPC) to 
consolidate the plan prepared by the Panchayats for overall development at district level and 
Standing Committee for preparation of development plans, budget and annual accounts for 
the PRIs. 
2.1.1.1   Madhya Pradesh Profile- PRIs 
Madhya Pradesh (MP) located in central India, is the second largest state of the country. The 
area of the State is 3.08 lakh Square km out of which the rural area is 3.00 lakh Square km. 
As per census 2011, 72.31 per cent (5.25 crore) of total population (7.26 crore) reside in 
rural areas of Madhya Pradesh (MP). 
GoMP has established 23,173 PRIs under Section 10 of the PRI Act . PRIs are functioning 
under Panchayat and Rural Development Department (PRDD) . The category wise detail is 
shown in Table-2.1.1.

Division of rural areas into wards, villages and blocks is notified by GoMP for the purpose 
of elections in PRIs.   
 2.1.2   Organisational structure of PRIs 
The Panchayat and Rural Development Department (PRDD),  headed by the Additional 
Chief Secretary (ACS), is the administrative department for overall supervision of  PRIs. 
Panchayat Raj Directorate (PRD)  established in December 2007, headed by the 
Commissioner is assisted by Chief Executive Officer (CEO), ZP at district level, CEO, JP at 
block level and Secretary, GP at village level.  In addition, for supervision and monitoring 
of Centrally Sponsored Schemes, office of the Development Commissioner has also been 
established. 
2.1.3   Audit Objectives 
We aimed to assess: 
(i) the PRIs have been adequatel y empowered to effectively discharge their 
responsibilities; 
(ii) adequate and timely allocation of funds were given to PRIs;  
(iii) the functionaries required for carrying out the devolved functions are placed at the 
disposal of PRIs; and 
(iv) functions intended to be discharged by the PRIs as per the Act have been transferred. 
2.1.4   Audit Criteria 
We sourced audit criteria from: 
• The 73rd Constitution (Amendment) Act, 1992; 
• The Madhya Pradesh Panchayat Raj evam Gram Swaraj Adhiniyam 1993 and rules 
incorporated thereunder; 
• Madhya Pradesh District Planning Committee Act, 1995; 
• The Central and State Finance Commission recommendations; and 
• Notifications, circulars, orders and instructions, issued by the GoI, GoMP and PRDD.

2.1.5   Scope and Methodology 
The Performance A udit (PA) covered the period 2016 -21 using Multi -Stage Sampling 1 
Method. As GoI sanctions funds based on population to ZPs, JPs and GPs, the same criteria 
has been adopted for selection of ZPs, JPs and GPs. We selected for audit: - 
• 10 ZPs (one ZP from each division);  
• 10 JPs (one JP from each selected ZP); and 
• 20 GPs (two GPs from each selected JP).  
The details of selected PRIs are shown in Appendix-2.1.2. The geographical representation 
of the sampling is shown in Map-2.1.1 below: 
Map-2.1.1 
 
We examined four2 out of 29 functions/activities to ascertain the status of devolution.  
Methodology adopted during the PA was scrutiny of records at the Directorate and district 
units of the selected departments,  ZPs/JPs/GPs and offices of the District Planning 
Committee. We scrutinised the sanctions released under recommendations of Central 
Finance Commission (CFC), State Finance Commission (SFC), records related to adoption 
of provisions of 73rd CAA, adoption of Panchayat Extension Scheduled Area (PESA) Act3, 
transfer of functions/  activities, functionaries and funds to PRIs. We further scrutinised

It is evident from the above table that as per the provisions of 73 rd CAA, the PRI Act was 
suitably amended. Further, for effective working in three tiers of PRIs, Rules have also been 
framed. In this regard, Chief Secretary , GoMP issued (January 1997) guid ing principles to 
the departments for devolution of programme, activiti es and responsibilities  as indicated 
below: 
• All necessary steps should be taken promptly to make the three -tier Panchayat Raj 
system strong and effective as per the framework of the 73rd amendment; 
• Necessary administrative structure and staff should be given to form “District 
Governments” with ZP and JP as chief implementing units; 
• GP should be develop ed as implementing units of all the programme/  activities of 
ZP/JP in the villages; 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
14 
 
• Planning and implementation for economic development and social justice shall be 
the responsibility of Panchayats. For achieving the same, budget and staff should be 
made available by the departments to PRIs; 
• The staff should be transferred to PRIs according to programme/ activities devolved; 
and 
• Since the work of the departments were to be transferred to Panchayats, the budget of 
the schemes/programme except expenditure incurred on establishment, would be 
transferred to Panchayat.  
Further, concerned departments issued instructions and orders to transfer the functions, funds 
and functionaries. PRD D also issued instruc tions from time to time  (1995-2011) to 
implement the provisions of PRI Act. Audit test-checked the compliance of the provisions of 
the PRI Act and instructi ons issued in selected PRIs and issues noticed in  selected four 
activities/functions are discussed in succeeding paragraphs. 
Status of devolution of 29 functions/activities enlisted in 11th Schedule Section 243G of 73rd

The present status of devolution of all 29 functions mentioned in the 11th schedule of Article 
243G and funds and functionaries are detailed in  Appendix-2.1.3(i) & 2. 1.3(ii) can be 
summed up as below: 
• 12 departments issued orders for devolution of functions, functionaries and funds to the 
PRIs as per the provisions of 73 rd CAA and PRDD also issued three orders for such 
devolution in favour of PRIs; 
• Four functions (four7 departments) were devolved to the PRIs but taken back by the 
concerned departments subsequently; 
• No record was available with the PRIs at any level in respect of devolution of functions 
and moreover, in test checked departments also no devolution was found as per records 
as there was no transfer  of staff, functions and assets was done by the concerned 
departments. Most of the functions which were devolved to the PRIs, were being carried 
out by the concerned departments  and expenditure was also being incurred by the 
departments. Thus, there was no actual devolution of powers and responsibilities to the 
PRIs by the other departments even after issuance of orders in this regard; and 
• Seven8 functions were found with PRDD/ PRD along with two9 other departments. 
In reply to audit observation, Directorate, Veterinary Services replied (March 2022) that the 
proposal for devolution of functions, functionaries and funds were sent (July 2020) to the 
GoMP. The reasons for non -devolution of functions, funds and functionar ies were not 
intimated by other departments as of December 2022.  
Thus, GoMP failed to comply with the provisions of 73rd CAA even after adoption of the 
Act 27 years ago.  
Department did not furnish reply on the issue.

2.1.8   Institutional empowerment for effective implementation of provisions  
of 73rd CAA 
The Articles 243I, J and K of 73rd CAA provide for establishment of institutional 
mechanisms for effective and smooth functioning of PRIs including provisions for financial, 
public participation, social audit  and capacity bui lding by formation of Institutions . In 
addition to above, Article 243 ZD of 74th CAA provides constitution of a District Planning 
Committee for comprehensive planning of the district. The State Institute of Training and 
Rural Development was also strengthened through state p olicy (1998) on trainings for 
functionaries of PRIs. The above arrangements are represented in Chart-2.1.1:  
Chart-2.1.1: Showing the Institutional empowerment for strengthening of PRIs 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The status of Institutes constituted as per the provisions of 73rd CAA and their working are 
discussed in the succeeding paragraphs:

It is evident from the above table that Finance Department constituted the SFCs with a delay 
ranging from 53 to 565 days and Third and Fourth SFC submitted the ir reports with delay 
of 94510 and 146211 days respectively. Further, Finance Department took 167 to 462 days to 
submit Action Taken Reports (ATR) to GoMP after submission of SFC reports.  
Audit further noticed that third SFC recommended financial assistance @ four per cent of 
divisible revenue of the state to PRIs and GoMP is allotting accordingly till date. Fourth SFC 
has recommended devolution at the rate of 5.5 per cent. However, GoMP has not taken any 
action yet though decided to consider the recommendation s of the 4th SFC along with the 
report of next finance commission . Details of issues regarding implementation of the 
accepted recommendations of first to third SFCs are discussed in Paragraph 2.1.9.4.  
No action has been taken on the reports submitted by 4th SFC till date even after lapse of the 
award period12 and recommendations of 5 th SFC also are not submitted by the commission 
till date even after lapse of more than five years since its constitution.  
Audit concludes that  the delay in constitution of SFCs and implementation of the ir 
recommendations has defeated the purpose of the constitution of the SFC as their 
recommendation are not timely implemented by the government resulting in deprival of the 
PRIs of tentative favourable recommendations made by the SFCs.  
Department replied (October 2022) that the fifth SFC submitted its report to the Honourable 
Governor on 30th April 2019.  
Reply is not acceptable as the department could not provide any evidence in support of its 
reply. Further, the department did not attribute any rea son for delay in acceptance of 
recommendations of SFCs.  
2.1.8.2   State Election Commission (Local Bodies) 
In pursuance to the provision 243K of 73rd CAA, GoMP constituted (February 1994) the 
Madhya Pradesh State Election Commission to conduct regular election of PRIs in fair 
and transparent manner to provide Constitutional status to PRIs.  
The last election for PRIs was held in the state in  2014-15 and the elections were due 
after completion of five years of previous elected bodies  in 2019-20. However, it  was

reservation process and delimitation process , public participation in local governance 
could not take place in last two years. 
Capacity buildings of Elected Representatives (ERs) and PRI officials 
2.1.8.3   State Institute of Rural Development (SIRD) 
SIRD, Jabalpur was formed and registered (1987) under MP Society Registration Adhiniyam 
1973 for training and capacity building of Elected Representatives ( ERs) and officials of 
three-tier of Panchayats (ZP/JP/  GP). It is an autonomous body  controlled by a committee 
chaired by the Hon’ble Minister of PRDD and funded by the GoMP and GoI. The Institute 
works along with six 13 Regional Training Centres (RTCs)/ Extended Training Centres 
(ETCs) located at different regions of MP. For the training of ERs and officials , the se 
Institutes were to design the course, prepare annual calenda rs and impart training 
accordingly. Further, the evaluation of the participants w as to be obtained after the 
completion of training. 
As per the work plan (2015) under National Capacity Building Framework (NCBF), GoMP 
identified the ERs and officials, whom the trainings were to be imparted. The identified ERs 
and officials of various categories are shown in Table-2.1.7 below:

2.1.8.3(a) Training  
Training calendars/action plans would incorporate designing of training programmes based 
on formulation of training objectives which are derived from Training Needs Analysis 
(TNA) of the targeted groups. The key elements to derive training action plans/calendars are 
shown in flow Chart-2.1.2: 
                                 Chart-2.1.2: Showing the key elements of training calendars 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
Audit compared TNA, calendars and progress reports of the actual trainings imparted and 
following observations were noticed:  
2.1.8.3(b)i   Training conducted for ERs  
As per the work plan (2015) under NCBF , all ERs were to be given fundamental training 
within one year of being elected. In election of PRIs ( 2015), 3.92 lakh ERs were elected. 
Director/Principal of SIRD/ETC/RTCs were responsible to prepare training calendars and 
impart trainings to ERs within one year of election. 
Audit noticed that during 2016-21, however, 17 trainings were held against 16 planned but 
only 1.19 lakh ERs (30 per cent) could be imparted off campus training for one to five days

Department accepted the audit observation and stated (October 2022) that training could not 
be imparted due to absence of required approval by the competent authority and clarity in 
order issued by the  state in respect of  training programmes. Further, the required training 
could also not be imparted due to completion of tenure of ERs of PRIs , probable election 
year and Covid-19 pandemic as well.  
2.1.8.3(b)ii   Training of Trainers (ToT) / Master Trainers  
As per NCB Framework, for imparting quality trainings to large number of ERs of Panchayat 
and functionaries, decentralised training mode is efficient in which trainings are organised 
at multiple locations. To fulfil the requirement, SIRD prepared training programme for 
ToT/master trainers. 
Audit noticed from the training calendars and progress reports of the SIRD/ RTC/ETCs that 
during 2016-21, total 11,128 master trainers had been imparted training under Rashtriya 
Gram Swaraj Abhiyan  (RGSA) and Gram Panchayat Development Plan (GPDP) schemes 
against 18,159 targeted master trainers  to train 3.92 lakh ERs and 0.29 lakh PRI officials.

monitoring by the department.

on trainings prior to 2020 -21 was made as per training remuneration rate decided by 
administrative committee of SIRD, which was lower as compared to the cost norms of RGSA 
on which basis budget provision for training was made. Further, desired number of trainings 
were not organised due to lack of staff and lower participation of trainees.

20 elected members of ZP and Urban Local Bodies  (ULBs) including two members

Collector being the Secretary of DPC, shall be responsible to maintain all the records to 
prepare agenda for meetings, database of district, lists of amenities exist ed in PRIs. CEO,

programme being implemented within the districts and send regular progress report to state 
government.

as here:

during the period from 2016-21. Thus, 64 per cent meetings were not conducted. Reasons

meetings conducted and shortfalls thereof is shown in Appendix-2.1.4.

consolidation of development plans received from the panchayats and urban local bodies.

DPC. However, DPCs prepared work plans at their level along wit h the  available 
resources of various departments.  
• In five22 DPCs, Government nominated the members and in two23 DPCs, the proposal for 
nomination of members were sen t to the government but no such record was available 
regarding approval of the proposals of nominations. Three24 DPCs did not even send the 
proposals to GoMP. 
• None of the  DPCs sen t the progress reports to GoMP after review of the schemes/ 
programmes being implemented within the districts as no regular meetings were being 
conducted in all the districts.

2.1.8.5   Social Audit Committee 
Article 243 (J) of CAA  provides that  State may, by law, make provisions to audit the 
accounts of Panchayats. In pursuance to the above provisions, MP Panchayat Evam Gram 
Swaraj Adhiniyam 1993 was suitably amended and MP Social Audit Rules 2013 (December 
2013) were framed. GoMP established MP State Samajik Sampariksha Samiti  (MPSSSS) 
(January 2013) to build capacities of Gram Sabhas  for conducting social  audit and 
facilitating verification of records of works done under various schemes. MPSSSS is headed 
by Additional Chief Secretary , PRDD. Further, the Madhya Pradesh Social Audit Rules, 
2013 envisage that social audit shall be taken up in every GP at least once in six months for 
which at State level, an annual calendar was to be prepared and circulated to all District 
Programme Coordinators for making necessary arrang ements and accordingly the  social 
audits were to be conducted by Gram Sampariksha Samitis (GSSs) constituted at village 
level. As per circular issued by MPSSSS (June 2017), the remedial action shall be taken 
within 15 days on the issues of social audit rep orts and action taken repo rts were to be 
uploaded on the portal. 
2.1.8.5(a) Status of Social Audit 
Audit noticed that MPSSSS prepared annual Social audit calendars for social audit of the 
works executed in GPs under various schemes. The status of social audit s planned and 
executed during 2016-21 is given in Table-2.1.11 below:

2.1.8.5(c)   Social Audit of PRIs in selected GPs

(MGNREGA), read with rule 6 of Social Audit Rules 2013, Social Audit of every GP was

constituted at village level. After the approval by GS, social audit reports along with  
remedial action taken on the same were to be sent to the concerned JPs/ ZPs within 15 days 
for uploading the reports on the portal.

2.1.9   Revenue profile of PRIs  
Article 243 H of the Constitution of India provides power to Panchayats to impose taxes and 
also envisages that State government would provide grant-in-aid and also assign revenue 
which is collected as dut y, toll and fee as specified by the law. Further, Central Finance 
Commission (CFC) and SFC also recommended for providing grants for smooth functioning 
of PRIs. To provide financial assistance, GoMP created head Grant-53 for PRIs specifically 
and also provided financial assistance to various departments under minor head 196, 197 
and 198 under the Major Heads of the concerned departments. 
The GoI releases funds to GoMP and GoMP in turn releases the same to PRDD/ PRD and 
PRD release s the funds to PRIs through N ational Electronic Fund Transfer (NEFT)  for 
various purposes. The fund flow is shown through flow Chart-2.1.3: 
Flow chart-2.1.3: showing the fund flow from GoI to PRIs 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2.1.9.1   Sources of Revenue 
PRIs receive financial assistance based on recommendation of CFCs and SFCs constituted 
from time to time. Further, PRIs collect their own revenue in the form of tax and fee as per 
the provisions of PRI Act. The status of financial assistance and sources of revenue of PRIs 
during the period 2016-21 is shown in Table-2.1.14 below:

that non transfer of staff of PHE department to PRIs.  
Audit concludes that the GoMP allocated the funds under Grant No. 53 to other departments 
for execution of the functions mentioned in 11 th schedule of 73rd CAA and PRI Act 1993. 
Thus, the devolution of functions, funds to PRIs was inadequate and ineffective due to funds 
being released to other departments under Grant No. 53 and expenditure being incurred by 
the respective departments for the functions devolved to PRIs. 
Department replied (October 2022)  that the responsibilities of execution of works were 
assigned to departments as they had better technical knowledge and experience as compared 
to GPs.   
Reply is not acceptable as department could not enhance capacity of officials as well as 
Elected Representatives (ERs) of PRIs to work independently despite a lapse of almost 29 
years since the enactment of 73 rd CAA as trainings were imparted to only three per cent 
officials and 30 per cent ERs during 2016-21.

Audit noticed that during 2016 -21, GoMP provided ` 39,732.15 crore through Integrated

found that DDOs received an amount of ` 1,872.5331 crore as financial assistance under the

departments and noticed that DDOs incurred expenditure against the released financial 
assistance.

under minor heads 196, 197 and 198 to the PRIs for execution of schemes/programmes 
falling under devolved functions. However, BCOs instead of devolvin g the funds to PRI s, 
utilized the fund on their own.

released to BCOs of aforesaid department s as being technically sound and to complete the 
works in prescribed time frame.  
Thus, funds for execution of schemes/programmes falling under devolved functions were 
not released to PRIs.

GoMP though accepted SFC recommendation to devolve four per cent of the divisible tax 
revenue, yet except for 2016-17, the devolution to PRIs steadily declined and there was short 
devolution of funds of ` 2,930.69 crore to the PRIs during 2016-21 against the recommended

recommendations of SFC to strengthen the PRIs. 
Thus, GoMP did not adhere to the accepted recommendation of SFC.  
Further, the 14th Finance Commission recommen ded Performance Grant of ` 1355.64 
crore33 to the PRIs of Madhya Pradesh for the period 2016-20. However, only ` 562.48 crore 
was released by the GoI for the period only in 2016-17 and 2017-18. No Performance Grant 
was released by Government of India in the year 2015 -16, 2018-19 and 201 9-20. 
Performance Grant of ` 793.16 crore though recommended by the 14th Finance Commission 
for the respective years but was not released. Thus, there was short release of ` 793.16 crore 
as Performance Grant to the PRIs of the State due to lack of complian ce to performance 
related following two conditions  stipulated by the 14th Finance Commiss ion. (i) making 
available reliable data on all receipts and expenditure through audited accounts; and (ii) 
improvement in own revenues. 
The department did not furnish any reply on this issue.  
2.1.9.6    Avoidable payment of interest on delayed release of CFC grants  
GoI provided financial assistance to strengthen the PRIs under 14 th and 15 th CFC. As per 
sanction orders, the state needs to transfer the amount to local bodies within fifteen days of 
the receipt from GoI. In case of delay, State government would have liability to pay interest 
at bank rate of RBI, for the number of days of delay.  
During the s crutiny of sanctions, files and release orders at PRD  level, audit noticed that 
GoMP had paid the interest of ` 19.83 crore to PRIs on account of delay ed release of CFC 
grants during the year 2017-18 and 2019-21. Year-wise details of the interest paid is shown

2.1.9.7(a) Levy and collection of taxes by PRIs

Rules, 1996 provides to le vy and to collect the taxes from the households as per the rate 
approved by GS.

was not effective and the purpose of such devolution was defeated. 
Department stated (October 2022) that online monitoring system was developed to increase 
the own revenue of GP and almost all GPs were utilising this system. 
The reply of the department is not acceptable as despite launching of online monitoring 
system, 80 per cent selected GPs failed to levy and collect their own tax revenue.   
2.1.9.7(b)   Collection of water charges 
As per Rule 4 (e-1) of the Alternate Tax Rent and Fee (C ondition and Exceptions) Rules, 
1996, GPs would levy and collect  water charges as fixed and collect in proportion to the 
expenditure reported by the GPs in previous financial year on operation and main tenance 
of water s upply schemes in those GPs that were operating and maintaining water supply 
scheme. Third SFC also recommended that electricity dues would be reimbursed from SFC 
grant where the Nal-Jal Yojana was in operation and water charges were being collected by 
the GPs.  It is the responsibility of Secretary to maintain demand and collection registers in 
respect of taxes levied and collected by the GP.

2.1.9.8   Non-submission of Utilisation Certificates (UCs)  
As per the sanction orders issued by CFC and SFC, release of SFC grant to the ZPs/JPs was 
subject to utilization of 70 per cent of previous year grant and 100 per cent of the year prior 
to previous years along with interest, if any. It is the responsibility of CEO, ZPs/JPs and GPs 
to submit the UCs for each financial year. Commissioner, PRD should ensure to collect UCs 
from all tiers of PRIs  and maintain records in respect of UCs sent by PRIs . Further, the 
Additional Chief Secretary, PRDD  issued (June 2017) order to every ZPs and JPs for 
maintaining single bank account in any nationalized bank. 
Audit noticed that during 2016-21, grants of ` 14,721.11 crore and ` 5,903.82 crore were

Audit observed that PRD sent UCs to GoI of  ` 12,729.11 crore against the CFC grant

maintained at PRD in respect of UCs obtained from the PRIs for the funds utilised by them. 
Further, as per the sanction orders of PRD, the PRIs were to submit progress of expenditure 
after the end of every month and UC for each year to PRD in respect of amount released 
under SFC. However, it was seen, neither PRIs furnished UCs to PRD nor PRD monitored 
the status of submission of UCs by PRIs. Resultantly, factual status of utilisation of the 
amount released could not be ascertained by audit.  
Department accepted the audit observation and stated (October 2022)  that instruction for 
submission of UCs would b e issued to PRIs and action would also be taken for making 
provision regarding UCs of SFC grant on Panchayat Darpan Portal.

noticed are discussed in the paragraphs below: 
2.1.10.1   First meeting of Elected Representatives (ERs)

the election result. Further, note (iii) below S ection 34 of PRI Act stipula tes that the ERs 
should hold their posts in PRIs for five years from the date of first meeting of the PRIs and 
not beyond that. Next election would be held before completion of election period or before 
six months in case of dissolution of the body. 
Audit noticed that out of 40 selected PRIs, the first meeting was conducted in five38 PRIs 
with a delay ranging between four to 60 days. However, in case of two 39 GPs the status of 
first meeting was not clear.   There was no delay in conducting first meetings in remaining

2.1.10.2   Meetings conducted in GPs 
Article 243 A of the CAA read with Section 6 and 7 of the PRI Act provide powers to Gram 
Sabha (GS) to conduct its business. As per Section 129-A (a) of PRI Act 1993, GS is a body 
consisting of persons whose names are included in the electoral rolls relating to the area of 
a panchayat at the village level. Further, Section 6 (1) of PRI Act envisages that GS meetings 
should be held quarterly (January, April, July and Octob er) and as per Section 7 (2) of the 
Act, annual GS meeting should be conducted at least three months before the next financial 
year. During annual GS meeting, annual account, budget for next year and administrative 
report of GP pertained to previous year wo uld be discussed. The required quorum for 
meeting is one-tenth of the total members of the GS with mandatory participation of 50 per 
cent women members.  
In 20 selected GPs, audit observed that out of 400 quarterly and 100 annual meetings were 
to be conducted during 2016-21, but only 229 quarterly and 82 annual meetings were held 
without the quorum as per the provisions ibid.  
The year-wise details of the quarterly and annual meetings conducted during 2016-21 are 
given in Table-2.1.22.

available in the minutes of meetings regarding these approvals/reports.

for organising all meetings of Gram Sabha well in time would be issued.

women ERs should be 50 per cent of the total ERs elected in GPs through direct election on 
the basis of rotation of all the wards of village.

2.1.10.4   Rotation for constitution of JP  
As per Section 22 of the PRI Act, every Janpad Panchyat shall be constituted consisting the 
elected members from the constituencies,  the members of the State Legislative Assembly 
whose constituencies fall within the JP and one fifth of the Sarpanches in the territorial area 
of JP would also be members on rotation basis for a period of one year. The Sarpanch who 
is member of this committee under the clause shall not be a member of the other committee 
constituted under section 47. 
In test checked JPs , audit noticed that JPs were constituted but none of the JPs adhered to 
the provisions of rotation of the Sarpanches. The members nominated from the Sarpanches 
in the territorial areas of JP remained for entire tenure of five years  instead of rotation of 
members as per the provisions. Thus, the representation of all Sarpanches in JP’s Territorial 
area was not ensured to facilitate their participation in planning and development in their 
jurisdictions. 
Department did not offer its comment on adherence to the provision of rotation of Sarpanch 
for constitution of JP. 
 
 
                                                           
42  One to 20 per cent (in 97 meetings), 21 to 40 per cent (in 59 meetings) and 41 to 44 per cent                         
(in 6 meetings). 


 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021

2.1.10.5   Preparation of Accounts and Audit of Panchayat Accounts 
Article 243J of the 73rd CAA provides for maintenance of accounts by the Panchayats and 
auditing of such accounts.  Further, Section 7K, 73 and 129 of PRI  Act envisage that there 
shall be a separate and independent audit organisation under the GoMP to conduct the audit 
of Panchayats. The following observations are made on scrutiny of records in the selected 
districts. 
2.1.10.5(a)  Preparation of accounts by PRIs 
In pursuance of Article 243J, GoMP incorporated ZP/JP/GP (Budget Estimate) Rules, 1997 
and ZP/JP/GP (Accounting) Rules, 1999 in PRI Act for maintenance of accounts in PRIs. 
On the recommendations of  11th CFC, Comptroller and Auditor General of India  (C&AG) 
recommended budget and accounting formats (eight43) in 2002. Ministry of Panchayati Raj 
and GoMP accepted these accounting formats in 2007 and 2010 respectively for 
implementing in PRIs.  
Further, PRDD developed Panchayat Darpan portal (prd.mp.gov.in) (April 2014) and the 
Panchayats were to make entries in respect of Cash book and Works Registers  only and all 
the scheme wise details for financial accounting and CAG’s statutory reports were to be 
generated by the portal. The web portal also facilitates to generate reports in prescribed eight 
formats and income and expenditure details for monitoring purpose. Further, para 10.111 of 
recommendation of 13 th CFC envisages that , accounts of PRIs were to be maintained in 
prescribed formats.  
At state level, we noticed that none of the test checked PRIs uploaded their accounts in eight 
accounting formats on  Panchayat Darpan  portal. Moreover, the Directorate also did not 
produce the copy of accounts prepared in prescribed accounting formats (October 2021).  
We noticed that none of the selected PRIs at any level prepared annual accounts. However, 
ZPs/ JPs/ GPs were uploading the e-transactions on ‘Panchayat Darpan’ portal.  
Resultantly, the financial status of the PRIs could not be ascertained by audit.  CEO, ZP and 
JP and Secretary, GP were responsible for preparation and uploading of the accounts in the 
prescribed format. 
The department accepted the audit observation  and stated (October 2022) that instructions 
for uploading accounts of PRI would be issued.

Audit of PRIs was being conducted by Director of Local Fund Audit (DLFA) and office of 
Principal Accountant General  (PAG), Audit -144 Madhya Pradesh Gwalior. The status of 
audit conducted by DLFA and PAG is discussed in succeeding paragraphs:

2.1.10.6   Status of implementation of Panch ayats (Extension to Scheduled Areas) Act 
(PESA), 1996 
Article 243M of the Constitution  of India provides that Parliament may, by law, extend its 
provisions to the Scheduled and Tribal Areas. PESA Act, 1996 mainly aims to preserve the 
traditions and customs  of the people, their cultural identity, community resources and 
customary mode of disputes resolution etc. by making Gram Sabha and Gram Panchayat 
Centres of self-governance. The GSs have mandatory executive functions to approve plans 
of the Village Panch ayats, p ower to manage village mark ets, o wnership of minor forest 
produce, power to control institutions, functionaries in all social sectors and power to control 
local plans and resources including Tribal Sub Plan. Further, GoI released funds to appoint 
District Coordinator at ZP and Block Coordinator at JP level and M obiliser at each GP of 
PESA districts/ blocks. 
Audit noticed that in Madhya Pradesh , 20 districts were (five49 fully and 15 50 partially) 
identified for implementation of PESA Ac t which covered 89 blocks, 5,211 Panchayats 
covering 11,784 villages having 153.17 lakh (21 per cent of State) of tribal population. The 
diagrammatic representation of PESA and Non-PESA districts is shown in Map-2.1.2: 
Map-2.1.2 showing the PESA and Non-PESA districts of MP
 
 
Audit noticed that GoMP amended Panchayat and Gram Swaraj Adhiniyam and added all 
the provisions related to PESA Act in the Chapter-14 of the Adhiniyam. Audit further noticed 
                                                           
48  ATIR (2013-14)- June 2016, CAG Report (2014-15)-July 2016 and CAG Report (2015-16)-July 2017. 
49  Alirajpur, Barwani, Dindori, Jhabua and Mandla 
50  Anuppur, Balaghat, Betul, Burhanpur, Chh indwara, Dhar, Hoshangabad, Khargone, Khandwa, 
Ratlam, Seoni, Shahdol, Sheopur, Sidhi and Umaria 
Non-PESA 
District 
Partial PESA

Chapter II: Performance Audit 
 
45 
 
that GoMP did not frame PESA Rules as envisaged  by GoI. However, GoI released an 
amount of ` 86.85 crore during 2019-21 under Rashtriya Gram Swaraj Abhiyan to appoint 
Coordinators at ZPs and JPs level and Mobilisers at each GP for execution of activities till 
February 2021 and the funds were  released to the PESA districts fo r appointment of 
Coordinators/Mobilisers.  
Audit test -checked three 51  ZPs, one JP ( JP Gogawan of Khargone ZP) and two GPs 
(Gogawan and Shahpura of Gogawan JP ) covered under PESA Act and noticed that an 
amount of ` 13.81 crore was released for appointment of Coordinators for PESA in selected 
ZPs/ JPs/ GPs but no coordinators/ mobilisers were appointed in any of the selected ZPs. In 
audit of test-checked PRIs it was seen that in absence of appointment of 
coordinators/mobilisers in PRIs the activities such as preparation of development plans 
including social sectors development, resources for such plans, power to manage traditional 
village markets, ownership of minor forest produce could not be carried out.  
PRIs replied that recruitment process was initiated at  the PRI level but the High court, 
Jabalpur has placed stay on the appointment of mobilisers / coordinators . However, 
department did not offer any comment during exit conference.  
2.1.11   Limited manpower to fulfil powers and responsibilities 
Section 69 of PRI Act provides power to the state government or authorised authority 52 to 
appoint Chief Executive Officer (CEO)/  Additional CEO at ZP/ JP and Secretary for each 
GP. For the adoption of provisions of 73 rd CAA and to strengthen district panchayat to act 
as district government and JP as its chief executive units, and to develop to GP as executive 
units to implement all the schemes which are to be executed within the jurisdiction of GP . 
Principal Secretary, PRDD MP also issued instruction (January 1997) to provide staff and 
administrative structure t o the PRIs. Further, Third State Finance Commission also 
recommended to transfer of staff in phased manner to empower the PRIs and State 
government also gave consent to take decision in this regard. Under Section 49, 50 and 52 
of the PRI Act, read with  Madhya Pradesh Panchayat (Powers and Functions of Chief 
Executive Officer) Rules, 1995 and Gram Panchayat (Powers and Fun ctions of Secretary) 
Rules, 1999, provide provisions to discharges the responsibilities of PRIs  as shown in 
Appendix-2.1.8, rest with CEO, ZP/ JP with their staff and Secretary of GPs.

We noticed the following: 
2.1.11.1   Formation of Panchayat cadre  
Third SFC recommended (October 2008) to create district cadre of Panchayat Secretary at 
GP level and  a pool of technical skilled personnel at JP level so that GP can take their 
services on the basis of works and payments  and GoMP accepted  (February 2010)  the 
recommendation. Apart from this, as per guidelines issued (October 2009) by GoI, Ministry 
of Panchayati Raj (MoPR), a quasi GP cadre of field level functionaries, whose work is vital 
to the GP and whose work touches the lives of people at large, should be created. This would 
include; Panchayat Secretary, Accountant, Technical Assistant, Computer Opera tor, 
Teachers etc.  
Audit found that GoMP did not create (December 2021) Panchayat cadre as envisaged in the 
guidelines issued by GoI . I n March  2011, GoMP notified MP Panchayat Service (Gram 
Panchayat Secretary Recruitment and Conditions of Service) Rules,  2011, for Secretary of 
GPs cadre for each district. Audit also noticed that Secretary and Rojgar Sahayak  (two 
persons) execute all mandatory functions envisaged in PRI Act along with implementation 
of all the Centrally Sponsored/ State Schemes in jurisdiction of GPs.  
Department did not offer any comment on this issue.

would be fulfilled by the staff sanctioned for the purpose. GoMP notified (March 2011) MP 
panchayat Service (Gram Panchayat Secretary Recruitment and Conditions of Service) 
Rules, 2011 under which the CEO, ZP was declared appointing authority for the post of 
Panchayat Secretary.

• CEO, ZP should conduct inspection of all the JPs and any two GPs in his jurisdictions

• CEO, JP should prepare roster and conduct inspections of 10 per cent of total GPs in his

In test checked PRIs (ZPs/ JPs), we noticed that all the PRIs conducted inspection but only 
three54 PRIs prepared inspection reports  that too intermittently and remaining ZPs and JPs  
did not prepare reports/notes after inspections to be sent to higher authorities.  Five55 PRIs 
prepared rosters for inspections to be carried out in a year while 15 PRIs did not prepare any 
rosters. Further, records regarding sending the inspections reports to higher authorities were 
also not available. 
In absence of proper records (rosters, tour programme, inspection reports), we could not 
ascertain whether inspection were actually carried out or not. We also could not assess the 
impact of the inspections on the working of the PRIs.

be issued in this regard. 
2.1.12.2   Monitoring  
At state level, ACS PRDD reviews the implementation status of scheme s through video 
conferencing with all CEO of ZPs and JPs and Divisional Commissioners. 
At state level, Honourable Chief Minister conducted video conference once (Thursday) in a 
month and the minutes of the meetings were being  circulated to the ACS, PRDD  and 
Commissioner, PRD.  
Audit also noticed that CEO of selected ZPs were participating in video conference  of 
Honourable Chief Minister. PRD and five56 ZPs provided agenda of the meetings whereas 
agenda was not available i n remaining five57 ZPs. No video recording was available with 
any of the test checked ZPs. Minutes of the video meetings were uploaded on the website of 
the department. However, no records regarding remedial action taken on the issues discussed 
during the video conference was available at any level.  
Department accepted and stated (October 2022) that instructions for taking remedial action 
on minutes of video conference meeting would be issued.  
Reply of department is self-explanatory that no follow up of remedial  action on minutes of 
video conference meetings was taken by the department.

2.1.13   Ancillary issues  
2.1.13.1   Status of development Plans in PRIs 
In compliance to the  recommendation of 14th Finance Commission, the Ministry of 
Panchayati Raj (MoPR), GoI  made it a mandatory activity for GPs to prepare Gram 
Panchayat Development Plan (GPDP)  for incurring expenditure by them . The M oPR 
circulated first guideline in 2015 for this purpose and accordingly GoMP made state specific 
guidelines in 2015. In view of contemporary developments and need for comprehensive 
GPDP, the MoPR issued revised guidelines for GPDP in 2018 . As per guidelines, at State 
level, an Empowered Committee should be constituted. G Ps need to prepare a 
comprehensive perspective plan keeping in mind the resource and meticulous annual action 
plan for the current year.  The GPDPs should be approved by GS and uploaded on e -Gram 
Swaraj Portal and also sent to JP. In turn JP will consolidate all GPDPs as Block Panchayat 
Development Plan (BPDP) and send it to ZP for preparation of District Panchayat 
Development Plan ( DPDP). In this process of development planning , those projects and 
activities which are to be implemented in more than one PRI area and which can be 
implemented by higher level of PRI because of its higher institutional capacity and technical 
competency, may be referred to the JP/ZP as the case may be.  ZPs were made responsible 
for regular monitoring of implementation of GPDPs. 
Audit scrutiny of related files, minutes of the GS of test-checked GPs and Governing Bodies 
of ZPs/JPs  regarding preparation, approval revealed the following status of developmen t 
plans during 2016-21 as shown in Table-2.1.27 below:

2.1.13.2   Preparation and submission of Administrative Reports by PRIs 
As per Section 73 of PRI Act read with MP Janpad Panchayat and Zila Panchayat (Annual 
Accounts and Administrative Report) Rule 1998, annual accounts and administrative reports 
were to be prepared and submitted for approval in general bodies. Further, as per Para 4 of 
the circular issued (January 2011) , it is mandatory to submit the Administrative Reports to 
Commissioner, PRIs after approval by 30th June every year.  
At state level, we noticed that no PRIs submitted the administrative reports to the Directorate 
PRI. In reply to audit observation , the Directorate PRI  stated that updated status would be 
collected from the concerned PRIs. 
Further, in test-checked PRIs, audit noticed that none of the PRIs (ZPs/ JPs/ GPs) prepared 
administrative reports. However, CEO of six60 PRIs did not give specific comment on the 
issue and Secretary of GPs stated that no such records are available.  
Thus, due to non-availability of administrative report, various issues related to administrative 
matter of respective PRIs could not be addressed even the same was provisioned in the 
guidelines/ rules.
