Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
52 
 
2.1.14   Conclusion 
We noticed that the  objective of 73 rd CAA and PRI Act to strengthen the PRIs to make 
devolution of power and responsibilities to PRIs to ensure their effective role in 
developmental activities and local self-governance could not be achieved due to following 
reasons:  
(i) None of  the departments transferred functions as envisaged in Article 243G of        
73rd CAA except seven functions already with PRDD. 
(ii) None of the departments transferred functionaries/ officials under the control of PRIs 
even after 27 years.  
(iii) None of the departments transferred their assets to PRIs. 
(iv) In Madhya Pradesh only 30 per cent ERs and three per cent officials were trained 
during 2016-21 despite availability of funds. 
(v) Despite an increase in divisible fund, there was steep decline in devolution of funds 
(2017-18 to 2020 -21) which declined 64  from 4.17 per cent  in 2016 -17 to                     
0.79 per cent of the divisible funds in 2020-21.  
(vi) PRIs did not levy taxes adequately to fulfil their need as provided in the PRI Act. 
(vii) Due to no devolution of functions, funds and functionaries, PRIs had shortage of 
functionaries to collect taxes. 
(viii) The reservation for SC/ST/ Backward and women were adopted, but the participation 
of women in 178 meetings out of 196 meetings was one per cent to 44 per cent only. 
No meeting was conducted with desired quorum.  
(ix) The Gram Panchayats are functional with only two functionaries viz. Panchayat 
Secretary and Rojgar Sahayak only. 
We conclude that the objectives of 73rd Constitutional amendment could not be achieved so 
far as the functions, functionaries and funds were not transferred effectively to the PRIs as 
envisaged in the Act and they could not be transformed into institutions of self-governance 
even after lapse of 27 years since enactment of the Act. 
2.1.15  Recommendations 
(i) GoMP should issue notification regarding devolution of functions and frame rules 
accordingly. 
(ii) GoMP should frame timeline for constitution of SFCs, submission of reports by SFC 
and furt her for acceptance by the Government and submission of ATR on the 
recommendations of SFC. 
(iii) The department should monitor progress of trainings being imparted by the training 
institutes as per the trainings calendar for the year for achievement of targets. 
(iv) GoMP should strengthen Social Audit System to bring more transparency in working 
of Panchayats. 
                                                           
64  Devolution of divisible funds 2016-17 (4.17 %), 2017-18 (3.44 %), 2018-19 (2.88 %), 2019-20 (1.03 %) 
and 2020-21 (0.79 %). 

 
Chapter II: Performance Audit 
 
53 
 
(v) GoMP should devolve all funds and functionaries to PRIs of 29 functions enlisted in 
11th schedule of the Constitution so that PRIs may increase their revenue col lection 
capabilities. 
(vi) GoMP should transfer the fund relating to Grant No. 53 to PRIs only after transfer of 
remaining 22 functions.  
(vii) GoMP should release the divisible fund as per accepted formula of SFC. 
(viii) GPs should drive campaigns and motivate residents of  the villages to deposit taxes. 
GoMP should conduct Information, Education and Communication (IEC) activities 
for awareness among villagers regarding payment of taxes.  
(ix) GoMP should ini tiate substantial number of awareness drives to generate interest 
among the citizens to participate in decision making for village development. 
(x) GPs should convene the Gram Sabha on holidays or festival days so that participation 
of villagers increases.   
(xi) The CEOs and top district officials need to handhold GPs and create an at mosphere 
of participation.                              
(xii) GoMP may also enlist the help of some institutions like National Institute for Rural 
Development and Panchayati Raj (NIRD&PR), S tate Institute for Rural 
Development (SIRD) etc. to generate interest. 
(xiii) GoMP should frame PESA Rules immediately for effective implementation of PESA 
Act in scheduled area of Madhya Pradesh. (Subsequently, GoMP framed (November 
2022) PESA Rules) 
(xiv) GoMP should review the reasons for shortage and initiate timebound recruitment to 
fill up the shortage. 
(xv) Department should conduct inspections to improve effective implementation of 
activities for the better governance in PRIs. 

 
 
Panchayat and Rural Development Department 
 
2.2  Performance Audit on Implementation of Pradhan Mantri Awaas  
Yojna - Gramin 
Executive Summary 
Public housing programme was a major focus area of the Government as an instrument of 
poverty alleviation. Government of India (GoI) introduced (April 2016) Pradhan Mantri 
Aawas Yojna - Gramin (PMAY-G) with the objective to provide a Pucca houses with 
basic amenities to all the houseless households and those households living in kutcha and 
dilapidated houses in rural areas by 2022. Beneficiaries of PMAY-G were to be selected 
using housing deprivation parameter in the Socio Economic and Caste Census (SECC), 
2011 data . P ayment of assistance  was to be made  in beneficiaries’  bank/post office 
accounts through Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) in four instalments , based on the stages 
of construction.  PMAY-G, programme implementation and monitoring are executed 
through “AwaasSoft”, a web based transactional electronic service delivery platform.  
The per formance of implementat ion of PMAY -G covering the period 2016 -21 was 
reviewed by test-checking of the records of Directorate, PRDD, 10 selected ZP (districts), 
20 JPs (two in each audited ZP) and 60 GPs (three GPs in each audited JPs) during August 
and November 2021, which revealed the following: 
• GoMP transferred GoI share of ` 13,937.21 crore with a delay of 3 days to 11 months 
from State Consolidated fund to State Nodal Account (SNA) . State corresponding 
matching share amounting of ` 8,989.89 crore was also released with a delay of one day 
to 11 months to SNA.  
(Paragraph 2.2.6.1(a)) 
• 1st instalment of assistance amount was released with a delay ranged between one day 
and 4 years to 53 per cent  (13,18,233 beneficiaries), not released to 14 per cent 
(3,48,319) beneficiaries and rel eased within the prescribed time period  to 33 per cent 
(8,38,324 out of 25,04,876) beneficiaries.    
(Paragraph 2.2.6.1(b)) 
• In selected districts , PMAY-G assistance of ` 15.66 crore was released to 1,555  
beneficiaries, who possessed 2/3/4 wheeler vehicle be fore sanction of house  and were 
ineligible under the scheme.  
(Paragraph 2.2.6.2(c)) 
• Analysis of SECC data revealed that house was sanctioned twice to same beneficiary in 
64 cases. In 98 cases, one hous e was sanctioned to the actual beneficiary and another  
house was sanctioned to his/her family members who were not identified for PMAY-G 
scheme as per SECC data. 
                                                                                                        (Paragraph 2.2.6.2(d)) 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
56 
 
• Assistance of ` 2.74 crore was released to 317 beneficiaries, who were decided ineligible 
by the Appellate Committee of five selected districts (Alirajpur, Dhar, Gwalior, Mandla 
and Ratlam). Further, assistance of ` 42.20 lakh was provided to 36 beneficiaries of 
three selected districts (Dhar, Ratlam and Gwalior), who were listed as in -eligible on 
grounds of pucca house, CM Awaas/IAY by respective GPs. 
                                                               (Paragraph 2.2.6.2(e) & Paragraph 2.2.6.2(f)) 
• PMAY-G house was sanctioned to the minors in 90 cases (out of 140 test-checked) and 
benefit was provided to their relatives, whose names were not there in SECC 2011 list. 
                                                                                                         (Paragraph 2.2.6.3(f)) 
• 14 per cent (2.51 lakh out 18.28 lakh  sanctioned during 2016 -20) of completion of 
houses with a delay of one day to 3 years, leading to non-achievement of objective of 
the scheme to provide house within the targeted time.      
                                                                                                      (Paragraph 2.2.6.4(a)ii)                                                                                                       
•  As per NIC data, 1st inspection of 23.98 lakh houses sanctioned during 2016 -21 in the 
state, was to be done up to 31st March 2021. We noted that no inspection was conducted 
in 18 per cent (4.20 lakh) cases and in 23 per cent (5.52 lakh) cases inspection was done 
after three months with a delay of one day to 4 years, leading to delay in release of 
subsequent 2nd instalment to beneficiaries required to continue the construction work.  
(Paragraph 2.2.6.5(a)) 
• Assistance to the beneficiary was to be released based on uploading the photograph of 
construction stage  on AwaasSoft . We noted that in 70 cases only completion level 
photographs were uploaded  on AwaasSoft . Second instalment was released in 2262 
cases, wherein photograph of construction up to Plinth level were not uploaded. Third 
instalment was released in 710 cases, wherein photograph of construction up to Lintel 
level was not uploaded. 
(Paragraph 2.2.6.5(b)) 
 
2.2.1   Introduction  
Public housing programme was a major focus area of the Government as an instrument of 
poverty alleviatio n. Government of India (GoI) introduced (April 2016)  Pradhan Mantri 
Aawas Yojna - Gramin (PMAY-G) to fulfil Government’s commitment to provide ‘Housing 
for All’ by 2022 . Under PMAY -G, the funding patter n between Central and State 
Government was 60:40 during 2016-21. 
The objective of the Scheme was to provide Pucca houses with basic amenities to all the 
houseless households and those households living in kutcha and dilapidated houses in rural 
areas by 2022. To achieve the objective of ‘Housing for All’, the overall targeted number of 
houses to be constructed by the year 2021 -22 was 2.95 crore  in the country . In M adhya 
Pradesh (MP) 26,28,593 houses were targeted to be completed during 2016-21. 
Further, under PMAY-G, programme implementation and monitoring are executed through 
“AwaasSoft”, which is a web based transactional electronic service delivery platform. A brief 
of the scheme is detailed in Table-2.2.1.

One of the most important features of PMAY -G is the selection of beneficiaries to ensure

objective and verifiable. Beneficiaries of the scheme are selected using housing deprivation

parameters1 in the Socio Economic and Caste Census (SECC), 2011 data which was required 
to be verified by the Gram Sabhas. 
Flow Chart of registration, sanction and release of benefit in PMAY-G 
 
Under PMAY-G all payments to the beneficiary were required to be made electronically into 
their bank/post office accounts that are linked with Aadhaar.  Since inception (April 2016) 
of PMAY-G, all beneficiaries were benefitted using Direct Benefit Transfer  (DBT). The 
fund relating to sch eme is placed in a dedicated single bank account in a 
scheduled/commercial bank at the State level called as State Nodal Account (SNA) and is 
operated electronically through Fund Transfer Order (FTO). Assistance to beneficia ries is

2.2.2   Organisational structure for implementation of the scheme 
PRDD is the nodal department for implementation of the scheme in the State. A dditional 
Chief Secretary cum Development Commissioner (ACS & DC) oversees implementation of 
the scheme in the State. ACS&DC  is assisted by the Director, PMAY-G who is the Nodal 
Officer for the scheme. At the district level, Chief Executive Officer (CEO), Zila Panchayat 
(ZP) who is assisted by Project Officers (POs)  implement the Scheme. At Block and field 
level, CEO, Janpad Panchayat (JP) is responsible for implementation of the scheme with the 
assistance of Gram Panchayat (GP). At State level, a Programme Management Unit (PMU) 
headed by the State Nodal Officer is also functioning for guidance, implementation  and 
monitoring. Flow chart showing the responsibilities from GoI to different implementing 
levels is given as:

Chapter II: Performance Audit

2.2.3   Audit objectives 
The Performance Audit was conducted to assess: 
• the mechanism for identification and selection of beneficiaries under the Scheme was 
transparent and adequate; 
• the system of inspection and monitoring for timely completion of dwelling units were 
in compliance with the Scheme guidelines; and 
• effective and adequate financial management system.  
2.2.4   Audit Scope and Methodology 
2.2.4.1   Sampling 
Performance Audit on implementation of PMAY -G covering the period 2016 -21 was 
conducted (between August and  November 2021) by test -checking of the  records of 
Directorate, Panchayat and Rural Development  Department (PRDD) and 10 selected ZPs 
(districts). Nine2 districts including three tribal populated districts were selected on the basis 
of expenditure, tribal population, geographical location, train connectivity amid Covid and 
Gwalior district was selected for the pilot study. 
Further, we test checked records of PMAY-G in 20 JPs (two in each audited ZP) and 60 GPs 
(three GPs in each audited JPs) selected on logical 3 basis. The details of selected ZPs, JPs 
and GPs are given in Appendix-2.2.1. Further, we also conducted Joint Physical Inspection 
(JPI) of 15 houses constructed under PMAY-G in each selected GP (15x60 = 900) with the 
departmental officials. 
During 2016-21, a total of 26,28,525 houses (21,64,522 houses (82.35 per cent) completed) 
were sanctioned in the State and an amount of ` 24,723.96 crore was paid to beneficiaries. 
In 10 audited districts, ` 5,936.59 crore was paid to beneficiaries for construction of 6,50,715 
houses (24.75 per cent of total houses sanctioned in the State), out of which 5,34,816 houses 
(82.19 per cent) were completed. 
The methodol ogy adopted  for audit  was analysis of IT data (beneficiar y registration, 
sanction, release of instalments and inspection etc. from the scheme management software) 
and test checking of files and records maintained by the selected units and JPI of houses. 
An Entry conference with Additional Chief Secretary cum Development Commissioner , 
PRDD, Bhopal was held on 01 December 2020 to discuss the objectives, scope, criteria and 
audit methodology. 
                                                           
2 Alirajpur, Chhattarpur, Damoh, Dhar, Guna, Mandla, Rajgarh, Ratlam  and Satna. Out of these, 
Alirajpur, Dhar and Mandla are tribal districts. 
3 Before field work, we analyzed PMAY -G data collected from National informatics Centre (NIC) and 
on the basis of data analysis we found the number of cases of sanction of houses un der PMAY-G. 
During field audit we prioritized these JPs and GPs for detailed scrutiny.

It can be seen from above table, that out of total funds released by the GOI/State/other

2.2.6.1(a)  Delay in release of Central and State share in State Nodal Account  
GoI sanction orders specified that State Government was required to transfer the fund from 
the Consolidated Fund of the State to the bank account o f the State implementing age ncy 
dedicated to PMAY -G within three  days of receipt from GoI . The State share was also 
required to be released within 15 days from the date of release of GoI share.  
We noticed that during 2016-21, GoMP delayed (ranged from three to 328 days) transfer of 
GoI share amounting to ` 13,937.21 crore from State Consolidated fund to SNA. Similarly, 
GoMP delayed (ranged from one to 316 days) release of State share amounting to ` 8,989.89 
crore to SNA. 
We further noticed that d uring 2019-20, GoMP transferred ` 216.63 crore (Central share       
` 129.98 crore and State share ` 86.65 crore) to Personal Deposit (PD) account in March 
2020 and further transferred to SNA in May 2020 . Further, during 2020 -21, GoMP 
transferred ` 764.26 crore out of ` 771.63 crore (State share) to PD account in March 2021 
which was further transferred to SNA in July 2021. GoMP transferred remaining State share 
of ` 7.38 crore out of the ` 771.63 crore to SNA in next financial year i.e. 2021-22 (sanction 
order 11.10.2021). Thus, the GoMP did not follow the directions of the GoI. 
Government stated (July 2022) that the amount (matching State share of the received Central 
share) could not be withdrawn on time due to non -availability of budget provisioned under 
PMAY-G. It was further stated that due to the provision made in the third supplementary 
budget, the amount could not be withdrawn at the end of the financial year, so the amount 
was deposited in the PD account.  
The reply of the Government is not acceptable as GoI sa nction order and PMAY -G 
guidelines clearly stipulates that State government must relea se/transfer GoI funds and its 
matching share into SNA within three and 15 days respectively.  
2.2.6.1(b)  Release of instalment to sanctioned houses 
As per para 9.2.1(D) of framework for implementation of PMAY -G, 1st instalment should 
be released within 15 days to the beneficiary account after sanction of house.

and due for 1 st instalment in  the state . Details of release of 1 st instalment are  given in

2.2.6.2   Identification and selection of beneficiaries 
Paragraph 4 of framework for implementation of PMAY-G envisaged that identification and 
prioritization of the beneficiaries will be done based on housing deprivation parameters in 
the SECC-2011 data. Priority was to be  assigned based on parameters reflecting housing 
deprivation in each category viz. SC, ST, Minorities and Others in each priority groups (in 
order of Houselessness followed by the number of rooms; Zero, One and Two rooms).  In 
two sub -groups (Automatic Included Households 6 and Other Households ), Automatic 
Included Households shall not rank lower than other households within a priority group. 
Inter-se priority within the two sub -groups will be determined based on their cumulative 
deprivation scores which are calculated from the socio -economic pa rameters with each 
having equal weight. Households with higher deprivation scores will be ranked higher within 
the sub-groups. 
Once the category wise priority lists are generated from SECC data and suitably publicized, 
a Gram Sabha will be convened. The Gram Sabha will verify the fac ts based on which the 
households are identified as eligible. Complaints regarding wrongful deletion/changed 
ranking are examined by an Appellate Committee 7 constituted by the State Government. 
Thereafter, GP wise final PWL for each category will be publish ed on the notice board of 
GP and entered on AwaasSoft.  
 
                                                           
5  210 cases in 2016 -17, 313 cases in 2017 -18, 550 cases in 2018 -19, 3802 in 2019 -20 and 3,43, 444 in        
2020-21. 
6  Households without shelter, destitute/ living on alms, Manual scavengers, primitive tribal groups, 
legally released bonded labourer. 
7  District Magistrate/Collector or his nominee, another official and at least one non-official member. 

 
Chapter II: Performance Audit  
65 
 
Audit generated a report from AwaasSoft portal (on 13th Dec 2021) and noticed that a total 
of 47,41 ,213 beneficiaries in the State were identified  based on  housing deprivation 
parameters for providing benefit under the PMAY-G during 2016-22. The Gram Sabha, after 
deletion of 16,54,806 beneficiaries who were found ineligible on verification of facts of 
eligibility and identified 30,86,407 eligible beneficiaries for PMAY-G. 
Discrepancies noticed in priority setting and eligibility verification are discussed below:  
2.2.6.2(a) Setting of Priority  
Separate priority lists, satisfying the principles of prioritization, w ere required to be 
generated for SC, ST, Minorities and Others for each Gram Panchayat subject to availability 
of eligible households. System generated category wise ranked priority list could be 
downloaded from the MIS-AwaasSoft. Thereon, the lists will be circulated to the concerned 
GPs for verification by Gram Sabha. 
We scrutinized Priority setting for allocation of ranks as per priority groups, sub-groups and 
deprivation score as enunciated in the guidelines in the selected GPs. We noticed from the 
priority lists downloaded from the AwaasSoft that setting of priority in 19 GPs out of selected 
60 GPs was not done  as per norms. Due to non-setting of priority  as per norms , the 
beneficiaries who had more deprivation score were ranked lower to those beneficiaries who 
had lower deprivation score. Details are given in Appendix-2.2.2 
Government stated (July 2022) that priority of eligible household was decided on the basis 
of their category. It is further stated that priority list was revised after approval of Gram 
Sabha which was initially competent for revision.  
The reply is not acc eptable as the Department did not prioritize the beneficiaries as per 
norms. Further, the reply is general in nature and not specific to observations. 
2.2.6.2(b) Non-adherence of priority list 
During scrutiny of sanction lists of houses  in 60 audited GPs, we noticed  that out of total 
18,935 sanctioned cases, 8,226 beneficiaries superseded the more deprived beneficiaries in 
the priority list and were sanctioned house earlier than them. CEO of concerned ZPs did not 
adhere to the sequence of priority number in PWL in sanctioning house.  
Government stated (July 2022)  that the implementation and progress of the scheme are 
weekly reviewed at the State level by the Additional Chief Secretary/Principal Secretary. 
The sanction of houses against the target allotted to the distric ts are also reviewed. It is 
further stated that the process of sanctioning a house to the beneficiar y is adopted so as to 
sanction the next prioritized beneficiary of the priority list in cases where  beneficiaries in 
the list have migrated, died without an heir, unable to provide required documents for online 
registration etc. 
The reply is not acceptable  as Department did not submit any record in support of their 
contention. Further, sanction of house was not done as per priority as stipulated in the 
guidelines.

two/three/four-wheeler/fishing boat etc. are to be automatically excluded. Further, ACS & 
DC, PRDD instructed (March 2017) that as per registration of vehicle in Regional Transport 
Office (RTO), beneficiaries who purchased new motorized two-wheeler vehicles after 2016 
would be ineligible for the PMAY-G. 
Scrutiny revealed that 2,0378 beneficiaries out of t otal 6 ,24,053 beneficiaries had 2/3/4 
wheeler vehicle before sanction of house in audited 10 districts. But these beneficiaries who 
were in possession  of motorized two/three/four-wheeler vehicles were sanctioned house 
under PMAY -G during 2016 -21. We furthe r noticed that CEO, JP released PMAY -G 
assistance of ` 15.66 crore to 1,555 out of 2,037 ineligible beneficiaries. Details are given 
in Appendix-2.2.3. District-wise identified cases are given below in Table-2.2.4.

Panchayat Secretary and Panchayat Co-ordinating Officer of concerned GPs were required

2.2.6.2(d) Benefit provided to more than one beneficiary in same household 
During analysis of SECC data audit noticed  that in 13,743 entries, out of 37 ,00,058 
registered in AwaasSoft, the details of the household were found to be repeating multiple 
times. This duplication ranged between two to 25 times. We noticed that 324 (162 duplicate) 
out of 13,743 beneficiaries repeating two times were sanctioned house. On further scrutiny 
of 162 duplicate cases, we found that same beneficiary was sanctioned twice in 64 cases and 
in 98 cases one house was sanctioned to the actual beneficiary and another house was 
sanctioned to his/her family members  during registration of beneficiary who w ere not 
identified for PMAY-G scheme as per SECC data.  
We have noticed that there were multiple entries of sa me person having same household in 
SECC database. Against these multiple entries, multiple PMAY -G registration numbers 
(field “PMAY Id” in AwaasSoft) have been allotted in such cases. This AwaasSoft data was 
sent to State Government for verification of eligibility of registered persons at Gram Sabha/ 
Gram Panchayat level. It was noticed that department also deleted/stopped sanctions of 
multiple entries. 
From the above, it can be i nferred that there is no system to alert in the portal to identify 
duplication of beneficiaries . Duplicate beneficiaries should have been deleted instead of 
providing assistance twice or diversion of assistance to family members. 
Government did not submit the relevant or specific reply to audit observation.   
2.2.6.2(e) Benefit provided to ineligible beneficiaries declared by Appellate Committee 
As per para 4.5.3 of framework for implementation of PMAY-G, the State government shall 
constitute a three-member Appellate Committee (AC) at the district level headed by District 
Magistrate/Collector or a nominee , another official and at least one non –official member. 
As per the order (March 2017) of ACS & DC, PRDD, the AC shall review the reason of in-
eligibility of those household declared in-eligible at JP level and decide the eligibility.  
During scrutiny of records of ZP s (Alirajpur, Dhar, Gwalior , Mandla  and Ratlam ), we 
observed that JPs of these districts forwarded 1,8 66 cases to AC for consideration  of 
eligibility. Out of 1,866, AC declared eligible to 819 beneficiaries and decided 746 cases as 
ineligible and decision was pending in 301 cases. We noticed that after decided by AC as

Action Plan (AAP) approved by the Empowered Committee of the Ministry of Rural 
Development, GoI. After approval from the Ministry, the State was to finalize district wise 
and category wise targets and upload the same on the AwaasSoft.  As per para 5.3.1 , the 
beneficiaries were to be registered on MIS AwaasSoft after receipt of targets for the year.

It is evident from the above table that  the 57 per cent houses (15.09 lakh against allocated

This shows that the instructions as prescribed for registration of beneficiaries and sancti on 
of houses in PMAY -G guidelines w ere not adhered and indicates lack of monitoring by 
CEO-JP/ZP and State level. 
Government stated (July 2022) that there was a provision on the Awaas portal that the 
beneficiary could be registered before allotting the tar get but the process of sanction of the 
house could only be done after allotting the target. It is also stated that for convenience of 
work, more beneficiaries could be registered before allotment of target.  
The reply is not acceptable as Department sanctioned and released the first instalment to the 
beneficiaries in addition to registration of houses which was  contrary to  norms of the 
guidelines. 
2.2.6.3(b) Basic details of beneficiary not entered in the AwaasSoft  
According to Para 5.3.1 of framework for implementation of the PMAY -G, the details of 
bank account and  MGNREGA Job card  of PMAY -G beneficiary  was required to be 
mandatorily entered during registration. In addition, the Aadhaar number of the PMAY-G 
beneficiary was also required to be seeded on AwaasSoft. 
2.2.6.3(b)i  Status of registration of MGNREGA Job Cards 
We test checked authenticity of MGNREGA Job Card  of 2,000 out of total sanctioned 
18,935 PMAY-G beneficiaries in selected 60 GPs on MIS-NREGASoft portal and found 
1,377 Job Cards were available on MIS-NREGASoft portal and remaining 623 MGNREGA 
Job Card number were not found in MIS -NREGA Soft portal. Further, out of 1,377  Job 

 
Chapter II: Performance Audit  
71 
 
Cards, 1,097 Job Cards matched with the PMAY -G beneficiary households and remaining 
280 Job Cards belonged to household other than the beneficiary of the PMAY-G. 
Thus, Panchayat Secretary of the concerned GP instead of entering MGNREGA job card of 
the actual beneficiary accepted MGNREGA Job Card of person other than the beneficiary  
household of the PMAY-G. Further, at the time of entry of MGNREGA Job Card at JP level, 
genuineness of MGNREGA Job Card was also not verified. 
Government stated (July 2022) that houses could not be sanctioned to the beneficiary without 
a job card and it was only acceptable if beneficiary was relat ive of the head of the job card 
holder and his name was included as a member of the family in that job card.  
Reply is not acceptable as 623 MGNREGA Job Cards were missing in MIS -NREGASoft 
portal and 280 job cards belonged to households other than beneficiary of PMAY-G.  
2.2.6.3(b)ii  Status of Aadhaar card of beneficiaries 
We test checked information collected from 3,000 beneficiary out of 18,935 PMAY-G 
beneficiaries in selected 60 GPs and noticed that  out of 3,000, in 2,93113 cases (97.70 per 
cent) Panchayat Secretary collected Aadhaar numbers of the beneficiaries. 
Data in MIS report  on AwaasSoft revealed that Aadhaar of only 847 out  of these 3,000 
beneficiaries (28.33 per cent) were seeded whereas 97.70 per cent of beneficiaries provided 
Aadhaar number. This shows that Aadhaar number of the beneficiaries were collected by the 
GPs but its seeding was not being done in AwaasSoft by Janpad Panchayats.  
Entry of Aadhaar number ensures greater transparency and accountability. Aadhaar obviates 
fake/ghost beneficiaries by elimination through de-duplication. Once Aadhaar Card number 
is provided by the beneficiary it should be entered in the system to identify the true identity 
of the person who has been provided  the benefit . Non-entering of A adhaar number in 
registration reflects lack of monitoring in implementation of scheme. 
Government stated (July 2022) that entering an Aadhaar number is not mandatory on the 
Awaas Portal as per the guideline.  
The reply of the Government is factually correct but there is a provision for seeding aadhaar 
number of PMA Y-G beneficiaries on Awaassoft portal. If  Aadhaar number was collected 
from the beneficiary, same should be entered in the Awaas Portal for better transparency. 
2.2.6.3(c)    Status of Bank accounts 
PMAY-G guidelines (Para 5.3.1) and Development Commissioner, PRDD order (September 
2016) stipulates opening of bank account of the beneficiary in case, the beneficiary have no 
bank account.   
                                                           
13 2,755 Aadhaar number belonged to beneficiaries, 8 3 Aadhaar number belonged to relatives, 20 
Aadhaar number belonged to another person and in 73 cases Aadhaar number cou ld not be verified 
as either copy of the Aadhaar not found in record or name was not clear in the copy of the Aadhaar 
card. 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
72 
 
We verified b ank accounts number of 3,00014 beneficiaries in selected 60 GPs form bank 
payment list. We noticed that out of 3,000 bank accounts, 2,884 bank accounts belonged to 
the beneficiary, 88 bank accounts belonged to family members of the beneficiaries, 24 bank 
accounts belonged to others and  in four cases benefit provided  in wrong ac count (bank 
account of other person given by beneficiary).  
Thus, in case of 116 bank accounts (3.87 per cent), the Panchayat Secretary of the concerned 
GP did not ensure collection of bank account details which pertain to the beneficiary and in 
case the beneficiary had no bank account, opening of bank account was not ensured. Further, 
concerned JPs also did not ensure that the bank accounts belonged to the beneficiary which 
was against the PMAY -G guidelines/instructions and can lead to transfer of benefit to the 
wrong person and may defeat the very objective of the scheme. 
Government stated (July 2022) that the joint account was valid as per guidelines and there 
was a process of recovery of the amount if the wrong account is registered on the portal. The 
account registered on the portal is sent to PFMS for verification of names as per SECC and 
PFMS/Bank have to ascertain that the name of the beneficiary as entered in  AwaasSoft 
matches with the name of the account holder. The instalment is paid to the beneficiary at the 
district level only when both names (SECC & PFMS/Bank) are matched.  
Reply is not acceptable as the benefit was transferred to another person's bank account whose 
name did not match with beneficiary. This indicates that the similarity of names in SECC & 
PFMS/Bank was not verified by block official. 
2.2.6.3(d) Benefit provided to other person 
PMAY-G guidelines (Para 4.4.1) stipulates that if the inclusion has been done based on 
wrong facts, if the household has constructed a pucca house or has been allotted a house 
under any government scheme or migrated since the time of the survey or has died leaving 
no successor, the Gram Sabha shal l delete the name of such families from the system 
generated priority list. Also, Director, PMAY-G vide order dated January 2018 clarified that 
if name of the nominee is not available in SECC data, required details were to be sent to the 
department for inclusion in SECC data. 
Scrutiny of 530 beneficiary records in selected six GPs of Gwalior district, revealed that five 
beneficiaries died before sanction of house but name of the beneficiary was not changed in 
MIS and house was sanctioned  in the name of deceased person . We noticed that  during 
registration, personal information (bank account etc.) of family members/other person was 
collected and benefit provided. In two cases name of family member of t he beneficiaries 
were not available in SECC family member details, however, assistance of PMAY -G was 
provided to different person not related to beneficiary.  
Reply of Government is awaited.  
 
 
                                                           
14  520 female and 2,480 male and consisting of 1,571 ST, 346 SC, 1,079 others and four minority.  

 
Chapter II: Performance Audit  
73 
 
2.2.6.3(e) Benefit provided to beneficiaries with no name   
Scrutiny of AwaasSoft data revealed that name of the beneficiaries were not mentioned in 
1,246 out of 25,24,951 sanctioned beneficiaries in the State during 2016-21. We noticed that 
either name column  was left blank or dash (..) were entered  or “unknown” mentioned in 
place of name. Further, in 950 out of the 1 ,246 cases, benefit was released. This indicates 
lack of monitoring by the CEO of concerned JP and ZP who sanctioned the benefit despite 
non-availability of name of the beneficiary.  
Government stated (July 2022) that by matching the names of family members in the SECC 
family details on the AwaasSoft portal, the benefit of the house could be given even if it was 
mentioned as “unknown” in place of the name of head of that family.  
Reply is not acceptable as family details were found as blank/unknown in 119 cases  out of 
207 verified cases in the AwaasSoft portal. This shows benefit has been provided to no 
name/unknown cases without mentioning family details in these cases in SECC data.  
2.2.6.3(f) House sanctioned to Minor 
As per point no. 21 in FAQs issued for implementation of PMAY -G, minor should not be 
sanctioned houses under the Scheme . However, in point no. 22 of the FA Qs it has been 
clarified that if beneficiary is minor and their father’s name is  included in SECC 2011 list, 
then house may be sanctioned to the father . Further, m inutes of Empowered Committee 
meeting (June 2017) stipulates that in cases where Minor name i s written as family head 
even though the parents are alive, it might be treated as wrong data feeding issue during the 
SECC survey and data correction is required. 
During scrutiny of records in audited 13 JPs, audit noticed that in 90 cases out of test checked 
140 cases15 houses were sanctioned to the minors as the name of adult family members were 
not included in family details in SECC List. The house under the scheme was sanctioned to 
minor without updating/ rectifying the details of family members in SECC  data. However, 
benefits were provided to family mem ber (adult)  by taking their documents during 
registration whose names were not there in SECC 2011 list. This shows that the actual benefit 
was provided to the person whose name was not in the SECC 2011 list. 
Government stated (July 2022) that according to GoI instructions, the benefit to eligible 
minor beneficiary had been given by entering the information and documents of guardian of 
minor in AwaasSoft portal.    
Reply is not acceptable as  the Department did not correct the SECC data as directed in 
meeting of the Empowered Committee and sanctioned the houses to the minors. 
2.2.6.3(g) Allotment of PMAY-G house   
As per Para 5.3.2 of framework for implementation of  PMAY-G, allotment of house shall 
be made jointly in the name of husband and wife except i n the case of a 
widow/unmarried/separated person. The State may also choose to allot it solely in the name 
of the woman. In the case of beneficiaries selected under the quota for persons with

2.2.6.4(c)i    Construction of toilet 
Construction of toilet was made an integral part of the PMAY-G house.  The toilet shall be 
provided to beneficiaries through funding from Swachh Bharat Mission-Gramin (SBM-G), 
MGNREGA or any other dedicated financing sources. The house shall be treated as 
complete only after construction of the toilet. 
In the JPI of 744 completed houses under PMAY-G, audit observed that 560 houses (75.27 
per cent) had toilets while 184 houses (24.73 per cent) were found without toilets. Still these 
houses were shown as ‘completed’ (Table-2.2.17) while as per framework, the house was 
to be treated as ‘complete’ only after completion of construction of the toilet. 
Government stated (July 2022) that the SBM (Swachh Bharat Mission) scheme ha d been 
operational since a decade ago and if the beneficiary had already been given the benefits of 
the toilet in the past, then benefit could not be given again.  
Reply is not acceptable as 184 houses were found without toilet and it was the responsibility 
of the department to ensure the construction of toilet in house before treating as complete.  
2.2.6.4(c)ii   Access to safe drinking water 
The beneficiary of PMAY -G should be provided access to safe drinking water in the 
convergence with the National Rural Drinking Water Programme (NRDWP) of the Ministry 
of Drinking Water and Sanitation or any other similar schemes. 
JPI of 744 completed houses under PMAY-G revealed that 499 (67.07 per cent) houses had 
access to safe drinking water either through convergence or by their own arrangement 19.   
                                                           
19  Tube well, hand pump, borewell etc. 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
82 
 
215 (28.90 per cent) houses had access to water through river, well etc. Remaining 30 houses 
were facing difficulty in accessing clean drinking water (see Table-2.2.17). 
Government stated (July 2022) that efforts were being made to provide clean drinking water 
to the beneficiaries.  
The reply is not acceptable as the department did not ensure safe d rinking water to each 
household of PMAY-G. 
2.2.6.4(c)iii  Electricity connection to beneficiary houses 
Convergence with Deendayal Upadhayaya Gram Jyoti Yojna (DDUGJY) of Ministry of 
Power and any other related schemes was proposed in the framework to provide electricity 
connection to the beneficiary house under PMAY-G. 
JPI of 7 44 completed houses revealed that 697 houses ( 93.68 per cent ) had electricity 
connection either through convergence or through their own arrangement. Thus, 4 7 
constructed houses ( 6.32 per cent ) remained without electri city connection. (see  
Table-2.2.17). 
The Government did not submit the reply to audit observation. 
2.2.6.4(c)iv  Clean and efficient cooking fuel to the beneficiaries 
To provide clean and more efficient cooking fuel to the beneficiaries of PMAY-G, the State 
should strive to get LPG connections to them under Pradhan Mantri Ujjwala Yojna (PMUY) 
of Ministry of Petroleum and Natural Gas.  
In the JPI of 7 44 completed houses,  it was seen that 55 6 houses ( 74.73 per cent ) were 
provided with LPG connections and in 188 houses ( 25.27 per cent ) there was no LPG 
connections (see Table-2.2.17). 
Government accepted the facts and stated  (July 2022) that according to the eligibility, the 
benefit of Ujjwala scheme was being given gradually to the beneficiaries. 
2.2.6.4(c)v  Connectivity of houses with road. 
States may, through convergence with MGNREGA, develop group/individual amenities like 
development of house sites, bio fencing, paved pathways, approach roads or steps to the 
house, soil conservation and protection works etc., for the beneficiaries of PMAY-G. 
JPI of 744 completed houses revealed that 709 houses (95.30 per cent) had road connectivity 
while 3 5 houses ( 4.70 per cent ) still required road connectivity as on November 2021  
(see Table-17). 
The Government did not submit the reply to audit observation. 
2.2.6.4.3(c)vi  Irregularities in payment of wages under MGNREGA  
As per para 8.1 (b) of  framework for implementation of PMAY -G, it has been mandatory 
that the beneficiary of PMAY -G would get wage component for 90 20 person days at the 
current rates with regard to the unskilled labour component for construction of the house 
                                                           
20   95 person days in hilly states, difficult areas and IAP districts

under convergence with MGNREGA. Server to server integration between AwaasSoft and 
NREGASoft has been developed so that work for construction of house is generated on

• MGNREGA wage provided without release of any instalment for construction of 
house

 Audit noticed that 76,917 beneficiaries (4.20 per cent) out of 18,27,983 completed houses 
during 2016-17 to 2020-21 were not paid MGNREGS wage. 
• MGNREGA wage provided for extra mandays 
It has been observed that 1,30,095 out 25,24,951 total sanctioned cases during 2016-17 to 
2020-21 were paid 8,52,556 excess person days than admissible 90/95 days. 
The Government accepted the facts and stated (July 2022) that audit suggestions will be 
taken care of in future. 
2.2.6.5   Monitoring and Inspection 
Monitoring under PMAY -G is conceived to b e multi-level and multi -agency with profuse 
use of technology. 
2.2.6.5(a)   Lack of Monitoring

of sanction . Gram Panchayat Secretary/Gram Rojgar Sahayak  of the resp ective GP is 
responsible for conducting 1st inspection and uploading photographs on AwaasSoft. 
As per data provided by NIC, out of total 25,24,951 sanctions during 2016-21 in the state, 
1st Inspection was to be done in 23,97,563 houses  up to March 2021.  The status of 1 st 
inspection conducted by the department is given in Table-2.2.18.

the houses by beneficiary within time. It is further stated that the 2nd instalment delayed due

instalment used in personal needs or beneficiary migrated for long period.  
The reply clearly indicates that the first inspection was  not done by Gram Panchayat 
Secretary/Gram Rojgar Sahayak within time as stipulated in scheme guidelines. 
2.2.6.5(b)  Irregularities in uploading of photographs 
As per framework for implementation of PMAY -G, photographs of every house should be 
taken before, during and after construction of the house. The mobile application 
“AwaasApp” may be used  to capture geo -referenced and time stamped photograph and 
uploaded on the AwaasSoft. The release of assistance to the beneficiary is based on the 
photograph of the stage of construction uploaded on AwaasSoft. The chronological stage of 
uploading of photograph is: 
1. Existing Site 
2. Proposed Site 
3. House Sanctioned (release of 1st instalment) 
4. Plinth (release of 2nd instalment) 
5. Lintel (release of 3rd instalment) 
6. Completed (release of 4th instalment) 
Out of 25,24,951 sanctioned cases in MP  during 2016 -21, following irregularities were 
noticed: 
• In 70 cases only completion level photographs were uploaded on AwaasSoft. 
• In 2,262 cases where 2 nd instalment was  released without uploading Plinth level 
photograph. In 908 out of 2,262 cases, plinth level photographs were uploaded after 
release of 2nd instalment with delay ranging from one to 1,192 days. 
• In 710 cases where 3 rd instalment was released without uploading Lintel level 
photograph. In 239 out of 710 cases, lintel level photographs were uploaded after 
release of 3rd instalment with delay ranging from two to 1,272 days. 
• In 20 cases where final instalment was released without uploading Completion level 
photograph. In 5 out of 20 cases, completion level photographs were uploaded after 
release of final instalment with delay ranging from 17 to 87 days. 
• In 1,001 cases Lintel level photographs w ere uploaded before uploading of Plinth 
level photograph. Further, in 56 cases Completion level photographs were uploaded 
before uploading Lintel level photographs. 

 
Chapter II: Performance Audit  
85 
 
• Plinth and Lintel level photographs were uploaded on same day i n 5,741 cases and 
Lintel and Completion level photographs w ere uploaded on the same day  in 8,794 
cases. 
Thus, subsequent instalments were released without verification of stages of construction of 
house and indicates lack of monitoring. 
Government stated (July 2022) that the amount was paid to the beneficiaries in three 
instalments during 2016-17 and 2017-18. Therefore, in some cases it is possible that the last 
instalment has been released without uploa ding the co mpletion level photograph of the 
house.  
The reply is not acceptable as release of subsequent instalments was made without ensuring 
the required level photographs of the house uploaded in the AwaasSoft portal. 
2.2.6.5(c)   Deficiencies in GI tagging 
As per  guidelines, G eographical Indication (GI) tagging of houses constructed under 
PMAY-G are to be done . The physical progress of stage wise construction on ground is 
verified and monitored through geo-referenced, date and time stamped photographs captured 
by inspectors or beneficiaries using mobile based AwaasApp and uploaded on AwaasSoft.  
We collected data of GI tagging  (geo locations of houses  constructed under PMAY -G) of 
6,24,053 beneficiaries of audited 10  districts and analysed the data in Power Bi and M S 
excel. We noticed that geo tagging of the same house on different level of construction which 
was supposed to be within permissible accuracy of 25 metres as per user manual of 
AwaasApp21 was varied. We noticed that GI tagged photographs of houses constructed under 
PMAY-G were clicked beyond the permissible limit of 25 meters as detailed in  
Table-2.2.19.

2.2.6.5(d)   Test checked geo-tagged photographs of houses   
Stage-wise geo-tagged photographs of 1,030 houses uploaded on AwaasSoft that were 
completed within 90 days were examined in selected district and irregularities observed are 
mentioned below: 
• Same image of a lready constructed house was uploaded in Plinth, Lintel and 
Completion level in 16 cases. In two cases, images were uploaded in lintel and  
completion level, in three cases uploaded at Plinth and Lintel level. 
• In 15 cases, photographs of lintel level were uploaded at plinth level. 
• Photographs of existing site and proposed site were uploaded in Plinth , Lintel and 
Completion level in one case. In one case existing site photograph uploaded in Plinth 
and lintel level, in one case Roof level photograph was uploaded in Plinth and Lintel 
level and in one case plinth level photograph uploaded in lintel level.  
• In two cases, completion level photographs were uploaded for incomplete houses. 
• In 105 cases, image uploaded at Lintel and Completion level was not clear enough 
to testify that it belonged to the same house. In 12 cases, image was not clear enough 
to know that house was completed or not. 
This show s that during release of subsequent instalments, verification of uploaded 
photographs was not done properly and reflects the lack of monitoring through AwaasSoft. 
Government accepted the facts and stated (July 2022) that audit suggestions would be taken 
care of in future. 
2.2.6.5(e)  Constitution of PMU 
As per Para 7.3 of PMAY-G Guidelines, a dedicated Programme Management Unit (PMU) 
at State and each ZP/JP level was to undertake the tas k of monitoring and supervision of 
implementation of the scheme. The PMU was to be headed by a full-time programme officer 
or a senior officer at the district level. The other members of District (D) PMU would be 
professionals in the field of construction, IT and Training Coordinator and support staff. The 
duties of DPMU included finalisation of priority list and annual select list, allotment of land 
to the landless beneficiaries, sensitisation of beneficiaries, organisation of training and credit 
linkage and monitoring of reporting on AwaasSoft etc. Similarly, BPMU at block level was 
to be headed by a full -time block level officer or coordinator assisted by technical support 
staff and MIS data entry operator.  BPMU would do registration of beneficiaries, iss ue of 
sanction orders, tagging of trained masons to beneficiaries, monitoring of progress of 
constructions and reporting through AwaasSoft etc. 

 
Chapter II: Performance Audit  
87 
 
Audit noticed that PMU at State level was constituted in June 2018 after lapse of more than 
two years since inception of the scheme. DPMU at District level was constituted in seven22 
districts out of 10 selected districts during 2018-19 whereas the implementation of scheme 
started from 2016-17. DPMU in Chhatarpur district was not constituted. No information was 
provided regarding constitution of DPMU in Dhar  and Satna . At JP level, BPMU was  
constituted in  eight 23 JPs (07 in 2018-19 and 01 in 2019-20) out of 20 selected JPs. BPMU 
was not constituted in five24 JPs. Seven 25 JPs did not provide any information in this regard. 
With the formation of dedicated PMU at all levels, irregularities as discussed in the report 
such as delayed release of instalment to beneficiaries, no/delayed inspection of house in 
progress etc. would have been minimised. 
The Government did not submit the reply to audit observation. 
 
2.2.7   Conclusion 
State completed 21.64 lakh houses (82.35 per cent) against 26.28 lakhs houses sanctioned 
(as per AwaasSoft) under PMAY-G during 2016-21, however, the timeliness prescribed for 
completion was adhered to in 86.28 per cent of the completed house. 
There was lack of v erification of beneficiarie s in exclusion process as b eneficiaries with 
2/3/4 wheeler vehicles were provided benefit. Further, priority setting and sanction as per 
priority as indicated in guidelines were also not adhered to by the State. 
MGNREGA Job Card of another person was registered on AwaasSoft portal instead of actual 
beneficiary. Aadhaar number of 97.70 per cent  beneficiaries were collected during 
registration but as per MIS only 28.33 per cent were seeded in AwaasSoft in selected Janpad 
Panchayats.  
Timely release of Centre and State share in State Nodal Account was not followed as per 
guidelines by the S tate. Timely release of 1st instalments to sanctioned bene ficiaries was 
done only in 33.47 per cent cases. 
1st inspection of sanctioned houses was done after stipulated time in 40.54 per cent cases 
and it reflects lack of monitoring at different levels. AwaasApp (mobile application) adopted 
for geo-tagging of photographs at different level of construction for monitoring of progress 
was also not accurate. This resulted in the lack of adequate control of AwaasApp in capturing 
geo-reference date and time stamped photographs  of same houses at different construction 
level within 25 metres. 
2.2.8  Recommendations 
(i) The State Government should take steps for providing funds in timely manner to 
SNA and should ensure timely release of assistance to beneficiaries.

(ii) Measures should be taken to ensure sanction of houses as per due priority of 
beneficiary in PWL. 
(iii) Responsibility should be fixed for wrong identification and inclusion of ineligible 
beneficiaries. Further, the State government should issue strict directions for  
verification of eligibility of beneficiaries by ground level officials before sanction of 
assistance. 
(iv) The State Government should fix responsibili ties of  the authorities for providing 
assistance to beneficiaries declared ineligible by Appellate Committee/Gram Sabha. 
Further, the amount of assistance should also be recovered from the ineligible  
beneficiaries. 
(v) The State Government should take action against the officials who were responsible 
for wrong selection of beneficiaries. 
(vi) The State Government in compliance to the instruction of GoI should correct wrong 
entry in the data and then allot hous e to eligible adult, if minor has been entered as 
head in SECC family details. Further, they should also take action against the 
officials who allotted houses to the minor. 
(vii) The State government should take effective steps for completion of the houses within 
prescribed time to avoid any possibility of utilization of fund for other purposes by 
the beneficiaries or migration of beneficiary etc. that can arise due to delay in 
completion of house. 
(viii) The State government may take concerted efforts to ensure that all the basic amenities 
in all the constructed houses under PMAY-G are provided through convergence with 
other Government schemes/programmes. 
(ix) Due care should be taken in GI tagging  and monitoring should be strengthened and 
the AwaasApp should be enhanced acc ordingly for effective e -governance for 
implementation of scheme.

Chapter-III: Compliance Audit 
 
 
Panchayat and Rural Development Department 
 
3.1  Audit of  Infrastructure works done at the choice of e lected 
representatives of Zila Panchayats and Janpad Panchayats 
 
Introduction 
The Fourth State Finance Commission 1 (SFC) recommended (October 2015) allotment of 
additional funds as SFC grant to each Zila  Panchayat (ZP) and Janpad Panchayat (JP) for 
development of infrastructure in the constituency of President, Vice President and other 
elected members of ZPs and JPs . Accordingly, funds amounting to ` two crore and ` one 
crore were to be annually released to each ZP and JP respectively for this purpose.  
As per the SFC recommendation, infrastructure works were to be taken up based  on the 
recommendations of the  elected representatives; however, priority was to be given as 
detailed in Table-3.1.  
Table-3.1: Focus area for infrastructure work 
Focus Area Items of work 
Total Sanitation and Health Construction of public road, drainage system and other work 
related to sanitation viz., community toilets, piped water line etc.

resources 
Construction of rural roads where sanction was not given under 
Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak  Yojana (PMGSY) and Mukhya 
Mantri Gram Sadak  Yojana (MMGSY), culverts and internal  
Cement Concrete (CC) roads. 
Construction of public buildings 
and places 
Construction of Aganwadi Bhavan, Prathamik Shala Bhavan  
including playground, etc. 
Gram Chaupal , passenger waiting room, Ghat of River/Pond, 
boundary wall of school and Construction of cemetery etc. 
(Source: Scheme Guideline issued by Department) 
Accordingly, the Panchayat and Rural Development Department (PRDD), Government of 
Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) fixed  (December 2016 and March 2019)  the annual financial 
ceiling for sanctioning of works by the elected representatives of ZPs and JPs, as shown in 
Table-3.2.

Sarpanch/Secretary of Gram Panchayat s (GPs) were responsible for executi on of 
infrastructure works  under the  monitoring and supervision of  Chief Executive Officers 
(CEOs) of ZPs/JPs.  At state level, Commissioner/Director of P anchayat Raj Directorate 
(PRD), Development Commissioner (Ex -Officio Additional Chief Secretary /Principal 
Secretary), PRDD were responsible for overall monitoring and supervision of the schemes. 
3.2   Audit Objective, Criteria, Scope and Methodology 
Audit aimed to assess whether the infrastructure works were planned, executed and 
monitored effectively as per the scheme guidelines, rules and orders issued by the GoMP. 
Audit criteria was sourced from: 
• Scheme implementation guidelines issued by the PRDD and GoMP;  
• Orders and Circulars issued by the PRDD;  
• Provisions of the Schedule of Rates (SOR) issued by Engineer-in chief (E-in-C), Rural 
Engineering Services (RES) and related specifications; 
• Madhya Pradesh Works Department (MPWD) Manual;  
• Madhya Pradesh Budget Manual (MPBM); and  
• Madhya Pradesh Panchayat Raj Evam Gram Swaraj Adhiniyam 1993. 
Audit covered execu tion of schemes carried ou t during 2 017-20 in seven  ZPs of seven  
districts2. In these seven ZPs, Audit selected, seven JPs3 (one in each ZP) out of 574 JPs. 
Further, out of the seven selected JPs, Audit selected 14 GPs5 (two in each JP) out of 5756 
GPs. The selection of ZPs, JPs and GPs was made on the basis of  expenditure incurred as 
well as the logistic issues such as non -availability of trains/road transportation during the 
covid-19 pandemic period i.e. between August and November 2020.   
Audit examined 1 63 works (11 per cent ) involving expenditure of  ` 9.75 crore , out of 
1,4667 works involving ` 39.58 crore , in the selected ZPs/JPs . Out of the 163 selected 
works, Audit also carried out joint physical verification of 37 works with the Departmental 
authorities to ascertain the ground realities.

3.3.1   Non-submission of UCs by PRIs 
Department directed (De cember 201 6) that release of SFC grant s to the ZPs/JPs was 
subject to utilization of 70 per cent of previous year ’s grant and 100 per cent  of other 
years’ grant.  
Audit noticed that only five9 ZPs/JPs out of 14 selected ZPs/JPs10 submitted total 12 UCs 
out of the required 42 UCs for the year 2017 -20. Panchayat Raj Directorate (PRD) neither 
asked for utilization certificates nor maintain records of funds drawn and utilized by the 
districts. Thus, the Department failed to monitor the utilization of grants by  the ZPs/JPs. 
Consequently, during the year 2018 -20, grants were released to the districts without 
ensuring their utilization. 
Audit further noticed that although the Commissioner ’s office  was required to ensure 
submission of UCs,  it did not maintain the r ecords of utilization of grant . Therefore, the 
Department did not even have the data to enforce the conditions of grant as stipulated by 
the SFC.  
The Government replied (July 2022)  that as per SFC guidelines, grant s were released as 
per budget allotment  without obtaining UCs from the PRIs . However, cent per  cent 
valuation and verification of works being executed from these grants were done. 
Reply is not acceptable  as the department did not comply  with the norms of SFC 
guidelines in releasing the grants to ZPs/JPs. 
3.4   Execution of the scheme 
3.4.1   Incorrect work selection procedure 
According to scheme guidelines (March 2016 and August 2016), every GP was required to 
prepare a Gram Panchayat Development Programme (GPDP) for decentralizing the 
planning pro cess and t o ensure effective result -oriented utilization of funds . The 
infrastructure works w ere to be selected  from the GPDP  by the elected ZP/JP 
representatives. However, if any particular work covered more than one GP, then selection 
of such work from GPDP was not necessary. 
Audit noticed that out of 163 test checked infrastructure works , only 31 works (19 per 
cent) involving expenditure of ` 1.08 crore was selected from GPDP. Thus, administrative 
sanctions for the remaining 132 works issued by the concerned ZPs/JPs were against the 
scheme guidelines as detailed in Appendix-3.2. 
Government replied (July 2022) that works were sanctioned as per approved annual action 
plan of concerned bodies. 
                                                           
9 ZP Gwalior (two UCs) , Jabalpur (tw o UCs), Mandla (three UCs), Tikamgarh (two UCs), and JP 
Majhauli (three UCs).

various infrastructure works under the scheme for which the GPs were responsible.

geographical conditions, the works were not completed within prescribed time. However, 
almost all the aforesaid incomplete works had been completed.   
The reply is not acceptable as the Government could not produce completion certificates of 
completed works in support of their contention.   
3.4.3   Execution of works without laboratory testing 
As per instructions issued (February 2018) by PRDD, it was the primary responsibility of 
the executing agenc y and Sub/Assistant Engineer (SE/AE) of Rural Engineering Services 
(RES) Department to execute the quality work and the material as well as works were to be 
tested up to the prescribed frequency to ensure the quality of work.  Further, the rates given 
in the Schedule of Rates (S OR) issued (February 2016) by the RES Department, GoMP, 
were inclusive of the cost of  laboratory testing12 to be conducted as per the  prescribed 
frequency.  
Directorate, PRIs issued (June 2017) order to constitute a monitoring cell to evaluation the 
online & offline activities conducted under various schemes. 
Audit observed that  laboratory tests for ascertaining quality was undertaken in only 20 
works (12 per cent ) out of 163 test checked works . In respect of remaining 143 work s 
(Appendix-3.5) amounting to ` 9.12 crore, quality tests were not conducted. Consequently, 
the manner in which the RES Department evaluated the quality of work without any tests 
could not be ascertained by Audit.  
Government accepted audit observation and  replied (July 2022) that the laboratory testing 
of all works was not possible due to non-availability of lab facilities at all JPs headquarters, 
excess of works and GPs established in remote areas. However, RES ensured the quality of 
works through valuation and verification. 
                                                           
12 Various tests of material as well as complete works like water absorption test, compressive s trength of 
bricks and compressive strength of concrete (one test per 20 cubic meter or part thereof) etc. were 
provided in the SOR.

95 
 
Reply is not acceptable as the quality of materials to be used in works was not ensured as 
per aforesaid PRDD order.  
3.4.4   Payment without recording detailed measurements 
MPWD Manual provides that the MB is the basis of all accounts of quant ities of work 
done and purchase made, and it m ust contain complete record of facts to be conclusive 
evidence in the court of law. SE/AE of RES is responsible for recording detail ed 
measurement of works executed under the scheme. 
Audit observed that in  79 works (involving expenditure of ` 4.94 crore) out of 163 test 
checked works, only lump sum amount was recorded in the MBs by SE/AE instead of 
recording detailed measurements (as detailed in Appendix-3.6). In the absence of detail ed 
measurements, it could not be verified whether the works  were executed as per sanctioned 
estimates and whether quantity of materials and items of building works etc., were as per 
the sanctions. Further , the Department did not  have any me ans to verify adherence to 
technical specifications such as thickness of CC road. 
The gove rnment replied (July 2022) that  the AE verified the measurement books of all 
works which were recorded by SE after evaluation of works.  
Reply is not acceptable as detailed measurements were not recorded as per MPWD Manual 
in measurement books of above mentioned 79 works which were made available to audit.  
3.4.5   Irregular land use conversion  
Rule 3 of Madhya Pradesh Panchayat (M anagement o f Government l ands) Rules, 1995 
provides that  Government land shall be  used for the specific purposes  for which it is 
transferred to the Panchayat.  
We noticed violation of above rules in the following cases: 
• The GP, Supawali, (under JP, Morar), Gwalior district, sent (August 2017) a proposal 
to the JP Morar for construction of boundary wall in a plot of land (Khasra No. 574/1) 
by changing the title of the plot from fodder land 13 to graveyard land. Although the 
GP was not empowered to change the title of the land, the JP sanctioned  (October 
2017)  the boundary wall at a cost of ` 8.00 lakh and the GP incurr ed an expenditure 
of ` 7.25 lakh on constructing the boundary wall on the fodder land.  
• In another case,  GP, Dangwara  (under JP Nateran ) of Vidisha district, sent (August 
2018) a proposal to the JP Nateran for construction of a boundary wall of Shantidham 
(cremation ground) in a plot of land (Khasra No. 179) at a cost of ` 12.00 lakh  on 
Aabadi14 land instead of land of Shantidham. Although the GP was not empowered to

3.4.6   Execution of impermissible work 
The department  issued ( February and March 2018 ) the list of permissible and 
impermissible works executable under the scheme.  The list of impermissible wor ks inter

of water tanker, Expenditure on transportation of drinking water, Construction of gravel 
roads and expenses on solar light etc.  
Audit observed instances where the JPs executed impermissible works as detailed below: 
• CEO JP, Majhauli, Jabalpur, sanctioned (January 2019)  impermissible work s of 
drilling of four15 hand pumps at a cost of ` 3.47 lakh. 
• Similarly, CEO JP, Baldeogarh, Tikamgarh sanctioned (February 2019) a welcome 
gate at a cost of ` 1.50 lakh. 
Thus, the  JPs executed  impermissible works involving ` 4.97 lakh  in violation of  the

The government accepted (July 2022) audit observation and stated that letters for fixing 
responsibilities of concerned were issued (June 2022) to respective ZPs.

was any misuse of funds, joint re sponsibility of Sarpanch/Secretary would be fixed, and 
legal action would be taken against them. 
We observed that in 95 works (58 per cent) involving expenditure of ` 5.58 crore, out of 
163 selected works , geo-tagged photographs of the works being executed were not 
uploaded on the portal at any of the construction stages (Appendix-3.7). Non-uploading of

3.4.8 Other irregularities 
3.4.8.1   Issue of Administrative Sanction by incompetent authority 
The Scheme guidelines (August 2016) inter alia stipulate that administrative sanction of 
the work was to be issued by the CEOs of respective ZPs/JPs.  
In ZP Tikamgarh, audit observed that in five17 works pertaining to construction of culverts 
involving expenditure of  ` 32.50 lakh, the concerned GPs issued administrative sanction s 
instead of the CEO, ZP there by violating the provisions of the scheme guidelines. 
CEO, ZP Tikamgarh produced a letter of PRDD (December 2016) and stated that sanctions 
were in order. 
Reply of CEO is not acceptable as the  ibid order envisages th at administrative sanction 
were to be issued by the concerned ZPs and not  by the GPs . The ibid order elaborates 
general financial delegation of power which cannot be applied in special schemes. GPs are 
only implementing agencies. 
The government accepted the facts and stated (July 2022) t hat a letter to investigate the 
matter was issued (June 2022) to the CEOs of respective ZPs. 
3.4.8.2   Issue of improper Technical Sanction 
Engineer-in-Chief of RES issued (March 2017) model estimates for execution of different 
works in PRDD and issued instruction about the thickness of CC roads should be 200 mm. 
Audit o bserved that  in 24 works18 costing ` 1.58 crore , the Technical Sanction for 
construction of CC roads were accorded without mentioning the thickness of roads.  In the 
absence of clear instructions to the executing agencies to provide specific thickness for CC 
roads, the executing agencies were free to construct the roads as per their convenience and 
not as per the set standards.  
Government replied (July 2022) that districts had complied with the instructions regarding 
thickness of roads in construction.  
Reply is not acceptable as AE, RES issued technical sanction without mentioning thickness 
of the road as per model estimate.  
3.5   Monitoring and supervision 
Monitoring of scheme implementation was the respon sibility of the Deputy Director of 
Directorate, PRI s and CEOs of ZP/JP. The D epartment issued (August 2016 and June 
2017) guidelines that all works should be effectively monitored, and periodical appraisal of 
the quality control data should  be made. For this purpose, a monitoring cell headed by a 
Deputy Director was constituted in June 2017 at the directorate level.

Audit observed the following shortcomings with respect to monitoring of the scheme

3.5.1   Lack of proper monitoring of the scheme 
Guidelines of the scheme stipulate  that Sarpanch/Secretary of GPs were responsible for 
data entry of each and every work at Panchayat Darpan Portal as soon as it was sanctioned 
so that progress of the works could be watched/ reviewed by the superior authorities. 
In all selected ZPs/JPs, Audit observed that the physical and online portal details of all the 
1,466 works executed between 2017-18 and 2019 -20 did not match , as shown in  
Table-3.8. 
Table-3.8: Details of difference in online and off-line data 
Year No. of works as per Online 
portal

executed

The mismatch of figures indicates that the data  entry for online portal and the quality and 
authenticity of the data being entered was not being  monitored. Consequently, the data

data, the very purpose of online monitoring of infrastructure works was defeated. 
Government replied (July 2022) that the every work taken by GPs should be compulsorily

validation. Further the  department failed in proper monitoring  in the absence of reliable 
data on the portal.   
3.6   Conclusion  
The Department did not maintain the records of utilization of grant by ZPs/JPs and grants 
were being released in a routine manner. Further, ZPs/JPs neither submitted the UCs to the 
Department nor maintain ed scheme wise ledge r/accounting records. Improper planning  
made, failure to observe Government directives and ab sence of monitoring resulted in only 
14 (8.59 per cent) out of selected 163 works being completed on time. 
Further, due to weak internal control s and lack of quality monitoring system, t here were 
instances of incorrect selection of works, lack of quality control, non-recording of detailed 
measurements in MBs, and payments made for un-executed/partially executed works. 

 
Chapter III: Compliance Audit 
101 
 
3.7   Recommendations 
The PRDD and the implementing agencies (ZP/JP/GP) may take steps to ensure that: 
(i) SFC grants should be released only after ensuring utilisation of the grants released 
previously.  
(ii) Scheme wise ledger and other ac counting records  to account for all income and 
expenditure should be maintained at ZP and JP level. 
(iii) Completion period of each works should be fixed and it should be mentioned in 
administrative sanction of work. 
(iv) Quality test of materials should mandatorily be conducted for each work.  
(v) Detailed measurements should be recorded in the MBs of the work.  
(vi) Department should ensure uploading of  Geo-tagged photographs of all works in 
each stage of construction.  
(vii) Department should strengthen the monitoring and evaluation system at all levels of 
PRIs.  

            
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
  
   
 
 
 
 
PART – B 
 Chapter – IV 
 
An Overview of the Functioning 
of the Urban Local Bodies 
 
 

 

 
 
Chapter – IV 
 
 
AN OVERVIEW OF THE FUNCTIONING OF URBAN LOCAL 
BODIES 
 
 
4.1 Introduction 
The 74th Constitutional Amendment gave constitutional status to Urban Local  Bodies and 
established a system of uniform structure, regular elections and regular flow of funds 
through Finance Commission, etc. As a follow up,  the States are required to entrust these 
bodies with powers, functions and  responsibilities so as to enable them to function as 
institutions of Self-Government. 
Article 243Q of the Constitution envisages that there shall be constituted in  every State, 
Municipal Corporations for large urban areas; Municipal Councils  for smaller urban areas; 
and Nagar Panchayats (by whatever name called)  for areas in transition from a rural to an 
urban area. Further, Article 243W states that the Legislature of a State may, by law, endow 
the Municipalities with  such powers and authority as may be necessary to enable them to 
function as  institutions of Self-Government and such law may contain provisions for 
devolution of powers and responsibilities upon Municipalities. 
There are 16 Municipal Corporations, 9 9 Municipal Councils and 2 92 Nagar Parishads in 
the State as of March 2021. 
4.2 Organisational set up of Urban Local Bodies 
All the Urban Local Bodies are empowered to discharge the functions devolved under the  
provisions of Madhya Pradesh Municipal Corporation Act, 1956 and Madhya  Pradesh 
Municipalities Act, 1961, subject to monitoring powers vested in State Authorities provided 
therein. At the Government level, Urban Development and Housing Department (UDHD) is 
the administrative Department for Urban Local  Bodies. The organisational set up of 
governance of Urban Local Bodies is as under: 
Chart 4.1: Organisational set-up of Urban Local Bodies in Madhya Pradesh 
Principal Secretary, Urban Development and Housing Department 
 
 
Commissioner, Directorate of Urban Administration and Development  
 
  
Joint Directors (91) 
 
                                                      
1  Bhopal, Gwalior, Indore, Jabalpur, Narmadapuram, Rewa, Sagar, Shahdol and Ujjain  
Municipal Corporation (16)  
(Nagar Nigam)

(Nagar Palika Parishad)

Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
104 
 
4.3 Functioning of Urban Local Bodies 
Article 243W envisaged that the Legislature of a State may, by law, endow the 
Municipalities with such powers and authorities as may be necessary to enable them to 
function as institutions of Self Government and such law may contain provisions for 
devolution of powers and responsibilities upon Municipalities, including 18 functions listed 
in the Twelfth Schedule. The State Government devolved all 18 functions, enshrined in 
Twelfth Schedule of the Constitution  of India  to Urban Local  Bodies, as detailed in 
Appendix-4.1. 
Article 243X of the Constitution of India envisages that State Legislature may, by law, vest 
power in Urban Local  Bodies to impose various taxes for revenue collection. This 
constitutional provision has been incorporated in Clause 132 of Madhya Pradesh Municipal 
Corporation Act, 1956 and Clause 127 of Madhya Pradesh Municipalities Act, 1961. At the 
Urban Local Bodies level various types of taxes have been imposed including Property Tax, 
Composite Tax, Water Tax, Market Fee, etc. 
There are three types of Urban Local Bodies i.e., Municipal Corporation, Municipal Council 
and Nagar Parishad which have been described below: 
• Municipal Corporation 
According to Section 9 of Madhya Pradesh Municipal Corporation Act, 1956, a Municipal 
Corporation shall consist of  a Mayor and Councilors directly elected from the Municipal 
Corporation area. Section 37 of the Madhya Pradesh Municipal Corporation Act, 1956  
provides that there shall be a Mayor-in-Council for every Municipal Corporation constituted 
by the Mayor from amongst the elected Councilors to discharge the functions and conduct 
of business of the Mayor-in-Council, as may be prescribed. 
According to Section 25 of Madhya Pradesh Municipal Corporation Act, 1956, the Mayor 
shall have administrative control over the officers and staff of his office and exercise such 
powers and perform such functions as described in the Act and Rule made thereunder. 
The Commissioner of a Corporation shall be appointed by the State Government who is the 
Principal Executive Officer of the Corporation and shall have the right to speak at, and 
otherwise take part in any meeting of the Corporation or any committee thereof but shall not 
be entitled to vote or to move any proposition. The office of the Commissioner sh all have 
the administrative staff for the functions and duties as prescribed under the rules. The 
Commissioner shall perform all the duties imposed or conferred upon him by this Act and 
exercise supervision and control over the acts and proceedings of all municipal officers. 
• Municipal Council and Nagar Parishad 
As per Section 19 of Madhya Pradesh Municipalities Act, 1961, a Municipal Council, for a 
smaller urban area and Nagar Parishad, for a transitional urban area, shall have a President 
and Councilors elected from the respective areas by direct election. Under Section 70 of the 
Act, there shall be a President -in- Council (PIC) for every Municipal Council/Nagar 
Parishad, which shall be constituted by the President from amongst the elected councilors to 
discharge the functions and conduct of business of the PIC, as may be prescribed. The 

 
Chapter IV: An overview of functioning of the Urban Local Bodies 
105 
 
President of the Municipal Council/Nagar Parishad, who is the President of PIC, shall 
preside over all meetings, watch over the financial and executive administration and perform 
such executive functions as may be allotted under the Act. 
As per Section 87 of the Act, the Chief Municipal Officer of a Council shall be appointed by 
the State Government, who shall be the Principal Executive Officer of the Municipal 
Council/Nagar Parishad and shall have such powers and perform such functions as described 
in the Act and Rules made thereunder. 
4.3.1  Shortage of manpower vis-à-vis sanctioned strength 
Audit found that there was acute shortage of staff in all three types of Urban Local Bodies 
which was impacting adversely to performance of Urban Local Bodies. 
Audit noticed in 32 test-checked Urban Local Bodies that there were substantial vacancies 
in different cadres of manpower as of March 2021 against the total sanctioned strength as 
shown in Table-4.1 below:
