From the above Table, it could be seen that there were huge vacancies ranging from 44.73 
to 54.55 per cent in Urban Local Bodies which may clearly impact the working of Urban 
Local Bodies. Further, in 17 Urban Local Bodies, it was also noticed that excess staff in 
comparison to sanctioned staff was working in various 27 posts as detailed in Appendix 4.2. 
Audit also noted that there was shortage of staff in these posts in other Urban Local Bodies, 
such as Nagar Nigam Dewas 2, Nagar Nigam Bhopal 3, Nagar Nigam Chhindwada4. This 
irrational distribution  of person -in-position indicated that Human Resource Management 
was not adequate in the Department.  
4.3.2  Performance on Service Level Benchmarks 
Benchmarking is an important mechanism for introducing accountability in service delivery. 
Recognising its importance, Ministry of Urban Development, Government of India  
prescribed Service Level Benchmarks since 2008 for performance management of basic 
services. Ministry of Urban Development had also issued Service Level Benchmarks 
Handbook prescribing the standardised framework for performance monitoring in respect of 
four basic municipal services, viz., Water Supply, Sewerage, Solid Waste Management and 
Storm Water Drainage. Service Level Benchmark indicators include coverage of water 
                                                      
2  Shortage in Assistant Grade-3. 
3  Shortage in Assistant Revenue Inspector and Safai Sanrakshak. 
4  Shortage in Driver and Chaukidar. 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
106 
 
supply connections, quality of water, cost recovery in water supply, coverage of toilets, 
coverage of sewage network service, household coverage of solid waste management, 
segregation and disposal of municipal solid wastes and coverage of storm water drainage 
network. 
In compliance to the recommendations of Fifteenth Finance Commission, the Government 
of Madhya Pradesh, Urban Development and Housing Department had fixed (Decem ber 
2020) benchmarks for improving service delivery system.  
Urban Local Bodies are to generate performance reports on Service Level Benchmarks 
periodically and institutionalise systems for performance management.  
Out of the 26 parameters under three head s (Water Supply, Sewerage and Solid Waste 
Management), we analysed 13 parameters in the 401 Urban Local Bodies. Results are 
discussed below: 
• Water Supply  
Three hundred twelve (77.81 per cent) out of 401 Urban Local Bodies were yet to meet the 
target of ac hieving 100 per cent water connections. Further, 193 (48.13 per cent) Urban 
Local Bodies were still not able to provide targeted per capita daily water supply and 129 
(32.17 per cent) Urban Local Bodies were not able to install the target stipulated for wa ter 
meter against the connections. There are 185 (46.13 per cent) Urban Local Bodies which 
failed to meet the target set for public grievance redressal.  
• Sewerage  
Out of 401 Urban Local Bodies, 151 (37.66 per cent ) failed to meet the target set for 
achieving Coverage of toilets. There are 43 (10.72 per cent) and 35 (8.73 per cent) Urban 
Local Bodies which failed to meet the target set for coverage of sewerage and adequacy of 
sewage treatment respectively. Further, 124 (30.92 per cent) Urban Local Bodies failed to 
meet the target set for public grievance redressal.  
• Solid Waste  
Out of 401 Urban Local Bodies, 202 (50.37 per cent ) failed to meet the target set for 
achieving collection efficiency. There are 248 (61.85 per cent) and 207 ( 51.62 per cent) 
Urban Local Bodies which had failed to meet the target s set for segregation of solid waste 
and obtaining reused/recycled material from solid waste respectively. Further, 171 (42.64 
per cent) and 161 (40.15 per cent ) Urban Local Bodies faile d to meet the target set for 
scientific disposal of solid waste and public grievance redressal respectively. 
4.4 Audit Arrangement 
The State Government had appointed (November 2001) Director, Local Fund Audit (DLFA) 
for audit of accounts of Urban Local Bodies, who shall work under the Technical Guidance 
and Support (TGS) of the Comptroller and Auditor General (C&AG) of India. As per 
standard terms and conditions of TGS, C&AG of  India has the right to conduct such test 
check of the accounts and to comment on and supplement the report of the statutory Auditor, 
as he may deem fit. Further, the C&AG of India or his representative has the right to report 

 
Chapter IV: An overview of functioning of the Urban Local Bodies 
107 
 
to State Legislative, the report of audit at his discretion. 
Madhya Pradesh State Legislative Assembly has constituted (March 2015) Local Bodies and 
Panchayatiraj Accounts Committee (LBPAC) for examination of Appropriation Accounts of 
local bodies in the State. LBPAC is also responsible for examination of reports of C&AG 
laid on the table of the Legislative Assembly. 
• Technical Guidance and Support Provided by India n Audit and Accounts 
Department 
Section 120 of Regulation on Audit and Account, 2020 envisages the following 
arrangements regarding technical guidance and support to Urban Local Bodies:  
➢ Director, Local Fund Audit (DLFA) would prepare an annual audit plan for audit of 
Urban Local Bodies and forward it to the Accountant General (Audit) of the state.  
➢ The audit methodology and procedures for audit of Urban Local Bodies by the Local 
Fund Auditor would be as per various Acts and statutes enacted by the state an d 
guidelines prescribed by the C&AG of India.  
➢ Copies of Inspection Reports in respect of selected local bodies shall be forwarded 
by the Local Fund Auditor to the Accountant General (Audit) for advice on system 
improvements.  
➢ The Accountant General (Audit) shall also undertake training and capacity building 
of the Local Fund Audit Staff. 
The annual Audit Plan for 2021 -22 was prepared by DLFA, which was forwarded to the 
Accountant General (Audit). A total of 372 units were shown in the audit plan of DLFA for 
the year 2021 -22, out of which, 49 units (16 MCs, 33 NPPs) were being audited by the 
Resident Audit, while 323 units were under audit purview of post-Audit. The methodology 
and procedure as suggested from time to time by the Accountant General (Audit) we re 
followed by the DLFA. Particulars of the units of Urban Local Bodies planned for audit by 
DLFA in its audit plan for last three years along with actually audited units are as under:  
Table-4.2: Year-wise position of audit of units

DLFA. The frequently seen objections in their IRs are given below:

As per the facts given by DLFA, it emerged that there was a severe shortage of staff (50.80 
per cent). Against 1,012 sanctioned posts, only 498 personnel (49.21 per cent) were working. 
This had a direct impact on the Compliance Audit during  2021-22 (refer Table-4.2) by 
DLFA. Further, Financial Audit was not being done by the DLFA.

funds have been commented upon in the following paragraphs:

4.7  Fourteenth and Fifteenth Finance Commission Grants 
As per 14th (Period 2015 -20) and 15 th (Period 2020 -26) Finance Commission 
recommendations, allocations among various ULBs within the State were to be made by the 
respective States. As per 14th Finance Commission States were required to release the grants 
to the ULBs within fifteen days of it b eing credited to their accounts by the Union 
Government. In case of delay, the State Government must release the instalment along with 
interest at the bank rate of Reserve Bank of India paid from its own funds. Further, as per 
15th Finance Commission States were required to release the grants to the ULBs within ten 
working days of it being credited to their accounts by the Union Government. In case of 
delay, the State Government must release the instalment along with interest for the period of 
delay as per the effective rate of interest on market borrowing/ State Development Loans for 
the previous year. 
Audit scrutiny of information provided by the UADD  for the period 2016 -21 revealed that 
State Government received ` 3,226.33 crore and ` 1,768.50 crore as 14th and 15th Finance 
Commission grant respectively from Government of India (GoI) till March 2021. However, 
the State Government delayed the release of grants to ULBs and the interest of ` 13.77 crore 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
110 
 
was not released to ULBs along with instalments as recom mended by 14th and 15th Finance 
Commission, details are given in Appendix-4.3. 
Further, it was observed that there was difference of ` 1,000.43 crore between the data 
provided by UADD and Finance Department Government of Madhya Pradesh, in respect of 
Finance Commission grants during 2016 -21. Total release during the period 2016 -21 was  
` 4,994.83 crore as per UADD whereas it was ` 5,995.26 as per Finance Department 
Government of Madhya Pradesh. The difference in amount of released grants was in the years 
2019-20 (` 850.93 crore) and 2020-21 (` 149.50 crore). 
Non release of Performance Grant under 14th Finance Commission 
The 14th Finance Commission has recommended Performance Grant for the period 2015-20. 
The performance grants were to address the following issues: (i) making available reliable 
data on local bodies receipt and expenditure through audited accounts; and (ii) improvement 
in own revenues. In addition, the Urban Local Bodies would have to measure and publish 
service level benchmarks for basic services. State Governments must ensure compliance 
with the recommendations for availing Performance Grants for the Urban Local Bodies.  
The 14 th Finance Commission recommended Performance Grant of ` 1,035.32 crore to 
Madhya Pradesh for the period 2016-20. However, only ` 432.77 crore was released by the 
GoI for the period  only in 2016-17 and 2019 -20. No Performance Grant was releas ed by 
Government of India in the year 2017 -18 and 2018 -19. Performance Grant of   
` 229.75 crore and ` 260.91 crore though recommended by the 14th Finance Commission for 
the respective years but was not released. Thus, there was short release of ` 602.55 crore as 
Performance Grant to Urban Local Bodies of the State due to lack of compliance to 
performance related recommendations by the 14th Finance Commission.  
4.8 Devolution of funds to Urban Local Bodies 
As per provisions of Section 105 of MP Municipalities Act, 1961 and Section 87 of MP 
Municipal Corporation Act, 1956, there are mainly two sources of revenue for Urban Local 
Bodies, viz. Government grants and own revenue. The Government grants i nclude 
devolution of one per cent of divisible tax revenue of the State Government as per 
recommendations of the Third State Finance Commission. 
The Third State Finance Commission (SFC) recommended (accepted by the State 
Government in February 2010) that one per cent  of divisible tax revenue of the State 
Government should be devolved to Urban Local Bodies. During the year 2016 -21, the 
devolution of SFC grants by the Finance Department to Urban Local Bodies is shown in 
Table-4.5 below:

It can be seen from Table-4.5 that there was a short devolution of ` 226.50 crore to Urban

that whenever there is a deviation, there is a constant effort to pay it back in the subsequent 
years ahead. It was further stated  (November 2022) that short release during 2020 -21 was 
due to scarcity of funds during Covid -19 pandemic. However, the data showed that only 
during 2017-18, the fund was devolved in excess to the amount which needed to devolve in 
the year.  
4.9 Non-realisation of Tax Revenue/non-Tax Revenue 
As per Section 87 of Madhya Pradesh Municipal Corporation Act, 1956 and Section 105 of 
Madhya Pradesh Municipalities Act, 1961, the source of own revenue of Urban Local  
Bodies are through taxes, rent, fees, issue of licenses, etc. In case of non -receipt of the tax 
and non-tax revenue, the Municipal Corporations are required to take necessary  action for 
recovery as envisaged in section 173 to 183 of the Madhya Pradesh Municipal Corporation 
Act, 1956.  
Audit observed that tax revenue (property tax, composite tax, education and development  
cess, etc.) of ` 1,754.10 crore was recoverable up to March 2021 in 31 out of test checked 
32 Urban Local Bodies. This includes ` 638.20 crore as arrears of previous years. However, 
during 2020-21, an amount of ` 550.59 crore (31.39 per cent) could be recovered against 
the total demand, i.e., ` 1,754.10 crore. Similarly, against the total demand of ` 306.66 crore 
under non-tax revenue (water charges, license fees, land and building rent,  etc.) an amount 
of ` 174.71 crore (56.97 per cent) could be recovered by the 31 Urban Local  Bodies. The 
total outstanding amount of ` 306.66 crore of non -tax revenue includes ` 215.59 crore as 
arrears of previous years. The total outstanding tax and non -tax revenue as of March 2021 
was ` 1,335.46 crore. The Bhopal Municipal Corporation did not furnish information to 
Audit. This indicated that the action taken by these Urban Local Bodies under Sections 173 
to 183 of the Act ibid to recover the dues was not adequate which had possible bearing on 
programme implementation by the Urban Local Bodies through own funds. 
Further, an audit on ‘Receipts (Property Tax and Rent) in Urban Local Bodies’ was also 
conducted and included in Chapter V of this Report.

Chapter- V 
 
Compliance Audit 
Audit of transactions of the Urban Local Bodies brought out instances of lapses in the 
adherence of the norms of regularity, propriety and economy. This Chapter comprises 
findings of three Compliance Audit s viz., (1) Compliance Audit of Smart City Companies 
of Madhya Pradesh ; (2) Receipts (Property Tax and Rent) in Urban Local Bodies and (3) 
Water Supply in Urban Local Bodies as below: 
5.1   Compliance Audit of Smart City Companies of Madhya Pradesh 
 
5.1.1   Introduction 
Cities are engines of growth for the economy of every nation. Nearly 31 per cent of India’s 
population lives in urban areas and contributes 63 per cent of India’s GDP1. With increasing 
urbanization, urban areas are expected to house 40 per cent  of India’s population and 
contribute 75 per cent of India’s GDP by 2030. This requires comprehensive development 
of physical, institutional, social and economic infrastructure. All  these are important in 
improving the quality of life and attracting people and investments to a City, setting in 
motion a cycle of growth and development. The Government of India accordingly introduced 
the “Smart Cities Mission” on 25 th June 2015 for  the Development of Smart Cities. The 
objective was to promote sustainable cities that provide core infrastructure and decent 
quality of life to their citizens including a clean and sustainable environment and application 
of ‘Smart’ Solutions. Though the conceptualisation of Smart C ity varies from city to city , 
depending on the level of development, willingness to change and reform, resources and 
aspirations of the city re sidents; the core infrastructure elements in a Smart City would 
include: 
• adequate water supply, 
• assured electricity supply, 
• sanitation, including solid waste management. 
Smart Cities can be developed either on the lines of the Area Based Development (AB D) 
model or through Pan -city Development (PCD) model.  The strategic components of AB D 
in the Smart Cities Mission are city improvement (retrofitting 2), city renewal 
(redevelopment3) and city extension (greenfield development 4). In case of PCD, some 
components of smart solutions are expected to be provided to all citizens across the city.  
                                                 
1  As per Census 2011. 
2  Retrofitting - In retrofitting, an area consisting of more than 500 acres will be identified by the city, in 
consultation with citizens to achieve Smart City objectives to make the existing area more efficient and 
livable. 
3  Redevelopment- Redevelopment envisages an area o f more than 50 acres, identified by Urban Local 
Bodies, in consultation with citizens by replacing the existing built -up environment and enable  
co-creation of a new layout. 
4  Greenfield Development- Greenfield Development is in the vacant area more than 250 acres. 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
114 
 
In Madhya Pradesh, seven 5 cities were selected to be developed as Smart Cities under the 
“Smart Cities Mission” (SCM).  
5.1.2   Organisational set-up 
The implementation of the SCM at the City level was to be done by a Smart City Company 
(SCC) created for the purpose. The SCC will plan, appraise, approve, implement, manage, 
operate, monitor and evaluate the Smar t City development projects. In Madhya Pradesh , 
seven Smart City Companies  were formed (one for each selected city) by the equity 
participation of Madhya Pradesh Urban Development Company Limited a nd the Urban 
Local Body of the concerned city.  
The Collector of the District is the ex-officio Chairman of the SCC and the Commissioner 
of the Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) is the ex-officio Executive Director of the SCC for that 
city. At the implementation level, the Chief Executive Officer (CEO) is the head of the SCC 
and is responsible for day-to-day affairs of the company . The CEO  is assisted by Chief 
Financial Officer,  Company Secret ary, Superintendent Engineer, Chief Planner, 
Administrative Officer and Manager, e-Governance. The organization chart is shown below: 
 
Map-5.1.1: Showing the Seven Smart Cities of MP out of which four selected for audit.

5.1.3   Audit objectives and methodology 
Audit was conducted from June 2021 to September 2021 with a view to  ascertaining 
whether: 
• the projects were/are being implemented in accordance with Smart City Proposals 
under Smart City Mission Guidelines, and 
• the projects are achieving the intended objectives.  
Audit covered the period from April 2016 to March 2021 . The Audit Findings were 
benchmarked against the following criteria: 
• Smart City Mission Guidelines, 
• Smart City Proposals, 
• Madhya Pradesh Store Purchase Rules, 2015, 
• Madhya Pradesh Works Department Manual and 
• Advisories issued by Ministry. 
The Audit Methodology involved scrutiny of records of selected units, res ponses to Audit 
Queries, Joint Physical Inspection with Smart City staff, etc. Four Smart Cities out of seven, 
i.e., Bhopal, Indore, Ujjain and Gwalior were selected using the Stratified Random Sampling 
method6. Audit examined 100 per cent of all ongoing/completed contracts having contract 
value of more than 25 crore (38 contracts). Further, 25 per cent of contracts having contract 
value of less than ` 25 crore in each city were also selected (63 contracts).  
The Entry Conference was held on 20 July 2021, GoMP replies had been received (July 
2022) and added at respective places in the Report. 
The cooperation of the Companies in providing necessary information and records to Audit 
is acknowledged. 
Audit findings 
Deviations noticed from the Smart City Mission Guidelines are discussed in subsequent 
paragraphs. 
5.1.4   Planning  
 
5.1.4.1   Revision of Plan without citizen engagement 
According to para 6.3 of the SCM Guidelines, the Cities are to prepare Smart City Plans 
(SCPs) containing both ABD and PCD proposals. The SCP is collaborative and should 
dovetail the objectives and funds of all Government Departments, private agencies and 
citizens. Further, as per advisory7 issued by the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs 
(March 2016), SCCs should consult citizens at every stage of implementation of SCP. 
                                                 
6  SCCs were grouped in three strata based on the expenditure; 100 per cent units were selected from 
strata1 (expenditure > ` 500 crore), 50 per cent from strata 2 (expenditure > ` 100 crore and < ` 500 
crore) and 33 per cent from strata 3 (expenditure < ` 100 crore). 
7  Advisory no. 1, 9 March 2016. 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
116 
 
Audit noticed that the SCCs in all the four selected Smart Cities had initially engaged citizens 
in the planning process. However, SCC Bhopal changed the entire location of the projects 
under SCM (which were included in the approved SCPs) and other  SCCs had changed the 
scope of activities. However, in none of these cases, citizens were consulted before effecting 
the changes as mentioned below: 
• In Bhopal, the  location of ABD was initially proposed after required citizen 
engagement and on the justification that the site was close to the Habibganj (now 
Rani Kamlapati) Station, next to BRT corridor and existing business zones. But the 
initial approved location of Shivaji Nagar was changed later by GoMP on the request 
made by Bhopal SCC to a new location in North and South Tatya Tope Nagar. The 
initial Smart City Proposa l with the area  of Shivaji Nagar contained seven main 
activities8 but with the changed location, it contained 13 activities 9. However, after 
the change in site, there was resistance from locals which has delayed the project. 
• In Gwalior, as per the initial plan , the location of ‘Maharaj Bada ’ was approved 
consisting of 71 activities. Later, the plan was revised with  changes in some of the 
works within the location but without citizen engagement. 
• In Indore, initially the selected area was 164.45 acres comprising of 16 components 
of the project . However, the approved area of five of the  components were either 
reduced or increased resulting in reduction the total area of the project to 123.34 acres 
without citizen engagement. 
• In Ujjain, the initial Smart City proposal for the Mahakal area, Mill area and Railway 
Station area was approved comprising of 30 activities. The plan was later revised but 
included 39 activities, and the area was reduced. All the changes were made without 
citizen engagement.    
As discussed above, the locations as well as major components/ activities of various projects 
were revised which shows that the Smart City Proposals were prepared without adequate 
survey and feasibility. The objective of the SCM was that the proposal w ould be citizen-
driven from the beginning by ensuring citizen consultation. However, the Citizens were not 
involved during the process of change in plans. Further, in Bhopal and Indore , the SCC 
carried out various works without  Information and Communication Technology  (ICT) 
elements as discussed in Paragraph 5.1.5.3 below. 
GoMP stated (August, 2022) that in the case of Bhopal, Government changed the plan due 
to various concerns raised by the stakeholders and agencies ; in case of Indore , the revised 
plan was just a reconstitution of plan looking on ground reality and the feasibility during the 
implementation of SCM  and according to the opinion of  the representatives of the local 
citizens, while in the case of Ujjain, the land was not available for the earlier planned project, 
hence, the area was reduced in revised plan. The reply is silent about citizen engagement by 
the Gwalior SCC. 
                                                 
8  Electricity provisions and energy efficiency, Sewage Waste Management, Information and 
Communication Technology, Water Supply, Mobility, Area Improvement, Housing. 
9   Tunnel, Power, Transportation, Water Supply, Water Treatment Plant, Drainage, Sewer, Sewage 
Treatment Plant, Recycled Water, Solid Waste Management, Information and Communication 
Technology, Fire Fighting, Land Scaping and Road Furniture. 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
117 
 
The replies are not acceptable as change in the plan without citizen engagement is against 
the vision and spirit of the mission.

2016-17 to 2020-21), the four SCCs could only spend ` 2,326.45 crore (94 per cent) 
of the funds resulting in savings of ` 150.25 crore. The non -utilisation was highest 
in the case of Gwalior at 61 per cent. 
The shortfall in release of funds was not only against the provisions of the SCM guidelines, 
but it also affected the implementation of the SCPs (prepared by the Cities) under the Smart 
Cities Mission. Further, the fact that Gwalior and Ujjain SCCs, could not fully utilise even 
the allocated funds indicates poor planning as well as implementation. 
5.1.5.2   Excess expenditure on Administrative/Office Expenses 
According to para 11.4 (ii) of Smart City Mission Guideline s, five per cent funds will be 
used towards meeting Administrative and Office Expenses (AOE) of the SCCs towards 
preparation of SCPs, Project Management Consultancies, Pilot Studies connected to ABD 
and deployment and generation of Smart Solutions  and capacity building . Further, the 
Directorate Urban Administration and Development, Government of Madhya Pradesh  had 
issued orders (May 2018) that one per cent (out of the five per cent provisioned for AOE) 
shall be retained at State level in  the accounts of Madhya Pradesh Urban Development 
Company Limited which shall be utilized for payment of State level consultancies, capacity 
building, training, etc.  
Audit observed that till March 2021, the AOE of three out of the four selected Smart Cities 
was more than four per cent11, which were over and above the limits as described in mission 
guidelines and the orders issued by the State Government. Details are shown in Table 5.1.2 
below:

5.1.5.3    Irregular expenditure on execution of projects in violation of 
Mission Guidelines 
As per Para 5.1.4 of the Smart City Mission Guidelines read with the Advisory12 issued by 
the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs for Smart Cities, Pan-city development envisages 
application of selected smart solutions to the existing city -wide infrastructure. Application 
of smart solutions will involve the use of technology, information and data to make 
infrastructure and services better and using smart technologies for the development of the 
poor and marginalized will be an important part of the Proposal.  Addition of non -ICT 
projects as a Pan -city initiative are not permissible. In the case of ABD model, a pre -
determined area is to be identified which c an be developed as a smart city  either through 
retrofitting (additions) or redevelopment (replacement) or as greenfield (new area). In case 
of ABD, non-IT components can be taken up. 
Audit scrutinised 53 projects under Pan-city development model and observed the following 
issues:  
• Construction/Creation of Pan-city assets without ICT elements  
The SCC, Bhopal /Indore incurred a total expenditure of ` 91.33 crore towards creating 
‘smart’ assets under Pan-city model. However, the smart assets created did not have ICT 
components as detailed below:

➢ Expending the amount under the Pan-city development even though the same was to 
be taken up under ABD.

allowed as per advisory of Ministry (No 1, 9 March 2016). 
SCC Bhopal had stated that the same was done after the approval of Board of Director. 
GoMP had not submitted any reply in this regard. However, purchase of flats outside ABD 
project area is not in consonance with the Guidelines ibid. 
5.1.5.4  Selection of projects already being undertaken by ULBs and related 
transfer of funds 
As per para 10.1 of Mission Guidelines, the implementation of the Mission at the City level 
will be done by a Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) created for the purpose. The SPV will plan, 
appraise, approve, release funds, implement, manage, operate, monitor and evaluate the 
Smart City development projects. The execution of projects may be done through joint 
ventures, subsidiaries, public-private partnership (PPP), turnkey contracts, etc. 
Further, as per para 10.3 of Mission Guidelines, funds provided by the Government of India 
in the Smart Cities Mission to the Smart City Company will be in the form of tied grant and 
these funds are to be utilized only for the purposes for which the grants have been given. 
In the initial years, the Smart City Companies received sufficient grants on time. However, 
Audit observed  that the Companies selected such projects which were not even in their 
approved plans and were, in fact, regular works to be carried out by ULBs and had nothing 
to do with the Smart City Mission.  In three Smart Cities, an amount of ` 170.78 crore was 
either transferred to the respective ULBs or payment was made for the works which had 
been undertaken by the concerned ULBs. Such cases are discussed in the Table-5.1.5 below:

transferring money to ULBs for their existing works but should instead engage themselves 
fully in creation of fresh assets. This also deprived other projects of the funds to the tune of

Tower to shortage of funds as mentioned in Paragraph 5.1.5.5 below.

to expending funds for acquiring the already created assets of Urban Local Bodies. This also 
hints at the possible insufficient capacity of Smart Cities in planning for timely utilization 
of funds held with them.

due approval of State Government and Ujjain SCC  and it was decided that the rem aining 
work may be completed by UMC.  
The reply is not acceptable as expenditure on the activities not allowed under Smart City 
Mission  was not just against the ‘vision and spirit’ but also against the prescribed Guidelines 
of SCM. The creation of SPV is to carry out the Works and not to get it done through other 
agencies. Consequently, the decisions taken by the companies were erroneous to that extent.

5.1.5.5   Improper planning for Commercial Complex in Bhopal 
The SCC, Bhopal issued work order (October 2018) for construction of a Commercial 
complex Signature Tower (consisting of one Basement and Ground floor plus eight floors)  
to a contractor at a cost of ` 34.93 crore.  
After the award of the contract, the SCC changed the scope of the work (September 2019) 
by increasing the height of the Complex to 10 floors, and two basements (for parking) so as 
to provide a better monetization model. Estimated cost of the revised projec t was ` 72.73 
crore. The work was to be completed by January 2021. 
After a lapse of 12 months (since changing the scope of work) and a fter incurring 
expenditure of ` 21.06 crore, the project was moving at tardy pace with only the two 
basement floors having  been completed. However, the SCC, instead of penalizing the 
contractor for the shoddy work, decided to sell the Complex in its present stage. The reasons 
for arriving at this decision were not available in the records. The Board in its resolution 
(August 2020) accordingly approved the resolution without seeking reasons for the change 
in plan. Audit observed that: 
• The SCC, Bhopal suffered from complete lack of foresightedness and vision while 
planning for the project. Consequently, after incurring an expenditure of ` 21.06 
crore on building merely two floors, the SCC decided to sell the building which was 
totally illogical and indicated extremely weak planning processes in the SCC. 
• The Board approved the decision without seeking reasons for the same, especially 
since a significant amount had been spent on construction. 
• The SCC , Bhopal  by stopping the work midway (in order to sell t he project) 
indemnified the contractor from paying penalties considering the tardy pace of work 
despite spending almost 36 per cent of the estimate costs. There were no records of 
missed timelines/milestones vis-à-vis the payments processed. 
• After a lapse of 24 months since the stoppage of the work, the SCC, Bhopal is yet to 
sell the under-construction building, which remains in its incomplete condition. 
Image-5.1.1 below shows the incomplete work status of Signature Tower. 
Image-5.1.1: Incomplete commercial complex i.e., Signature Tower 
 
(Photo: Audit Team during Joint Physical Inspection, 26 July 2021) 


 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
126 
 
Thus, poor planning and lack of vision of the SCC, Bhopal  resulted in blockage of ` 21.06 
crore. Furthermore, according to the agreement, interest free secured advance if paid to the 
contractor was recoverable from the future running bills. In this connection, Audit also 
observed that secured advance of ` 2.55 crore was given to the contractor out of which   
` 0.80 crore still not recovered (August 2022). In the event of a complete stoppage of work, 
the chances of recovery of the amount appear to be remote, resulting in undue favour to the 
contractor. 
GoMP stated that the Design was changed to develop  this project to achieve maximum 
permissible floor area ratio and due to financial issues of the Company , the Board decided 
to sell it as on current stage.  
However, after a lapse of 24 months since the stoppage of the work, the SCC, Bhopal is yet 
to sell the under-construction building. It remained in incomplete condition and resulted in 
blocked amount of ` 21.26 crore due to poor planning.  
5.1.6   Project Execution 
 
5.1.6.1 Work awarded to L2 bidder due to non -compliance with tender 
requirements by the SCC Gwalior 
The SCC, Gwalior invited tenders (August 2020) for the work of  “Design, supply, install, 
operate, maintain and transfer of musical fountain at Katora Tall” , in response, three 
agencies submitted bids  (August 2020) . As per N otice Inviting Tenders, the technical 
evaluation involved two stages - first stage was verification of pre -qualification of online 
submitted documents and visit by SCC officials to bidder’s previous project for verification 
and the second stage involved technical presentation by the bidders qualified in first stage.  
Audit observed that the SCC, Gwalior did not follow the requirements of technical 
evaluation and approved all the bidders for financial evaluati on. Consequently, the SCC 
opened the financial bids (September 2020) without visiting the previous project of the 
bidders. After financial evaluation, the bid of the L1 agency was rejected on the grounds of 
furnishing false experience certificate in respect of pre -qualification criteria for experience 
of completing similar work. The work was then awarded to L2  bidder at a cost of ` 2.91 
crore. Thus, the company failed to properly evaluate the correctness of bids at the technical 
evaluation stage and proceeded onto financial evaluation, which resulted in award of contract 
to L2 bidder instead of L1 bidder. 
GoMP had not submitted reply in this regard.  
5.1.6.2   Excess expenditure due to irregular retendering 
The SCC, Indore floated (November 2017) a Notice Inviting Tender (NIT) for the work 
‘Improvement of Water Supply and Sewerage Systems with operation and maintenance for 
a period of 10 years. The NIT attracted only a single bidder and was accordingly cancelled. 
The SCC again floated a tender in January 2018. In response, three bidders participated in 
the bidding process.  

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
127 
 
Based on the bidding process, M/s Tejas  Constructions and infrastructure Pvt. Ltd. was 
found to be the lowest bidder (L1) with the quoted rate of ` 201.43 crore. However , L2 
bidder (M/s Vishnu Prakash R Punglia Limited) objected to the tendering process citing that 
the practice adopted for onli ne opening of financial bids was corrupt and fraudulent and 
calculation of lowest bid was wrong. However, as per the noting in the SCC, Indore files, in 
the same letter, the L2 had accepted that the process of calculation of lowest bidder was 
correct. Further, the Company which was managing the portal “www.mpeproc.gov.in,” also 
stated that all the three bids were brought on the same platform and it could be certified that 
the process adopted wa s a bsolutely fair and transparent. Despite these, tender was not 
awarded to L1 bidder and fresh Notice Inviting Tender was floated as third call (April 2018).  
After the invitation of third call, M/s Vishnu Prakash R Punglia Limited was found to be the 
lowest bidder at the bid price of ` 237.50 crore, which was higher by ` 36.07 crore than the 
lowest bid quoted in second call. This has not only resulted in an excess financial burden of 
` 36.07 crore but also raises doubt about  the process where  work was allotted to the 
contractor who had questioned the tendering process earlier. Hence, the immature decision 
to move for inviting third call was not justified. The work was to be completed by September 
2020 and the work got delayed by two years. 
GoMP stated that in the interest of the project, and to avoid legal complication retendering 
was done. However, the company itself, in one of the notes, stated that L2 bidder of second 
call had accepted that the bidding process was correct.  
The reply is not acceptable as the SCC flouted the financial rules resulting in undue benefit 
to a contractor, while raising doubts on the entire tendering process.  
The Govern ment should fix responsibility o n the concerned officials, who violated the 
financial rules, resulting in loss to the Government exchequer. 
5.1.6.3   Re-ordering to the same supplier instead of calling fresh tenders 
As per clause 22 of Madhya Pradesh Store Purchase rules, 2015, another supply order to the 
same supplier, in continuation of the supply order already placed earlier, may be place d by 
a government agency. But the quantity of the total supply in the new supply order should not 
exceed 50 per cent of the previous order. 
The SCC, Indore had placed an order to M/s Hy va India Pvt. Ltd. (June 2017) for supply, 
erection, and commissioning of Transfer Station Equipment (Six static compaction systems 
with loading mechanism, 18 detachable containers and eight Hook Loaders with Vehicle). 
The Work Order was issued for a total cost of ` 8.47 crore. The supplier supplied all the 
materials ( December 2017) as per order and the payments were  made accordingly 
(December 2017 to April 2018).  
After the completion of the supply order, the SCC, Indore assessed additional requirements 
for the same equipment and another work order amounting to ` 8.59 crore was issued to the 
same supplier on the basis of previous order, without resorting to tendering. There was no 
mention of any urgency in the documents relating to the grant of approval for work order.   

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
128 
 
Since the new work order was more than 100 per cent of the previous order , the action of 
the SCC, Indore in issuing the second supply order to the same vendor without resorting to 
fresh tendering was in contravention of Madhya Pradesh Store Purchase Rules 2015. 
GoMP stated that the additional requirement was assessed, and additional work order was 
given to M /s Hyva India Pvt. Ltd . The reply is not acceptable as  the competent authority 
cannot take decisions in supersession of extant orders  which stated that repeat orders for 
more than 50 per cent of previous order cannot be placed. 
5.1.6.4   Infructuous expenditure due to deficiencies in tendering process  
The SCC, Indore envisaged an intelligent transport management system for the public at an 
estimated cost of ` 40.00 crore. The system was to be installed at 30 places, and was to be 
replete with smart variable messages, reflective sign boards, cautionary boards, etc.  
Based on the tendering process, the L1 bidder was awarded the contract and a Work Order 
was accordingly issued for a contract amount of ` 40.56 crore (August 2019). The Contract 
also provided for depositing of Performance Guarantee in the form of Bank Guarantee by 
the selected bidder and the contractor accordingly deposited Bank Guarantee amounting to 
` 2.03 crore.  
The contractor accordingly started the work and upto March 2021, the payments of ` 23.38 
crore had been processed against the work done. The first running bill was paid in November 
2019. It was seen that the contractor thereafter failed to complete the work and the contract 
had to be terminated (July 2021). In this regard, Audit observed the following issues: 
• The Central Vigilance Commission’s Office Memorandum (December 2002) provides 
the eligibility criteria for award of work to a contractor after assessing pre-qualifications 
for the work which includes Average Annual Financial Turnover during the last three 
years and experience of the contractor having successfully completed similar works 
during previous seven years. It was seen that no such criteria for the assessment were 
fixed in the tender document to assess the capacity of the contractor to successfully 
execute the work. Thus, the SCC , Indore awarded the work order, on the basis of a 
deficient tendering process, to a contractor who was not competent to complete the 
work.  
• Consequently, the contractor could not complete the work and the contract was 
terminated (July 2021) under the clauses of the agreement. Thus, the failure of the SCC, 
Indore to adhere to a robust tendering process resulted in infructuous expenditure 
amounting to ` 23.38 crore. Further, the SCC has not penalised the contractor for failure 
to complete the work by encashing the Bank Guarantee amounting to ` 2.03 crore 
(submitted by the contractor before execution of work).  
GoMP stated that the matter is to be d ecided by the Honorable High Court. However, the 
fact remains that the contract was awarded to  a contractor which was not competent to 
complete the work , resulting in termination of the contract . The reply was silent about 
assessing per-qualification and n ot encashing the bank guarantee after termination of the 
contract. 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
129 
 
5.1.6.5   Acceptance of single tender  
The Public Works Department, Government Madhya Pradesh (January 2011) issued 
direction14 to all Government Departments to not accept/open bids if single bids are received 
in first call and to call for fresh bids again. If even after the second round of tendering single 
bids are received, then the competent authority can decide on opening the bid and accepting 
the same. 
The SCC, Indore floated an NIT for the work of  Development of Integrated Smart Road 
Network Phase-V (June 2018) having estimated cost of ` 48.51 crore. Only one contractor 
responded to the bid . O n the  basis of  technical evaluation , the contractor was found 
technically suitable, and the contract was awarded (Sept 2018) at ` 60.58 crore, which was 
24.88 per cent above the Schedule of Rates.  
Thus, the SCC , Indore in violation of GoMP  instructions, accepted single bid in the first 
round of tendering, resulting in irregular award of work amounting to ` 60.58 crore.  
GoMP stated that the work was awarded after proper justification with  approval of 
competent authority . However, no justifi cation was found to be given by the competent 
authority in record during the award of contract and the competent authority took decisions 
in supersession of extant orders.  
5.1.6.6   Irregular allotment of additional work without fresh tendering 
As per Madhya Pradesh Works Department Manual, tenders must be invited for all the works 
proposed to be given on contract unless the amount of the work propo sed to be given on 
contract is ` 15,000 or less. Further, the General Financial Rules (GFR) 2017, also provide 
that “no work shall be commenced, or liability incurred in connection with it until tenders 
are invited and processed in accordance with rules.” 
In SCC, Ujjain, Audit observed that the SCC, Ujjain awarded (October 2018) t he 
construction of a School Complex in Ujjain to a contractor (M/s M.P. Babariya) at a tender 
cost of ` 19.90 crore . After a lapse of two years from awarding the work contract, an 
additional work ‘construction of Urdu school’ under the existing contract was also allotted 
to the same contractor at an additional cost of ` 5.50 crore (October 2020) without resorting 
to a fresh tendering process. Thereafter another work of ‘Pipeline shifting and replacing with 
a new pipeline’ at ` 74 lakh was awarded (April 2021) to the same contractor as additional 
work without any tendering process. 
Consequently, the SCC , Ujjain  irregularly awarded additional work amounting to  
` 6.24 crore without following the rules, resulting in undue benefit to the contractor to that 
extent. 
GoMP stated that the work of execution of Urdu  School had to be taken on priority as  the 
school was to be shifted to this location. Since under the ongoing project , the rate of the 
contract was the lowest among all the running contracts, the board approved the addition of 
the same in the scope of the earlier work. During execution, pipeline issue was encountered 
                                                 
14  F52/1/10/19-51, 3 January 2021. 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
130 
 
and hence the work had to be added in the scope of work as regular tendering would have 
taken time and delayed the work. 
However, as new work was allotted to the same contractor without adopting tendering 
process and the reasons citing urgency were also not recorded  the decision in supersession 
of extant rules.

attributable to the contractors. Further the fact that delays were attributable to the change in 
scope of work and encroachment indicates poor planning and lack of co-ordination between 
different government agencies.

5.1.6.8   Environment Clearance not taken from State Environment Impact 
Assessment Authority  
As per notification15 issued by the Ministry of Environment and Forests, prior Environmental 
Clearance is required for construction of Buildings, Construction of Projects, Townships and 
Area Development Projects . These activities shall require prior Environmental C learance 
from the State Environment Impact Assessment Authority (SEIAA) before any construction 
work, or prep aration of land by the project management except for securing the land, is 
started on the project or activity.  
Audit observed that in three out of four selected Smart Cities, that ABD projects fall under 
categories of Building, Construction projects, Area Development projects or development 
of Townships. Therefore, these required prior Environment Clearance from SEIAA, but the 
Smart Cities did not do so before initiating the projects and starting the construction work. 
As a result, the Environment Impact Assessment of various projects could not be carried out 
in violation of the provisions of Ministry of Environment and Forests.  
GoMP stated that SCC, Bhopal had taken provisional approva l, reply is silent about other 
three cities. 
5.1.6.9   Non-installation of amenities 
The Smart City Plan had a compelling three-pronged vision of improving public life through 
quantum jump in quality of services, simplifying governance and aligning incentives of city 
functionaries. The idea is to maximize reu se of existing infrastructure, creating a backbone 
for smarter initiatives in the future and modernize service delivery. 
For operationalization of ICT components as a part of the smart city plan, the SCC, Bhopal 
entered into a concession agreement (October 2016) with Bharti Infratel, Ericsson, Smart X 
services Ltd. And HPL Electric & Power Ltd. 
As per the contract, for development of facilities, the concessionaire s were provide d 
obligations by SCC, Bhopal as detailed below: 
• Free of cost right of way for laying of fiber cables and installation of Smart poles for 
Telecom Cell within the Municipal limits for the duration of the contract (15 Years). 
• Uninterrupted electricity free of cost to the Bidder for the smart poles, surveillance 
camera and wi-fi access points, cellular base stations, EV charging point, 
Environmental sensors and any other device which were the part of the RFP. 
• Exclusive advertisement rights to be provided to the concessionaire to earn revenue          
out of advertisements, digital signages, wi-fi, conventional billboards on the telecom 
sites, traffic signals, street lightings, etc.

• Wasteful expenditure on development of a mobile application  
As per C lause 4.9 of the concession agreement, a  mobile application for disseminating 
citizen related information and services was to be developed free of cost by one of the 
concessionaires (M/s Bharti Infratel) and it  should have been made freely available for 
downloading to the citizens in Android/iOS/Windows platforms.  
Although the concessionaire was to develop the application free of cost, the SCC , Bhopal 
entered into an agreement with M/s Price Water Coopers Pvt. Ltd. (August 2016) for 
development of a mobile application (BHOPAL PLUS APP) at a cost of ` 3.98 crore.  
Thus, the SCC, Bhopal instead of getting a free application from the concessionaire, spent 
an amount of ` 3.98 crore on development of the mobile application, resulting in wasteful 
expenditure to that extent, besides granting undue favour to the concessionaire. 
GoMP stated that the application to be provided free of cost had limited functionality and 
the citizen app was required by the smart city to provide for the extended function ality like 
water tax, birth certificate etc.

The reply is not acceptable  as the free of cost application also included citizen se rvices, 
birth/death certificate, viewing and paying utility bills and the SCC,Bhopal had not utilized 
the same. 
5.1.6.10   Improper estimation of contracts 
Preparation of Detailed Estimate of the work after D etailed Survey and investigation for 
preparing Detailed Project Report is a prerequisite of planning. Preparation of estimates on 
the basis of actual data collected by field study and detailed survey & investigation helps in 
preparation of realistic  and correct estimate and consequently management of financial 
resources by the Government.  
Audit observed in 35 out of 100 works o f four Smart City Companies , that there were 
variations of (-)3.24 per cent to 73.05  per cent17 from the estimated cost as detailed in 
Appendix-5.1.2. The variation of individual items varied from 12 to 3 ,805 per cent which 
reflects improper estimation. Further, non- inclusion of essential items in the estimate at the 
time of preparation resulted in increase of cost of work as these were finally taken up at the 
time of execution of work as an extra item. Further, execution of additional quantity against 
the estimated quantity too resulted in increase in cost of work. 
GoMP stated that all projects are conceptualized , designed, implemented and monitored 
under supervision of in-house consultancy. After analysis of the variations , the Board of 
Directors approves the deviation. 
However, as huge variations indicates that the estimation was improper and not based on 
adequate survey and investigations. Moreover, specialised consultancy was involved in 
estimation, still the need arises for such deviations.  
5.1.6.11   Non-deposit/short deposit of royalty of ` 10.62 crore  
As per provisions of agreement and orders of Government of Madhya Pradesh , (February 
2003), the final bill of contractors shall be paid for the work only upon production of ‘No 
Dues Certificate of royalty’ issued by Mineral Resource Department.  
The Audit found in 18 works of Bhopal and Indore that the contractors executed construction 
works using various quantity of minor minerals, for which royalty had not been deducted 
properly by the Departments. Neither the royalty was deposited in the Government accounts 
nor was the ‘No Dues Certificate of royalty’ obtained from the contractors as required before 
making final payments. This led to loss of ` 10.62 crore to the government exchequer as 
detailed in Appendix-5.1.3. 
GoMP stated that royalty shall be paid by contractors and while closing the Contract, ‘No 
Dues Certificate’ will be obtained from the Contractors. However, fact remains that neither 
royalty was paid to the Government nor was the requisite No Dues Certificate obtained from 
the Contractors. 
  
                                                 
17  In 14 cases the variation was more than 25 per cent. 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit

5.1.7   Project monitoring  
 
5.1.7.1   Working of High-Powered Steering Committee  
As per Smart City Mission guidelines, there shall be a State level High Powered Steering 
Committee (HPSC) 18, which would steer the Mission Programme in its entirety. The key 
responsibilities of the HPSC are to provide guidance to the Mission and provide a State level 
platform for exchange of ideas pertaining to development of Smart Cities. The GoMP 
constituted the HPSC in July 2015 in compliance with the Smart City Mission guidelines. 
Audit observed from the records that the HPSC held two meetings in 2016-17 while it failed 
to hold any meetings during 2017-18 to 2020-21. In the absence of regular meetings, Audit 
could not ascertain the role of the HPSC and whether they performed any productive 
function with regard to providing guidance to the Mission. 
5.1.7.2   City Level Smart City Advisory Forum 
As per Smart City Mission guidelines, a City Level Smart City Advisory Forum (SCAF) 19 
will be established at the city level for all Smart Cities to advise and enable colla boration 
among various stakeholders. Accordingly, all the cities in Madhya Pradesh included under 
the Smart City Mission established20 the SCAF between September 2016 and June 2018.  
Audit observed that the meetings of the City Advisory Forum were not conducted at interval 
of once in three months. In Bhopal and Gwalior, meetings were not held for two years since 
the formation of the SCAFs while in Ujjain meetings were not held for an entire year since 
inception.  
GoMP had not submitted any reply in this regard. 
5.1.7.3   Other issues indicating poor monitoring 
Some of the issues concer ning monitoring of the functioning of the SCCs are mentioned 
below: 
• As per the terms of the contract, the contractor shall be liable to make payment to all 
its employees, workers, and sub-contractors and ensure compliance with the labour 
laws. Audit observed that the payment files/bills of contractors do not contain any of 
the documents related to Employees Provident Funds and Employees State Insurance 
payments by the contractors as per the terms of contract.  
GoMP stated that their EPF documents are verified during tendering Process and 
instructions issued to them.

the ULB. There would also be a State Mission Director, nominated by the State Government.  
19  Includes the District Collector, MP, MLA, Mayor, CEO of Smart City, local youths, technical experts, 
and at least one member from the area representing registered Residents Welfare Association among

20  Indore in September 2016, Bhopal in April 2017, Ujjain in September 2017 and Gwalior in June 2018.

However, as documentary evidence of deposi ts should be verified not only during 
the time of tendering but also during the payment of bills.  
• The contracts did not contain the necessary clauses such as the ‘Risk and Cost clause 
of the contractor’21. In the absence of Risk and Cost clause, the extra cost if any that 
will occur or has occurred due to leaving of the work midway by the contractor, 
cannot be recovered from the original contractor in case of termination  of the  
contract. 
GoMP stated that end to end defect liability for the implementation and operation is 
the responsibility of the implementing agency. However, if the Contract is terminated 
or Contract left work in between the SCC will have to bear extra burden.  
• According to the provisions of agreement, a contractor is required to take risk 
insurance policy from approved insurance companies jointly with the concerned 
Smart City Company and he will bear all costs towards the insurance for the full 
period of execution of works including the defect liability period for the full amount 
of contract against all loss of damage from whatever cause arising other than 
excepted risks for which he is responsible under the terms of the contract. During the 
audit it was observed that eight contracts valuing ` 425.17 crore were not insured in 
Bhopal. This resulted in undue financial aid to the contractors amounting to ` 17 lakh 
(calculated at 0.04 per cent of the cost).  
GoMP stated that all Contractors take insurance. However, no documentary evidence 
submitted and in files no record related to insurance was available. 
• As per provisions, quality tests of materials and executed items are to be done and 
test reports are to be kept in records. The contractors did not submit the records 
relating to test reports, thereby creating doubt regarding the quality of works. 
GoMP stated that all works carried out as Plan and quality of works is as per standard. 
However, no documentary evidence submitted and in files no record related to tests 
was available. 
• As per the Contract Conditions , Technical Persons were to be deployed for the 
projects by the contractors . In the event of non -deployment of Technical Persons, 
deductions at certain rates were to be made from the payments to be made to the 
contractors. In eight projects involving ` 475.06 crore, Technical Persons had not 
been deployed by the Contractors, but no deduction s were made as per contract 
provisions. 
GoMP stated that there is no deviation from the contract. However, no documentary 
evidence submitted and in files no record related with deployment of technical person 
was available. 
                                                 
21  In event of Contractor’s failure to execute work, the Company reserves the right to get the work 
executed through any means at the risk and cost of the Contractor. 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
137 
 
• The Smart City Companies keep their funds in Bank Accounts. In the interest of 
financial prudence, most of the SCCs maintain their funds in auto-flexi accounts so 
as to get higher interest on idle funds. Howeve r, it was observed SCC, Gwalior 
continued to maintain its funds in savings account. Consequently, owing to non -
monitoring of funds and non-maintenance of funds in auto flexi accounts, the SCC, 
Gwalior suffered an interest loss of ` 60.59 lakh. 
GoMP had not submitted any reply in this regard.  
• As per the agreement, mobilisation advance at the rate of 10 per cent of contract 
value can be given to a contractor. Such mobilisation advance bears interest of 10 
per cent per annum and shall be calculated fr om the date of payment to the date  of 
recovery. The recovery of such mobilisation advance including the interest shall be 
made through deduction from the contractor’s bills. 
The work of construction of a multi-storied government housing including internal 
electrification, plumbing, etc., was allotted to a contracto r (October 2018) for an 
amount of ` 314.40 crore with schedule time of completion of 24 months.  
As per the agreement the Contractor was paid mobilisation advance of ` 10 crore in 
December 2018 and ` five crore in March 2019. An amount of ` two crore was 
deducted from the 8th running bill (February 2020) as mobilisation advance and 
further, upto 14th bill (March 2021) the amount of ` five crore had been adjusted in 
the running bills, but the interest of ` 2.97 crore on the mobilisation advance has not 
been recovered till date. 
GoMP stated that the interest will be recovered.  However, the recovery as per the 
clause of agreement not done. For the year 2019-20 the interest was ` 1.50 crore non 
deduction of interest had  also resulted in further loss of interest to Company  
(` 12 lakh calculated @ eight per cent upto 31 March 2021). 
• The SCC, Bhopal initiated the work of Design and development of Smart Park near 
Bharat Mata Chouraha, Bhopal. For this purpose, an advance ` 10.00 lakh was paid 
(February 2020) to Madhya Pradesh Water and Land Management Institute Bhopal 
(WALMI) for survey, soil te sting and other works related to Smart Park. WALMI 
had not done any work for which the advance was paid , resulting in wasteful 
expenditure to that extent. 
GoMP stated that the advance will be us ed in some other work in future, however , 
as the SCC is losing interest on the deposited amount. 
5.1.8   Conclusion 
The Smart City Mission was aimed to promote cities that provide core infrastructure and 
give a decent quality of life to its citizens, a clean and sustainable environment and 
application of ‘Smart’ Solutions.  

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
138 
 
During audit, it was seen that t he Smart City Companies had diverted from their  approved 
plan and added the works which were being done by the ULBs and moreover, many of these 
works does not contain Smart elements as desired in the Smart City Mission Guidelines.  
There were instances of works remaining incomplete even after lapse of several years.  
There were lacunae in the tendering process where the established procedures and rules were 
not followed. Estimates prepared were not realistic which resulted in escalation in the cost 
of the project. Further, huge variations in estimated cost as well as in individual items shows 
that improper estimates were prepared without proper survey and investigation. There were 
delays in completion of works due to fault of the contactors and lack of coordination between 
the authorities also leading to the delay in achieving the objective the Smart City Mission. 
Consequently, the intended objectives/benefits from the projects could not be achieved even 
after lapse of the five years’ time. 
5.1.9   Recommendations 
In view of the audit findings, Audit recommends the following: 
• SCCs should select the Projects as per the spirit and vision of SCM and involve the 
citizens also in case of change from the approved SCP. 
• SCCs should invariably comply with codal  provisions with respect of awarding the  
work through tendering process and the Government should fix responsibility on the 
concerned officials, who violated the financial rules, resulting in loss to the 
Government exchequer. 
• SCCs should not pay for the wo rks undertaken by government entities under other 
schemes as the Grants are tied for SCM. 
• SCCs should ensure timely deposit of royalty to Government exchequer. 
• Midterm review mechanism should be strengthened by HPSC to review and guide 
the SCCs for course corrections and early completion of delayed works. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
139 
 
5.2       Compliance Audit on “Receipts (Property Tax and Rent) in Urban 
Local Bodies” 
 
5.2.1   Introduction  
Article 243W of the Constitution of India envisages that the Legislature of a State may, by 
law, endow the municipalities with powers and authority to enable them to function as 
institutions of self -government and such law may contain provisions for the devolution of 
powers and responsibilities upon municipalities. Further, as per A rticle 243X of the 
Constitution of India, the Legislature of a State may authorize a municipality to levy and 
collect such taxes, duties, tolls and fees. Section 133(1) (a)(b) of the Madhya Pradesh 
Municipal Corporation (MPMC) Act, 1956 and Section 129 1(a)(b) of the Madhya Pradesh 
Municipalities (MPM) Act, 1961 provide that the Corporation or Council may, by a 
resolution, at the time to final adoption of the budget estimates for the next financial year 
impose taxes or fees as specified and increase the rate of taxes or fees already imposed. The 
sources of own revenue of Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) are tax revenue 22 and non -tax 
revenue23. Being the predominant components of receipts, property tax and rent24 have been 
selected for the purpose of audit, under tax and non-tax revenue respectively. 
The Urban Development and Housing Department (UDHD), headed by the Principal 
Secretary to the Government of Madhya Pradesh, is the Nodal Department for the 
governance of all ULBs. The Directorate, Urban Administration and Development (UADD), 
functions as an interface between the State Government and the ULBs. The UADD is headed 
by the Commissioner who is assisted by nine Joint Directors 25 at Division level. 
Commissioner and Chief Municipal Officer are the executive head of the  corporations and 
other ULBs (Nagar Palika Parishads and Nagar Parishads) respectively. 
There are 40726 ULBs ((16 Nagar Palik Nigams (NN), 99 Nagar Palika Parishads NPP) and 
292 Nagar Parishads (NP)) in the State. All NNs, 58 NPPs having more than ` one crore of 
total receipt (Property Tax and Rent) and 50 NPs having more than ` 50 lakh of total receipt 
(Property Tax and Rent) were considered for sampling. Out of these three NNs, six NPPs 
and seven NPs were selected on the basis of Simple Random Sampling without Replacement 
Method. Total receipts (Property tax and Rent) of these units during 2018-19 to 2020-21 are 
shown in Table-5.2.1 below:  
 
 
 
 
                                                 
22  Tax revenue (property tax, tax on advertisement, tax on carriage/cart).    
23 Non-tax revenue (receipt from water supply charges, rent from shopping complex, land and estate 
building plan sanction fees, license fees, park fees, sale of condemned materials, interest on fixed 
deposit etc.). 
24  Rent refers to rent from shopping complex. 
25   Bhopal, Gwalior, Indore, Jabalpur, Narmadapuram, Rewa, Sagar, Shahdol and Ujjain.  
26  As per UADD’s Administrative Report 2020-21.

 (Source: Information provided by UADD) 
5.2.2   Audit Objectives 
The Compliance Audit seeks to ascertain whether: 
• Assessment, imposition and collection of Property Tax were done in a sound manner; 
• Property Tax Board (PTB) was adequately assisting ULBs in the process of improving 
their tax realisation efforts; 
• Rent was levied and realised effectively; and 
• Proper monitoring and evaluation system exists in the Department for collection of 
revenue. 
5.2.3   Audit Criteria 
The criteria for the Compliance Audit were derived from the following sources: 
• Madhya Pradesh Municipal Corporation (MPMC) Act, 1956, 
• Madhya Pradesh Municipalities (MPM) Act, 1961, 
• Madhya Pradesh Municipal (Transfer of Immovable Property) (MPMTIP) Rules, 
2016, 
• Madhya Pradesh Municipality Determination of Annual Rent Value of Buildings/ 
Lands (MPMDARV) Rules, 1997 as amended in 2020, and 
• Notifications, Orders, Circulars and Instructions issued by the State Government, 
Directorate of Urban Administration and Development and ULBs.  
5.2.4   Audit Scope and Methodology 
The Compliance Audit covered the period of 2018 -19 to 2020 -21 through test check of 
records, collection of data and information related to Property Tax and Rent of the 16 
selected ULBs (Three 27 Nagar Nigams, Six 28 Nagar Palika Parishads and Seven 29 Nagar 
Parishads) and Commissioner (UADD) between June 2021 to March 2022.  
In addition, the Property Tax Board had also been selected for their explicit comments on 
the irregularities observed in Audit and functioning of the Board.  
An Entry Conference was held on 20 July 2021 with the Commissioner, UADD, in which 
the audit methodology, scope, objectives and criteria were explained. The outcome of audit 
                                                 
27   NNs- (Bhopal, Chhindwara and Dewas). 
28   NPPs- (Bina, Biaora, Mandideep, Pithampur, Shajapur, and Tikamgarh). 
29   NPs- (Bankhedi, Depalpur, Khategaon, Khilchipur, Mahugaon, Nalkheda and Timarni).  

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
141 
 
was reported to the Government in March 2022 to obtain their views. The replies/views of 
Government were received in November 2022. Thes e have been suitably incorporated at 
appropriate places.   
Audit Findings 
The significant audit findings relating to Property Tax and Rent are discussed in the 
succeeding paragraphs: 
5.2.5   Property Tax  
A tax payable by the owners of buildings and lands situated within the city with reference to 
gross Annual Letting Value30 (ALV), also referred as Taxable Property Value (TPV) of the 
buildings or land is called Property Tax 31 (PT). It shall be charged, levied and paid, at the 
rate not less than six per cent and not more than 10 per cent of the ALV32. Further, properties 
owned by the Central government are exempted under the MPMC Act, 1956 and the MPM 
Act, 1961, however Service Charge is leviable on such properties for services provided by 
ULBs. According ly, issue relating to the Service Charge has also been covered in this 
Paragraph along with the Property Tax. 
5.2.5.1   Non revision of the rates for determination of ALV/TPV  
Section 138 of the MPMC Act, 1956 and Section 126 of the MPM Act, 1961 provide that 
the ALV/TPV of any building or land shall be determined as per the resolution adopted by 
the Corporation/ Municipality. The Commissioner/ Chief Municipal Officer (CMO) shall 
prepare the draft resolution for the next financial year and submit it before Corporation or 
Council in the respective ULB before 31 December of each financial year. In case, the 
Corporation / Council fails to adopt the resolution by 31st March of each financial year then 
the draft resolution shall be presented to the Mayor/ Presid ent and the resolution approved 
by the Mayor/ President shall be deemed to be the finally adopted by the Corporation / 
Council. 
Audit observed (between July 2021 and January 2022) in 12 33 out of 16 test checked ULBs 
that the Commissioner or CMOs had not pre pared and submitted the draft resolution to the 
Council/ Corporation during the period 2018 -19 to 2020 -21 and adopted old rates for 
determination of ALV/TPV.  
The Government did not offer any comment in this regard. However, during audit, the CMOs 
of four ULBs34 stated (between July 2021 and January 2022) that the proposals for revision 
                                                 
30  The ALV of any building or land, whether revenue paying or not, shall be determined  as per the 
resolution of the Corporation or Council adopted in this behalf, (on the basis of per square metre of 
the built-up area of a building or land) as the case may be, taking  into consideration, the area in which 
the building or land is situated, its location, situation, purpose for which it is used, its capacity for 
profitable use, quality of construction of the buildings and other relevant factors subject to rules,  as

5.2.5.2   Erroneous computation of ALV/TPV 
As per Rule 10 (1) of the MPMDARV Rules, 1997, every property owner of municipal area, 
shall himself calculate the taxable property value of his property and amount of property tax 
and deposit the amount of tax in the municipality within the prescribed time along with the 
return. He shall self -assess the ALV/TPV of his property as per the resolution adopted by 
the Corporation/ Council in this regard. There is a similar provision under the MPMDARV 
Rules, 2020.  Further, a consolidated tax is also levied by the ULB upon the households 
whose ALV is below the exemption limit36.  
Further, Rule 11 of MPMDARV Rules, 1997 provides that on scrutiny of the return, if the 
municipal officer finds that any of the information  mentioned therein is not correct or is 
doubtful or he deems it necessary to reassess the taxable property value due to any reason, 
then the municipal officer may take action for reassessment of the taxable property value 
under the provision of the Act (MP MC Act 1956 or MPM Act 1961). There is a similar 
provision under the MPMDARV Rules, 2020. 
While going through the information available with e -Nagar Palika Portal, Audit observed 
(between June 2021 and January 2022) that: 
(a) In six 37 out of 16 test checked  ULBs, ALV/TPV against 8,165 properties was not 
computed on e -Nagar Palika Portal due to non -availability of details of property such as 
usage type (commercial/ residential properties), usage factor (self-occupied/ rented), number 
                                                 
35  PIC for every Council is constituted by the President of the Council from amongst the electe d 
councilors within seven days from the date of election of Vice President (Section 70 of MPM Act 1961). 
36   Land and building, the annual letting value of which does not exceed six thousand rupees in case of 
municipal area having population of one lac or above and four thousand and eight hundred rupees in 
case of municipal area having population below one lakh (Section 127 A(2)(b) of MPM Act, 1961 and 
Section 135 (b) of MPMC Act, 1956). 
37  NNs- (Bhopal and Chhindwara), NPPs - (Shajapur and Tikamgarh) and NPs - (Mahugaon and 
Nalkheda). 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
143 
 
of floor and construction type (temporary and reinforced cement concrete construction, etc.) 
of properties. 
(b) In NN, Dewas, against the 1,36 1 properties, wrong ALV/TPV had been computed on  
e-Nagar Palika Portal as the area was erroneously mentioned as either zero or one square 
foot. However, the property tax was not computed on the said area of properties. 
(c) Consolidated tax was to be levied on properties whose ALV/TPV was less than  
` 4,800 (population below one lakh). Audit, however, observed that consolidated tax instead 
of PT was levied against 47 properties in NP Bankhedi despite their ALV was more than       
` 4,800 and thus PT was leviable on these properties. 
Thus, in the absence of computation or erroneous computation on e-Nagar Palika Portal, the 
PT in respect of total 9,573 properties could not be accurately ensured and also could not be 
levied which ultimately deprived the respective ULBs from the tax revenue in a shape of PT 
or Consolidated Tax. The details are given in Appendix 5.2.1. 
In reply the Government stated (November 2022) that the modified data will be uploaded in 
case of six38 ULBs after verification. Regarding NPP, Shajapur, it was stated that the error 
has been rectified in the ensuing year. Regarding NN, Bhopal, the Goverment stated that 
ALV/property tax is being calculated correctly through SAP Software/e -nagrapalika Portal 
during last few years.  
The fact remains that non -computation or erroneous computation of ALV/ TPV and not 
taking appropriate and timely corrective action led to non-imposition of property tax as well 
as consolidated tax wherever applicable. Further, despite the use of aforesaid IT systems, 
there were ambiguities in calculation of ALV as per the data provided by NN, Bhopal and 
in aforementioned seven audited ULBs as well. 
5.2.5.3   Non-recovery of arrears of Property Tax 
Section 173(1) of the MPMC Act, 1956 and Section 164(1) of MPM Act, 1961 stipulate that 
when any amount declared under the provisions of the Acts to be recoverable or payable on 
account of any tax, the Commissioner/ CMO shall, within the least practicable delay present 
a bill to the person liable for the payment. Further, as per Section 174(1) of the MPMC Act, 
1956 and Section 164(3) of the MPM Act, 1961, if the sum for which a bill is presented, is 
not paid and no objection has been preferred within 15 days from the presentation of the bill, 
the Commissioner/CMO serve upon the person to whom such bill has been presented a 
notice of demand.  
Further, Section 175(1) of the MPMC Act, 1956 and Section 167(1) of MPM Act, 1961, 
provide that the ULBs may recover the dues by way of sale of the movable property of the 
defaulter or by order of attachment and sale of immovable property belonging to him if the 
person on whom a notice of demand is served does not within 30 39/ 1540 days of service of 
                                                 
38  NNs-(Chhindwara and Dewas), NPP, Tikamgarh and NPs- (Bankhedi, Mahugaon and Nalkheda). 
39  Section 175(1) of the MPMC Act, 1956. 
40  Section 167(1) of MPM Act, 1961. 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
144 
 
such notice pay the sum demanded or neither satisfies the commissioner/ CMO of the reason 
for non-payment nor prefers appeal against the demand. 
While going through the data available at e -Nagar Palika Portal, Audit observed (between 
June 2021 to January 2022) in 1541 out of 16 test-checked ULBs that the Department has not 
recovered ` 347.98 crore (as of January 2022) as arrears of PT due from 1,14,030 property 
owners, where arrears of PT were more than ` 5,000 in each property. Further, Audit also 
observed that ULBs had not complied with the procedures for the recovery as laid down in 
MPMC Act, 1956 and MPM Act, 1961. Thus, the inaction of the ULBs in recovering arrears 
resulted in non-collection of realisable tax revenue of ` 347.98 crore, details of which are 
given in Appendix 5.2.2. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that action for recovery was being taken 
regularly by the 15 ULBs and nine 42 of the 15 ULBs had recovered an amount of ` 1.01 
crore in 2022-23. However, the reply needs further substantiation from the records. Further, 
regarding cases pertaining to NN, Bhopal, it was stated that an amount of ` 198.63 crore was 
recovered against the total demand of ` 529.66 crore during 2021-22.  However, it was not 
informed that how much of this amount pertains to the period covered in audit, i.e., 2018-19 
to 2020-21.  
5.2.5.4 Non levy of Property Tax on the properties owned by Board/ Nigam 
Offices 
Section 132(1)(a) of the MPMC Act, 1956 and Section 127(1)(a) of the MPM Act, 1961 
provide that PT shall be levied on buildings or land situated within the area of ULB. Further, 
Section 136(a) of MPMC Act, 1956 and Section 127-A(2)(a) of the MPM Act, 1961 provide 
that PT shall not be levied on the building and land owned by or vesting in the Union 
Government, the State Government and Corporation/ Council. 
Audit observed (June 2021 to January 2022) in six 43 out of 16 test checked ULBs that they 
did not impose PT of ` 1.71 crore on the properties44 which were not owned by Union/State 
Government or Corporation/ Council. This resulted in non -recovery of PT amounting to  
` 1.71 crore on these properties as detailed in Appendix 5.2.3. In case of two ULBs (NP 
Timarni and NP Bankhedi), the PT could not be computed due to non-availability of details 
of properties on the e-Nagar Palika Portal. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) regarding cases of six ULBs, that action 
for recovery is in progress and in cases of NPs - Bankhedi and Timarni, no comment was 
offered. This indicates that concrete action was lacking on the part of the Department.
