5.2.5.5 Non-imposition of Service Charge on the Central Government 
properties 
UADD, GoMP directed (September 2000) that Service charge be imposed on and collected 
from the Central Government buildings located inside ULBs in terms with Finance 
Department, GoI instructions (March 1967). The service charge was payable at the rate of 
75 per cent, 50 per cent and 33.33 per cent respectively of the property tax levied on property 
owners, depending upon whether Union of India or its department is utilizing the full 
services, or partial services or nil services.  
Audit observed (June to October 2021) from records in four 45 out of 16 test checked ULBs 
that service charge of ` 0.49 crore for the varying period 2011 -12 to 2020 -21 was not 
imposed on the properties of five Central Government Departments (Appendix 5.2.4 ). 
Further, though three Central Government Departments were functionin g under the area of 
four46 ULBs, but service charges could not be computed as the detail of properties, such as 
area, was not available with these ULBs. Thus, due to non-imposition of service charge and 
lack of information about the properties of Central Government Departments, the ULBs were 
deprived of considerable revenue. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) regarding cases of two NPPs-Tikamgarh 
and Mandideep, that action for recovery was being taken. In case of NPP, Biaora, it was 
stated that Railway Station property is exempted from PT, the reply was silent about the Post 
Office property. And in case of NPP, Bina , no comment was offered. It is obvious that the 
reply regarding the case of Railway property in NPP, Biaora was not relevant to the audit 
observation on Service Charge. Further, the replies indicate that concrete action was lacking 
on the part of the Department.  
5.2.5.6   Shortcomings in self-assessment return   
Section 138 of the MPMC Act, 1956, Section 126 of the MPM Act, 1961, and  Rule-10(1) 
of  MPMDARV Rules, 2020 provide that every property owner shall self-assess the ALV of 
his property located in ULB area as per the prescribed rates decided by the PIC and will pay 
amount of PT along with return in the prescribed format which inter alia included the details 
of property showing building /plot no/ name of road/ ward no etc. for deciding rate of ALV 
to be levied . 
Further, as per proviso to Rule -11 of MPMDARV Rules, 1997, the return received under 
Rule-10 will be checked by the ULBs and it can allow variation up to 10 per cent, however, 
in the case of variation of more than 10 per cent, owner of property shall be li able to pay 
penalty equal to five times the difference of return assessed by owner of the properties and 
the assessment made by the ULBs. There is a similar provision under the MPMDARV Rules, 
2020.

46  NN Chhindwara (details of Central  Government Department was not available with ULB), NPP

In eight47 out of 16 test  checked ULBs, Audit noticed (July 2021 to January 2022) that in

were accepted without calculation of the property tax in 177 cases. Besides, the cases of 
submission of returns and signatures of property owners were also not found in 357 and 156 
returns respectively. Address-related information of properties were not found mentioned in 
the 34 returns as well. None of the supporting documents (copy of Registry, Khasra and 
Khatoni) was found attached with the self -assessment in any of the cases. Thus, looking at 
the deficiencies in the self-assessment, the accuracy in computation of the PT could not be 
ensured by ULBs.  
The CMOs of ULBs48 stated that in future, the returns would be examined as per Rules. No

5.2.5.7   Constitution and functioning of Property Tax Board 
On the basis of the recommendation of the 13 th Finance Commission Para 10.160(vii), the 
State Government v ide Gazette Notification (March 2011), constituted a Madhya Pradesh 
Property Tax Board, in order to assist the local bodies to bring transparency in the process 
of levy/ collection of PT. The works allotted (November 2011) to the Madhya Pradesh 
Property Tax Board were to:  
• Review the prevailing process of assessment and imposition of PT and provide 
guidance to ULBs and State Government. 
• Provide guidance to State Government/ ULBs to bring transparency in the process of 
assessment of PT and ensure effective implementation. 
• Monitor and supervise the process of assessment, imposition and recovery of PT was 
as per rules. 
• Evaluate at least five per cent cases of PT, to prepare an annual work plan and publish 
a report in State Gazette. 
• Conduct training of revenue staff for their capacity building. 
• Provide suggestion on assessment of property tax on industrial properties. 
• Provide suggestions on the structure of various posts for revenue staff. 
During scrutiny of information gathered (Between June 2021 and September 2021) through 
Property Tax Board, Audit observed that: 
• There were no separate staff for the day-to-day functioning of the Property Tax Board. 
However, two officials on a contractual basis were looking after the functioning of 
the Board. This had probable bearing on the functions of the Property Tax Board as 
mentioned in the points below. 
• During the period of 2018 -19 to 2020 -21, one meeting of Property Tax Board was 
stated to be held in 2019 -20 only. In 2018 -19 and 2020 -21, meetings were not held 
due to the General Election 2019 and Covid-19 pandemic respectively. However, the 
minutes of the meeting were not furnished to Audit for verification. 
                                                 
47   NPs- (Bankhedi, Khategaon, Khilchipur, Mahugaon) and NPPs -(Shajapur, Tik amgarh, Bina and 
Pithampur). 
48   NPPs- (Bina, Tikamgarh, Shajapur) and NPs- (Khategaon, Khilchipur, Mahugaon and Bankhedi). 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
147 
 
• Board had not performed its mandatory duties and work, except in few cases where it 
had provided suggestions regarding rebate and levy of PT on the industrial properties. 
• Annual Work Plan was not prepared for 2018 -19, though the plans were prepared in 
2019-20 and 2020 -21. However, the same were not published in the State Gazette. 
Further, various activities of the annual work plans were also not performed at ULBs 
as well as at Directorate level. 
• No training programme was conducted during the period covered under audit except 
one online training programme relating to new rules on Property Tax during 2020-21 
for the revenue wing personnel of ULBs.  
Thus, in view of the above facts, it is evident that the constitution of the Board was a mere 
formality, and no concrete activity was undertaken by the Board to assist/ guide the ULBs 
in raising their PT revenue realising capabilities. Therefore, the objective of constitution of 
the Board could not be justified. 
On being pointed out in audit (June 2021), the  Deputy Director, UADD stated (September 
2021) that monitoring of work plan was being done by the Joint Directors at Division level, 
action was under consideration for recruitment of required staff and action regarding 
organisation of board meetings was un der process. Further, revenue staff were given 
continuous training by the training branch of the Directorate. No comment was offered by 
the Government in this regard. 
The replies of Deputy Director, UADD are not acceptable as compliance reports on the 
annual work plans were not available with the Department, therefore, it cannot be ascertained 
whether the activities of work plans were actually monitored. Further, the Board conducted 
only one online training programme and documentary evidence was not produce d to Audit 
to verify the veracity of the claim about continuous training given by the Directorate. 
5.2.5.8   Non-levy of PT on an open land/plots 
As per Section 132(1)(a) of the MPMC Act, 1956 and Section 127(1)(a) of the MPM Act, 
1961, PT was payable by the property owners on building or land located in ULB area on 
the basis of ALV/TPV.  
Audit observed PICs of two (NPP Tikamgarh and NP Bankhedi) ou t of 16 test -checked 
ULBs had passed a resolution for imposition of PT on open land/ plots. However, in 23 
cases, pertaining to these ULBs, PT of ` 0.12 crore was not imposed on open land/ plots. 
Further, NP Nalkheda did not pass any resolution for levy of PT on open land/ plots and PT 
was not levied on open land/plots, in this ULB.   
Thus, non-levy of PT on open land/plots resulted in non -levy of PT of ` 0.12 crore in the 
two ULBs (NPP Tikamgarh and NP Bankhedi), as detailed in Appendix 5.2.5.  
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that a resolution in this regard is pending 
with PIC in NPP Tikamgarh, while in case of NPs - Bankhedi and Nalkheda, no comment 
was offered. However, during the audit, CMO, NP, Bankhedi had stated (January 2022) that 
PT would be imposed on open land in future as per rules. CMO, NP, Nalkheda stated 
(September 2021) that the resolution could not be passed as PIC was in dissolved state since 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
148 
 
January 2019, this would be passed after constitution of the new Council. Replies indicated 
that concrete effort was lacking in this regard. Further, the reply of CMO, NP, Nalkheda is 
not acceptable as the resolution was not placed before PIC for approval for levy of PT on 
the open land/ plots since 1998. 
5.2.6   Rent 
As per Rule 2 (1) (q) MPMTIP Rule, 2016, the transfer of property means to confer the right 
to use of immovable property for certain period of time to one or more persons by the ULB 
under the condition as may be determined at premium and lease rent or as free hold. Further, 
Para 3(1) (2) of MPMTIP Rule, 2016 stipulates that ULB may grant lease of immovable 
property by inviting or without inviting tender.  
5.2.6.1   Premium of shops remained outstanding for recovery 
As per Rule 7 (2) of MPMTIP Rules, 2016, the whole amount of premium of shops should 
be remitted to the Government account within 120 days from the date of finalisation of the 
auction procedure. In the event of non -deposit of the amount of premium in the stipulated 
period, the Commissioner/ CMO shall notify that ‘the Lease is disqualified’ and tender shall 
be invited again. 
Audit observed (between June 2021 and January 2022) in six49 out of 16 test checked ULBs 
that against ` 22.87 crore, only ` 10.77 crore were deposited by the successful bidders even 
after the expiry from three to 875 days of the stipulated period of 120 days from the date of 
finalisation of the auction procedure. Thus, ` 12.10 crore against auctioned 94 shops were 
outstanding. Further, Audit also observed that the ULBs neither cancel led their allotments 
nor proceeded for the process of re -auction. Thus, due to inaction of the ULBs, amount of  
` 12.10 crore on account of the premium of 94 shops remained unrealised. The details of 
outstanding premium are given in Appendix 5.2.6. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that an amount of ` 1.33 crore had been 
recovered by three50 ULBs and recovery was in progress in remaining cases. Regarding NPP, 
Shajapur, it was stated that outstanding ` 43.00 lakh pertaining to 2020 -21 have been 
recovered. In the case of NN, Bhopal, it was stated that action was being taken under the 
MPMTIP Rules, 2016 and ` 61.08 crore had been recovered during 2021 -22. However, it 
was not informed that how much of this amount pertains to the period covered in audit, i.e., 
2018-19 to 2020-21. Further, in the case of NPP, Biaora, no comment was offered. However, 
during the audit, the CMO, NPP, Biaora had stated (August 2021) that Audit will be 
informed after taking necessary action. Further, progress of recovery wa s not informed to 
Audit by NPP, Biaora and reply of the Government regarding the amount recovered also 
needs further substantiation from records.  
5.2.6.2   Non-recovery of arrears of rent 
Section 173(1) of the MPMC Act, 1956 and Section 164(1) of MPM Act, 1961 stipulate that 
when any amount declared under the provisions of the Acts to be recoverable or payable on 
                                                 
49    NNs- (Bhopal and Chhindwara), NPPs- (Shajapur, Bina and Biaora) and NP Bankhedi. 
50  NN, Chindwara (` 1.10 crore), NPP, Bina (` 3.73 lakh), and NP, Bankhedi (` 19.48 lakh).

account of any tax, the Commissioner/ CMO shall, within the least practicable delay present 
a bill to the person liable for the payment. Further, as per Section 174(1) the MPMC Act, 
1956 and Section 164(3) the MPM Act, 1961 if the sum for which a bill is presented, is not 
paid and no objection has been preferred within 15 days from the presentation of the bill, the 
Commissioner/CMO serve upon the person to whom such bill has been presented a notice 
of demand.  
Further, Section 175(1) of the MPMC Act, 1956 and Section 167(1) of MPM Act, 1961, 
provide that the ULBs may recover the dues  by way of sale of the movable property of the 
defaulter or by order of attachment and sale of  immovable property if the person on whom 
a notice of demand is served does not within 30 51/ 1552 days of service of such notice pay 
the sum demanded or neither satisfies the commissioner/ CMO of the reason for non -

Audit observed (between June 2021 and January 2022) in 1253 out of 16 test checked ULBs, 
that an amount of ` 18.60 crore on account of rent of 3,396 shops had remained outstanding 
for recovery during the audit. In each of these cases, the arrears were more than ` 10,000. 
The ULBs neither recovered the arrears nor did they sale the immovable properties of the 
defaulter despite non-submission of reasons for non-payment of demand by the defaulters to 
the competent authority or non-filing of appeal against the demand in terms of the provisions 
of the Acts. Thus, inaction of the ULBs resulted in blockage of revenue of ` 18.60 crore as 
detailed in Appendix 5.2.7. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that an amount of ` 1.26 crore had been 
recovered by six 54 ULBs and recovery was under progress. In case of six 55 ULBs, it was 
stated that recovery was under progress. Regarding NN, Bhopal, it was also stated that an 
amount of ` 6.17 crore had been recovered during 2021 -22. However, it was not informed 
that how much of this amount pertains to the period covered in audit, i.e., 2018-19 to 2020-
21. Further, the reply regarding the amount recovered needs further substantiation from 
records. 
5.2.7   Monitoring Mechanism 
The Commissioner, UADD directed (May 2014) that CMOs of all ULBs will check self -
assessment returns of at least two big residential/ commercial properties along with at least 
five properties found under Geographical Information System (GIS)/ Multipurpose 
Household Survey (MHS) and monthly report of these sha ll be furnished by 10 th of every 
month to the Directorate.  
                                                 
51  Section 175(1) of the MPMC Act, 1956. 
52  Section 167(1) of MPM Act, 1961. 
53   NNs- (Bhopal and Chhindwara), NPPs - (Shajapur, Bina, Tikamgarh, Biaora and Mandideep) and 
NPs- (Nalkheda, Khilchipur, Khategaon, Bankhedi and Depalpur). 
54  NN, Chhindwara ` 1.01 crore, NPPs-(Mandideep ` 3.04 lakh and Bina ` 1.44 lakh, Shajapur ` 10.00 
lakh) and NPs- Bankhedi ` 0.27 lakh and Khilchipur ` 10.00 lakh).

Audit observed in five 56 out of 16 test  checked ULBs that the CMOs neither checked the 
self-assessment returns of residential/commercial properties and the properties found under 
GIS/ MHS nor furnished monthly reports thereon to the Directorate. Thus, due to the lack of 
monitoring of self-assessment returns, the accuracy in computation of the property tax could 
not be ensured. 
The Government did not offer any comment on this issue. However, during the Audit, the 
CMOs of respective ULBs had stated (between July 2021 and January 2022) that in futu re, 
action would be taken as per Rules. Further, Commissioner, UADD stated (March 2022) that 
checking of self-assessment returns was monitored at Directorate level as well as Division 
level. It was further stated that issues on PT, shop rent and other reve nue were being 
monitored through monthly and fortnightly video conferences at Division levels and the 
information regarding self -assessment returns would be made available to Audit after 
obtaining it from the ULBs concerned. 
However, despite the monitoring  as stated by the Commissioner, UADD,  there was non -
compliance of the  directions (May 2014) in the five aforesaid ULBs and there were 
deficiencies on issues relating to PT, rent as discussed in Paragraph 5.2.5.1 (Annual Letting 
Value), 5.2.5.3 (Arrears of PT), 5.2.5.6 (Self-Assessment), 5.2.6.1 (Premium of shops) and 
5.2.6. 2 (Arrear of rent) above. This indicated that the monitoring by the Directorate was not 
up to the desired level and needed further improvement. 
5.2.8   Conclusion 
As per the provision c ontained in MPMC Act, 1956 and MPM Act, 1961, the ULBs are 
empowered to self-govern by imposition of taxes or fees. Being the main source of revenue 
for ULBs, the increase in the PT revenue makes the ULB more self -dependent. However, 
during the period covered under Audit, it was seen that the ULBs did not recover ` 378.68 
crore as arrears of property tax, shop rent and premium of shops due to inaction of the ULBs 
in compliance of the laid-down procedure of recovery.  The instances of non -levy of PT of 
` 1.71 crore due on Board and Nigam office buildings had also been noticed. Besides, the 
Service Charge on the properties of Central Government had also not been levied. 
Irregularities were noticed related to acceptance of incomplete self -assessment returns 
without checking by competent authority. Thus, the accuracy of levy of PT could not be 
ensured. The functioning of the Property Tax Board was not as per the requirement of the 
13th Finance Commission recommendations. The above lapses indicated non -compliance 
with the provisions of Acts and Rules which impacted the revenue collection by the ULBs. 
5.2.9   Recommendations 
In view of above facts, the following Recommendations are required to be implemented by 
the Directorate of UADD: 
• The Directorate may consider strengthening the E -Nagar Palika Portal module to 
ensure the correct computation of ALV of the Properties by putting all the details in 
Portal.  
                                                 
56  NPPs- (Bina, Shajapur and Tikamgarh) and NPPs- (Bankhedi and Nalkheda). 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
151 
 
• The Directorate may consider strengthening the internal control and monitoring 
mechanism for ensuring timely issue of demand and action required to ensure 
recovery of due PT, rent and related amount.   
• The Directorate may consider strengthening the Property Tax Board to assist/ guide 
the ULBs in raising their Property Tax revenue realization capabilities. 
  

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
152 
 
5.3   Water Supply Management in Urban Local Bodies 
 
5.3.1   Introduction 
The 74th Constitutional Amendment made the Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) responsible for 
the administration and maintenance of Water Supply within the municipal limits. The 
financial and administrative control of ULBs in the State rests with the Directorate of Urban 
Administration and Development (UAD), headed by the Commissioner, under the Urban 
Development and Housing Department (UDHD). At the Municipal Corporation level, the 
Commissioner is assisted by Superintending Engineer (Water Supply), Executive Engineer, 
Assistant Engineer (AE), Sub-Engineer and other staff to discharge their duties for smooth 
water supply. The Municipal Council is headed by the Chief Municipal Officer (CMO) and 
the senior most engineer in the Municipal Council is in-charge of Water Supply.  
5.3.2   Audit Objectives 
The Compliance Audit is aimed at assessing whether:  
• Sufficient water is available and supplied to the consumers as per laid down norms; 
and 
• Drinking water and services of appropriate quality are being supplied to the 
consumers, in all the selected ULBs. 
5.3.3         Audit Criteria 
Audit criteria were sourced from the following: 
• Madhya Pradesh Municipal Corporation Act, 1956 and Madhya Pradesh 
Municipalities Act, 1961; 
• Central Public Health and Environmental Engineering Organisation 57 (CPHEEO) 
Manuals on Water Supply and Treatment, 1999 and Manual on Operation and 
Maintenance (O&M) of Water Supply System, 2005; 
• State Water Policy, 2003; 
• Specifications for Drinking Water (IS10500:2012) issued by Bureau of Indian  
Standards; and 
• Handbook on Service Level Benchmarks published by Ministry of Urban 
Development (MoUD), Government of India.  
5.3.4   Audit Scope and Methodology 
The Audit was carried out during June and November 2021 covering three-year period from 
2018-19 to 2020-21. Audit involved scrutiny of records of three selected ULBs viz. Gwalior 
Municipal Corporation (GMCn), Jabalpur Municipal Corporation (JMCn) and Vidisha 
Municipal Council (VMC) along with joint physical verification with the ULB authoriti es. 
                                                 
57  The Central Public Health and Environmental Engineering Organisation (CPHEEO) is the Technical 
Wing of the Ministry of Urban Development, Governmen t of India and deals with the matters related 
to Urban Water Supply and Sanitation including Solid Waste Management in the country.  

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
153 
 
Information was also collected from the office of the Principal Secretary, UDHD and the 
Commissioner, Directorate of UAD.  
Audit findings were sent to the Government in February 2022 for obtaining their views. The 
replies/views of Government were furni shed in November 2022, which have been suitably 
incorporated at appropriate place in the Report. 
5.3.5   Acknowledgement 
The Office of the Accountant General (Audit -II), Madhya Pradesh acknowledges the 
cooperation and assistance rendered by the officials of the selected ULBs during the audit. 
   Audit Findings 
Significant findings that emerged from this audit are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.  
5.3.6   Non-preparation of operation and maintenance plan 
As per para 2.4.2 of CPHEEO (O & M) Manual 2005, a comprehensive operation and 
maintenance plan shall be prepared to cover all the facilities. It is the responsibility of 
operational management headed by the Superintending Engineer to prepare operation and 
maintenance plans. 
On being enquired about preparation of a comprehensive operation and maintenance plan, 
all the test-checked ULBs intimated (July 2021, September 2021 and November 2021) that 
operation and maintenance plan has not been prepared. JMCn and VMC also intimated (July 
2021 and November 2021) that whenever required, the maintenance works were done by the 
staff of their Water Supply wing. The GMCn intimated (September 2021) that the 
supervisory staff were assigned duties of operation and maintenance and check lists were 
prepared for that purpose. However, documents to verify the same were not produced to the 
Audit.  
In reply the Government stated (November 2022), in the case of GMCn that the 
comprehensive operation and maintenance plan had been prepared every year and a Cell had 
been set up for operation and maintenance of Water Supply System under P ublic Health 
Engineering Department. Presently the work has been assigned to a Contractor. However, 
the reply was not supported by any documentary evidence in this regard. The reply was silent 
about preparation of comprehensive operation and maintenance plan in JMCn and VMC. 
5.3.7   Assessment of quantity of water required and distribution of   water 
Para 2.2.8.1 of CPHEEO Manual on Water Supply and Treatment, 1999 provides that piped 
water supplies for communities should provide adequately for domestic needs, institutional 
needs, public purposes, and industrial and commercial uses, etc. Audit observed the 
following: 
5.3.7.1   Non-assessment of institutional demand for water 
Table 2.1 of para 2.2.8.3 of CPHEEO Manual on Water Supply and Treatment, 1999 had 
prescribed different norms for the assessment of water required by the institutions like 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
154 
 
Hospitals (340 to 450 Litre  Per Capita per Day  (LPCD) per bed), Hostels and Boarding 
Schools/Colleges (135 LPCD), Schools/Colleges (45 LPCD), Restaurants (70 LPCD per 
seat), Cinema and Theatres (15 LPCD), etc.   
Thus, while assessing the demand of water the ULBs had to consider the institutional 
demand as well. Test-checked ULBs intimated (VMC – July 2021, GMCn – September 2021 
and JMCn – November 2021) that the actual need of water to institutions was not assessed 
by them.  
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in case of JMCn, 15 per cent non-
domestic demand was added under the AMRUT Phase -1 whereas in DPR for Phase -2, 
provision for institutional deman d was made. In the case of VMC, it was stated that 
institutional demand was considered only on a demand basis because additional requirement 
without demand would only increase expenditure. While in the case of GMCn, it was stated 
that assessment for institutional demand was done under the AMRUT Project -1. Reply 
confirms that all three ULBs had not done assessments on the basis of actual number of 
existing institutions as per the provisions in aforesaid CPHEEO Manual. Further, in GMCn, 
the institutional demand covered State and Central Government Institutions only. 
5.3.7.2 Huge difference between quantity of water received and   distributed 
due to water losses 
Para 2.6.5 of CPHEEO O&M Manual of Water Supply System, 2005 states that there are 
considerable losses between water produced and distributed which leads to reduction in the 
income of the utility. The manual also suggested that water audit should be done to get fairly 
accurate figures of the water production, water assessed, losses both physical and revenue. 
Such an audit could lead the ULBs to prioritise actions required to reduce these losses.   
On being enquired all the test -checked ULBs intimated ( VMC – July 2021; GMCn – 
September 2021 and JMCn – November 2021) that they have not conducted any water audit. 
Audit also observed that the test-checked ULBs did not have a procedure for measuring the 
quantity of water received, filtered and distributed in their network due to which they were 
unable to quantify the water losses in their network. 
According to Section 2.1.4 of Handbook on Service Level Benchmarks published by 
Ministry of Urban Development (MoUD), Government of India , the extent of water 
produced which does not earn the utility any revenue was termed as Non -Revenue Water 
(NRW). 
An analysis by Audit, considering that each Over Head Tank (OHT) was filled with potable 
water to its full capacity and the whole water was supplied to the inhabitants in one supply 
period, revealed considerable loss of water in test checked ULBs as detailed below:  
• GMCn purchases water required for th e purpose from the Water Resources 
Department, GoMP and the quantity of water as shown in the bills was considered 
as raw water received. Raw water thus received is treated in the three filtration plants 
having a combined capacity of treating 181 million litres per day (MLD)58 water.  Up 
                                                 
58  Tighra - 45 MLD; Motijheel Old - 68 MLD; and Motijheel New - 68 MLD.

(EE), Water Supply, GMCn intimated (September 2021) that water was supplied to 
the population every alternate day. Considering this, the total yearly storage capacity 
of the OHTs/Ground Level Service Reservoir (GLSR) was considered half in any 
respective year.

mention the basis on which the loss of water was enumerated. But in contradiction to the 
Government reply, EE, Water Supply, GMCn stated during the audit (November 2021) that 
GMCn had no records in respect of the quantity of water supplied in some of the wards. 
There is no Leakage Cell in the GMCn. Water leakage is possible as there are Canal leakages 
and other transmission losses and no flowmeter in filter plants to record the supply of water.

provide 135 litres of water per day for each person, as the total available capacity of filtration 
plants was 181 MLD only  which was very less in comparison to the population of GMCn. 
The EE, Water Supply, JMCn intimated (November 2021) that new OHTs and new lines 
had been laid under Atal Mission for Rejuvenation and Urban Transformation (AMRUT). 
The position of the water supply would change after proper functioning of the system during 
the year 2021-22.

158 
 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in case of JMCn, gap in 
recommended water and actually supplied water is due to population increase which was 
being minimized by implementation of new Schemes. Regarding GMCn, it was stated that 
the actual supply of water is between 161.65 to 189 LPCD which is more than the targeted 
water supply. However, as also mentioned in Para 5.3.7.2 above, the GMCn did not have 
records regarding water supplied, hence, it is not clear as to how the Department enumerated 
the quantity of water supplied. Further, the Government reply was silent regarding the issue 
in VMC.  
5.3.7.4   Zoning for equal distribution of water not done 
Para 10.2.3 of CPHEEO Manual on Water Supply and Treatment, 1999 envisages that 
zoning in the distribution system ensures equalisation of the supply of water throughout the 
area.  
The EEs, Water Supply of both GMCn and JMCn intimated (September and Novembe r 
2021) that zoning had not been done. The CMO, VMC (July 2021) stated that they had 
ensured equal distribution of water through OHTs.  
VMC did not have ward -wise population data for ascertaining the water required to be 
provided for the residents of the wards and water actually provided.   
On being pointed out, the EE, Water Supply, GMCn intimated (September 2021) that a 160 
MLD new filtration plant was being established at Jalalpur. After commissioning of the new 
plant, equal distribution to all would be ensured. The EE, Water Supply, JMCn also 
intimated (November 2021) that new OHTs were constructed under AMRUT and equitable 
distribution of water would be observed in future after proper operation of the new lines and 
OHTs.  
In reply, the Government stated  (November 2022) that in case of JMCn, Zoning for equal 
water distribution was being done gradually. As regards VMC, though population data was 
provided but the reply is silent about the equal water distribution through Zoning. In the case 
of GMCn, the Gov ernment stated that water supply level had been augmented through 
AMRUT Project and Ward No. 1-60 had been divided into 210 water supply zones for equal 
distribution of drinking water. However, the reply was silent about the six wards added in 
2014 and als o the equal distribution of water in all the water Zones could not be ensured 
through the process as some of the works were in progress (September 2022). 
5.3.7.5   Non-updating the maps and profile drawing of distribution system 
Para 8.4.2 of CPHEEO, O&M Manual of Water Supply System, 2005 envisages that the 
comprehensive maps of the distribution system which provide an overall view of the system 
with location of reservoirs, pumping stations, valve location maps and hydrants, etc. are to 
be prepared. Further, the maps are to be updated by conventional survey.  
No records regarding updating of maps and profile drawings by conducting conventional 
survey as envisaged in the CPHEEO O&M Manual of Water Supply System, 2005 were 
available in GMCn and JMCn for veri fication. The EE, Water Supply, GMCn intimated 
(September 2021) that all plans and profile drawings are updated from time to time through 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
159 
 
field survey. The EE, Water Supply, JMCn intimated (November 2021) that updation of 
profile drawing and distribution m aps would be done in future. Although the CMO, VMC 
provided (July 2021) copies of distribution maps prepared by the Contractor, yet it was not 
ascertainable whether these maps and profile drawing of the distribution system were being 
updated. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in the cases of JMCn and VMC, the 
work of updation of Maps were being taken up under new Projects whereas in case of GMCn, 
the maps had been prepared. However , the reply was silent about it s updatation b y 
conventional survey in GMCn as required under the Para 8.4.2 of CPHEEO, O&M Manual 
of Water Supply System, 2005.  
5.3.7.6   No Leakage Control Programme in existence 
Para 4.3.5 of the CPHEEO, O&M Manual of Water Supply Systems, 2005  states that the 
objective of leakage control programme is to reduce to a minimum the time that elapses 
between the occurrence of a leak and its repair.  
Audit observed that there was no laid down procedure for detecting visible and non -visible 
leakage in the test -checked ULBs . The CMO, VMC intimated (July 2021) that on 
verification of complaints from consumers regarding non-receipt of water or receipt of less 
water, leakage in the system is checked and attended to immediately. He also intimated that 
information of leakage is a lso gathered by valve man of ULB at the time of survey of the 
area during water supply time. He further intimated that 165 leakage cases were attended by 
the staff of the Water Supply wing during the years 2018-19 and 2020-21.   
GMCn and JMCn did not have the process of documentation of leakage cases in the 
distribution system (like date of receipt of information about leakage and the date on which 
they were attended, etc.) for monitoring the time lag in attending to the same.  During the 
years 2018-19 to 2020-21, GMCn and JMCn had spent ` 49.52 crore (GMCn: ` 5.67 crore; 
JMCn: ` 43.85 crore) on operation and maintenance works of distribution lines. In the 
absence of proper documentation, Audit could not ascertain the number of leakages that 
occurred during the period 2018-19 and 2020-21, and the dates on which these were attended 
to by GMCn and JMCn.  
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in the cases of JMCn, after complaints 
from consumers regarding less/non -receipt of water, leakage in the system is checked and 
attended to immediately. Further, old supply pipelines had been changed. Regarding GMCn, 
it was stated that PHED Divisions -1 and 2 are responsible for O&M of water system 
including leakage control, however, the reply was silent about the leakage control 
programme actually adopted for the purpose. In the case of VMC, no comment was offered.  
5.3.7.7   Households without water connections 
As per handbook of SLB of the MoUD , all the households are to be brought into the water 
connection network.  
Scrutiny of records revealed that only 1.45 lakh (46.03 per cent) of 3.15 lakh households in 
GMCn and 1.52 lakh (55.07 per cent) of 2.76 lakh households in JMCn had authorised water 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
160 
 
connections. Thus, 1.70 lakh households (53.97 per cent) in GMCn and 1.24 lakh households 
(44.93 per cent) in JMCn were out of water connection network. The percentage of coverage 
in VMC, was 73.02 per cent as 23,914 out of 32,751 households were having a uthorised 
water connections.   
The EE, Water Supply, JMCn intimated (November 2021) that surveys for identifying 
households without water connections were done from time to time and action to bring those 
households within the water connection network was made by organising special camps. The 
EE, Water Supply, GMCn intimated (September 2021) that public awareness programmes 
would be arranged to bring all households under the water connection network. VMC reply 
did not intimate action taken by them to bring a ll households under the water connection 
network. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in the cases of JMCn and VMC, 
efforts were being made to bring all household under water connection network. In the case 
of GMCn, it was stated that 80.37 per cent (1,99,341 out of 2,48,000 households) households 
are under water connection network and remaining households would be covered under 
AMRUT-2 Project. However, the huge reduction (about 0.67 lakh) in data about number of 
total households in GMCn b etween the dates of audit (September 2021 - 3.15 lakh) and as 
intimated by the Government (November 2022 - 2.48 lakh) creates doubt about the 
percentage of coverage of water connections. 
5.3.7.8   Water connections without meter 
Handbook of SLB of the MoUD  mentioned that cent per cent  metering of water supply 
connection is to be done. 
As per information gathered from the website (http://www.projectuday.gov.in) GMCn and 
JMCn had executed work orders for installation of meters for 1,00,000 and 1,55,200 
domestic consumers respectively, under the Asian Development Bank sponsored Project 
Uday. The EE, Water Supply, GMCn intimated (September 2021) that the meters were 
installed during the years 2010 and 2012 but were dismantled after some time as they were 
not wor king properly and are not in existence at present. The EE, Water Supply, JMCn 
intimated (November 2021) that water meters were installed only for bulk consumers. VMC 
intimated (July 2021) that no meter has been installed in the premises of domestic, 
commercial and industrial consumers.  
Test-checked ULBs were recovering water tax on the basis of fixed monthly rates. The fact 
remained that non -metering of water connections affected the revenue collection of the 
ULBs and it did not act as a deterrent against water wastage. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that the Department will make efforts in 
future to install water meters in these three ULBs.  
5.3.8   Quality of water supplied 
As per BIS specification IS10500:2012, drinking water used for human consumption shall 
comply with the requirement in respect of physical, chemical, biological and bacteriological 
parameters. 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
161 
 
5.3.8.1   Quality of supplied water not ensured 
State Water Policy 2003 envisages that the quality of surface and ground w ater shall be 
tested on a regular basis by the concerned department. As per para 2.2.9 read with para 15.1 
of CPHEEO Water Supply and Treatment Manual, 1999 the objective of the Water Works 
Management is to ensure that the supplied water is palatable and free from undesirable taste 
and odour for which laboratories (labs) with adequate facilities and manned by qualified 
personnel are essential.  The aim of laboratory examination of water is to ensure that potable 
water conforming to the drinking water standa rd is supplied to consumers. Annexure -17.1 
(Job description) of CPHEEO O&M Manual of Water Supply System, 2005 envisages that 
the responsibility of the Middle/Operational Management (Deputy Assistant to the 
Executive Engineer) is to monitor alum dosage and  chlorine dosage. Mainly four types of 
tests were to be conducted in the laboratory, i.e., physical, chemical, bacteriological and 
biological analysis, so that it prevents adverse effects on the health of the population.  
Audit observed that only seven 59 out of the 31 parameters 60 were being tested in the 
Motijheel lab of GMCn. As the samples were not tested for ascertaining the values of the 
remaining parameters, the quality of water supplied was not ensured. Thus, adverse effects 
on the health of the population cannot be ruled out. 
The EE, Water Supply, GMCn intimated (September 2021) that all parameters were not 
tested during 2018-21, as facilities for checking those parameters were not developed in the 
lab. 
Audit also observed that during the period 2018 -21, GMCn had collected 8,373 samples 
from different locations of the city for ascertaining their physical (2,791 samples), chemical 
(2,132 samples), bacteriological (659 samples) and biological (2,791) parameters. Out of 
these, results of 56 samples of phys ical analysis, eight samples of chemical analysis  
(alkanity 3, hardness 5) and 158 samples of bacteriological analysis (Coliform (MPN61) 157, 
faecal coliform 1) were found adverse. 
In JMCn, only Lalpur Filtration Plant had the facility for checking all the  required 
parameters. Test results of city samples were made available only for the period 20 January 
2020 to 2 September 2020 for scrutiny. During this period 188 samples were taken from 
different locations of the city, out of these 22 samples (15 samples in physical, five samples 
in bacteriological and two samples in both parameters) were found adverse. Action taken on 
the adverse samples by the EEs of GMCn and JMCn was not ascertainable in absence of 
records to verify them.  
The water testing lab of VMC was not functional due to the non-availability of chemist there. 
As such there was no system for regular checking of water samples. 
                                                 
59  Alkalinity, Chloride, Coliform (MPN), Hardness, Iron, pH and Turbidity,  
60  Commonly used in the format prepared by the State Research Lab, Bhopal for reporting the results 
of water samples tested. 
61  Most Probable Number (MPN) is used as a unit for counting of bacteria present in drinking water.

It is evident from the above table that during the calendar years 2018-20, a total of 2,91,020 
cases of water-borne diseases were reported in the test-checked ULBs. Thus, the possibility 
of contaminated water supplied by the ULBs during the period being th e cause of these 
reported cases, cannot be ruled out. The issues of supply of impure water in the areas under 
GMCn and JMCn were also reported in local newspapers as shown in Appendix-5.3.1. 
During the site visit of Ranjhi Filtration Plant (RFP) of JMCn, t he Audit party was guided 
by some residents of Ranjhi who had come to lodge complaint of impure water being 
supplied to them, to visit their area for verifying of pipelines going through drains there. The 
following images indicate the factual position: 
Image 5.3.1 and 5.3.2:  Ranjhi Manegaon, pipelines were found going through drains 
and residents of these areas were found complaining 
  
(Image:  Audit party, October 2021) 
The EE, Water Supply, GMCn  intimated (September 2021) that the distribution lines got 
damaged due to wear and tear as they were laid years back, and in some areas, distribution 
lines were parallel to the sewerage lines due to which the chances of sewage water getting 
into these damaged distribution lines could not be ruled out. The EE, Water Supply, JMCn 
intimated (November 2021) that officials from Water Supply wing verified the news report 
and took immediate action for rectifying the defects. It was also intimated that no proposal  
is under consideration for identifying areas where pipelines are going through drains. 


 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
163 
 
However, no records were available with JMCn for verifying the remedial action taken by 
them.In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in JMCn, water quality is being 
monitored in laboratory established in water treatment plant and work is in progress to shift 
pipelines going through drains. In VMC, the water testing laboratory is now in working 
condition and tests were being carried out, further, the samples are also being sent to PHED 
laboratory for testing. As regards GMCn, quality of water was being monitored as per the 
CPHEEO Water Supply and Treatment Manual, 1999. Further, it was also stated that the 
Report (data in Table 5.3.5) does not mention that the Diseases were result of water supplied 
by GMCn. However, the fact remains that the quality of water supplied was not ensured by 
the audited ULBs as reflected in tests they had conducted. 
5.3.8.2   Supply of bore well water without testing 
As per a report of CGWB the quality of ground water has undergone a change to an extent 
that the use of such water could be hazardous. An increase in overall salinity of the ground 
water and/or presence of high concentrations of fluoride, nitrate, iron, arsenic, total hardness 
and few toxic metal ions have been noticed in large areas in several states of India. These 
can cause problems like stomach pain, digestive problems, kidney stones, jaundice, typhoid, 
liver, heart and pancreatic damage.  
During test-check of r ecords in the test -checked ULBs, it was observed that as of March 
2021, 2,199 bore wells in GMCn, 2,981 bore wells in JMCn and five bore wells in VMC 
supplied water in their jurisdiction without any quality testing.  
Audit also observed that in GMCn (Motijheel Water Testing Laboratory,) during the period 
from April 2018 and March 2021 and, JMCn (Lalpur Water Testing Laboratory) during the 
period 21 January 2020 and 4 September 2020 (period for which record was produced), had 
collected 75 water samples from borewell (57 by GMCn and 18 by JMCn) from different 
locations of their jurisdiction, on the basis of complaints from its inhabitants. Out of the 57 
samples of GMCn, 16 samples were such in which either turbidity or coliform or both 
turbidity and coliform w ere not as per permissible limits prescribed under IS10500:2012.  
All the 18 bore well samples taken by JMCn also contained non-acceptable ranges of various 
parameters. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in JMCn, supplied bore well water is 
also used for purposes other than drinking. However, the reply is silent about regular testing 
in JMCn. As regards VMC, it was stated that testing is being done from time to time. Further 
the register relating to test results (for month August 2022) indicated that the testing is done 
after the issue raised in Audit. In the case of GMCn, it was stated that individual bore -well 
water was not being supplied to a particular lane. The bore-well water along with OHT water 
was supplied in Wards through the same  pipeline. The mixed water matches the quality 
parameter as prescribed in the CPHEEO Water Supply and Treatment Manual, 1999. 
However, the test results of water supplied from bore well was poor and both turbidity and 
coliform beyond permissible limits whic h was still reaching to the households despite 
mixing with OHT water. Further, the water quality of OHT water was also not adequate as 
discussed in Paragraph 5.3.8.1. 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
164 
 
5.3.8.3   Irregularities in use of Coagulant 
Para 5.4.1.3 of CPHEEO O&M Manual of Water Supply System, 2005 states that the 
selection of proper type and amount of coagulant to be added to the water to be treated  is 
very important. Overdosing as well as underdosing of coagulant may lead to reduced solids 
removal efficiency. This condition may be corrected by carefully performing jar tests.  
The observations on the use of coagulant noticed in the test-checked ULBs are discussed in 
the paragraphs below: 
• Regular testing of samples not done  
Para 5.3.6 of CPHEEO Water Supply and Treatment Manual, 1999 provides that samples of 
raw and treated water will be taken at regular intervals for analysis. Further, in large 
waterworks having their own laboratory, sampling is to be done daily.  
Audit observed that Bhongadwar Filtration Plant (BFP) of JMCn did not have a laboratory 
of its own, thus, water samples from this plant were being tested in labs of other filtration 
plants situated at Jabalpur (i.e., Lalpur or Ranjhi). Verification of water sample testing 
records for the period 21 January 2020 to 4 September 2020 (period for which records were 
produced), revealed gap of one to 137 days between one test and the subsequent test. 
Motijheel Water Testing Laboratory of GMCn had its own lab, however scrutiny of records 
revealed a gap of two to four days between conduct of one test and subsequent test 
(Appendix-5.3.2). 
The EE, GMCn intimated (September 2021) that regular tests would be conducted in future. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) regarding the issue in GMCn that quality 
of raw water for all the four water treatment plants was assessed regularly in the laboratory. 
But the fact remains that during the audit, there was a gap of two to four days between 
conduct of one test and subsequent test as per the records. Regarding the issue in JMCn, the 
reply was silent. However, during audit, the EE, Water Supply, JMCn intimated (November 
2021) that regular tests of water samples of BFP could not be done as they do not have their 
own laboratory.   
• Use of coagulant without jar test 
Para 5.4.1.4 of CPHEEO O&M Manual of Water Supply System, 2005 states that the jar test 
is still the most widely used method employed to evaluate the coagulation process and to aid 
the plant operator in optimising the coagulation, flocculation and clarification processes by 
using the correct doze of coagulant.   
Audit observed that the dosages of chemicals by BFP of JMCn and the filtration plant of 
VMC were not decided after conducting jar test. Thus, use of 2,56,885 kilogram (kg) alum, 
1,07,735 kg lime and 49,298 kg bleaching powder for water treatment by BFP during the 
period January 2019 and Mar ch 2021; and use of 28,693 alum bricks, 3,133.5 bags of 
bleaching powder and 2,421.5 bags and 2,080 kg of lime for water treatment during the years 
2018-19 and 2019-20 by VMC, was irregular.  

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
165 
 
The EE, Water Supply, JMCn intimated (November 2021) that the do sage of coagulant is 
decided by staff themselves on the basis of the results of water sample test. The CMO, VMC 
intimated (July 2021) that jar tests will be done in future.  
The Government reply (November 2022) is silent about the issue of use of coagulant without 
Jar test in VMC and JMCn. 
• Sufficient stock of coagulant not kept 
Para 7.3.3.2 of CPHEEO Water Supply and Treatment Manual, 1999 states that all plants, 
particularly small ones, should keep on hand at all times, a supply of chemicals sufficient to 
provide a safety factor. A storage of three months depending upon the location of the plant, 
source of supply, transport facilities and the arrangement with the suppliers for the supply 
of chemicals was advisable. 
Audit observed that BFP did not have stock  of alum for seven days 62, lime for 67 days 63, 
chlorine/bleaching powder for 101 days 64 between the period April 2018 and March 2021. 
Thus, during these days untreated water was distributed to the population.   
On being pointed out, the EE, Water Supply, JMCn  intimated (November 2021) that 
sometimes stock of coagulant got exhausted due to delay in completion of tender process.  
To avoid this situation, instructions would be issued to in-charge of filtration plants to initiate 
the tender process well in advance. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that expenditure in Water Treatment 
Plants/availability of storage is done as per requirement. However, sufficient stock of 
coagulant was not ensured by JMCn as Audit found that BFP in JMCn did not have sufficient 
stock of alum and chlorine/bleaching powder during varying period between April 2018 and 
March 2021. 
• Improper storage of coagulant 
Para 19.9.1 read with para 19.10.1 of CPHEEO O&M Manual of Water Supply System, 
2005 states that lime and alum should  be stored in a cool, clean and dry place as moisture 
has a tendency to cake these materials.

shown in Image 5.3.3 to 5.3.6 above in GMCn and JMCn both. Regarding the issue in VMC,

In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in JMCn, supply of pure drinking

regular water samples are tested in the laboratory to ensure pure water supply. In the case of 
GMCn, it was stated that collection of sample procedure may affect the result. Appropriate 
action had been taken by improving th e water supply management as well as testing 
frequency, however, the samples were collected in presence of an Assistant Engineer and a 
Sub Engineer of GMCn. Regarding the issue in VMC, the reply was silent. 
5.3.8.4 Biological analysis of sample of water an d silt/mud accumulated in 
OHTs not done at the time of cleaning of OHTs 
Para 7.3 of CPHEEO O&M Manual of Water Supply System, 2005 provides that sample of 
water and silt/mud accumulated in the tank is to be collected for biological analysis to see 
the presence of snails and worms. The test -checked ULBs informed (July 2021 – CMO, 
VMC, September 2021- EE, Water Supply, GMCn and November 2021 – EE, Water Supply, 
JMCn) in audit that samples of residual water and silt/mud were not taken at the time of 
cleaning of OHTs for its biological analysis.   
Thus, the provisions of CPHEEO O&M Manual of Water Supply System, 2005 for cleaning 
of OHTs were not followed in any of the test-checked ULBs. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in JMCn, cleaning of all water tanks 
is done every six months and records regarding cleaning have been maintained. In the case 
of GMCn, it was stated that the cleaning of OHT and other water tanks taken up regularly 
                                                 
66      Nephelometric Turbidity Unit (NTU) is the units of turbidity from a calibrated nephelometer.  

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
168 
 
on Pre-Monsoon and Post -Monsoon basis and as and when require d. Record of cleaning 
tanks of OHTs has been kept with the valve man and the Sub Engineer. Regarding the issue 
in VMC, it was stated that the work of cleaning the OHTs/reservoirs is done through the 
staff available in the water supply branch. However, the reply regarding all three ULBs was 
silent about taking samples of water and silt/mud accumulated in the tank for biological 
analysis to scientifically prove their contention about regular cleaning of OHTs. 
5.3.8.5   Storage of clear water by RFP and BFP of JMCn in damaged  sump 
wells 
During the site visit of RFP and BFP of JMCn, Audit observed that clear water (i.e. water 
after completion of the filtration process) was being stored in clear water sump wells which 
were in a highly depleted state  and required urgent repairs. Image -5.3.7 and 5.3.8 below 
depict the condition of both these sump wells as in the month of October and December 
2021, respectively.

Audit observed that a contract 67 was awarded by the JMCn under AMRUT scheme, which 
inter-alia included the work of repair and rehabilitation of both these sump we lls. The 
Physical Completion Certificate of this work was issued by the EE, Water Supply, JMCn on 
17 August 2021, stating that all works shown in the schedule have been completed on 31 
March 2021.   
The EE, Water Supply, JMCn intimated (November 2021) that  these sump wells were in 
damaged condition for a long time.  He also intimated that repair of these sump wells were 
taken up earlier too under various schemes but could not be done without interrupting water 
supply and that proposals for constructions of new sump wells would be forwarded under 
any new water supply schemes in future.  
The reply of the EE, Water Supply, JMCn contradicts the physical completion certificate of 
August 2021 issued by the same authority sufficiently substantiating the audit observation. 
The Government reply (November 2022) was silent on this issue.

5.3.9   Financial Management 
 
5.3.9.1 Compliance to Gazette Notification in respect of recovery of user 
charges for supply of water not made   
The guideline issued (March 2017) by the UDHD stipulates that user charges for supply of 
water should be decided in such a manner that expenditure inc urred on operation and 
maintenance is recovered from the user charges. Further, GoMP gazette notification 
(September 2020) also envisages to determine the rates of user charges for water supply, in 
such a manner that annual expenditure incurred on water supply services are recovered cent 
per cent. The procedure of determination of rates is also prescribed in paragraph 4 of GoMP 
gazette notification (September 2020).  
Audit observed that compliance to the UDHD guideline and gazette notification was not 
being done by the test -checked ULBs, and user charges for use of water was levied at the 
ad-hoc rate of ` 150 per month by GMCn, ` 170 per month by JMCn and ` 100 per month 
by VMC. Water user charges levied on flat rate basis by the ULBs resulted in non -
compliance of the Government instructions and acted as a disincentive for consumers to 
conserve water. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in JMCn, the user charges were fixed 
by the Municipal Corporation and sent to the Government for approval. But due to the 
Government's ban on implementing the new rate, the recovery was not done. In the case of 
VMC, expenditure statement of O&M was enclosed with the reply, but nothing was stated 
on the compliance to Gazette Notification ibid. In the case of GMCn, it was stated that the 
Municipal Corporation will follow the guideline for recovery of water user charge as per the 
procedure laid down in Gazette Notification. The Reply corroborates audit observation. 
5.3.9.2   Low recovery efficiency of water charges 
Section 225 of the MP Municipal Corporation Act, 1956 stipulates that the arrears of the 
water charges are to be recovered from consumers and in case of non -recovery the water 
connection should be disconnected. Further, the Madhya Pradesh Municipalities Act, 1961, 
Section 127 (1) (b) read with sections 164 and 165 provides that when any amount is declared 
to be recovered, the Chief Executive Officer may cause to be served upon the person liable 
for the payment, a notice of demand and that if such person does not pay the sum demanded 
in the notice, the amount may be recovered on the application of the Council to a Magistrate, 
and such Magistrate may order recovery by distress and sale of any movable property or 
attachment and sale of immovable property belonging to such person.  
The year -wise details of the demand, recovery and the percentage of recovery of water 
charges in test-checked ULBs is shown in Table-5.3.7.

It is evident from the above table that recovery of water charges in GMCn ranged between 
78.08 per cent (2018-19) and 53.61 per cent (2019-20). The efficiency in collection of these 
charges in JMCn ranged between 16.9 5 and 20.61 per cent during the years 2018 -19 and 
2020-21. Similarly, the efficiency achieved by VMC ranged between 10.73 per cent and 
10.83 per cent during the years 2019-20 and 2020-21. Information of demand, recovery and 
balance of water tax for the year 2018-19 was not provided by VMC. 
The amounts outstanding for  recovery on account of water charges were ` 16.93 crore in 
GMCn, ` 126.20 crore in JMCn, and ` 18.81 crore in VMC as of March 2021. Action for 
recovery of dues as per provisions of Madhya Pradesh Municipal Corporation Act, 1956 and 
Madhya Pradesh Municipalities Act, 1961 were not taken by the test checked ULBs. 
In reply, the Government stated (November 2022) that in JMCn and GMCn, continuous 
efforts have been made to recover the water user charges from the consumers. In the case of 
VMC, it was stated that water user charge is being recovered by organising special 
campaigns/camps in the city. But progress of recovery of water charges indicates 
inefficiency of these three ULBs. 
 
 
 
 
 

 
Chapter V: Compliance Audit 
171 
 
5.3.10   Conclusion 
The test-checked ULBs had not conducted water audit for having fairly accurate figures of 
water production, water assessed, losses (both physical and revenue), due to which they were 
unable to assess the exact quantity of raw water received for filtration,  water filtered, water 
received at OHTs and water distributed to consumers. Estimation carried out by Audit 
considering the storage capacity of available OHTs and the schedule of Water Supply 
followed by the ULBs revealed NRW ranging from minimum 18 per ce nt (VMC) to 
maximum 78 per cent (GMCn).    
CHPEEO Manual on Water Supply and Treatment, 1999 prescribes the norm of providing 
135 LPCD water to all, but maximum achievement by the ULBs as assessed by Audit was 
35 LPCD (in 2020 -21) by GMCn; 98 LPCD (in 2018 -19) by JMCn and 100 LPCD  
(in 2018-19) by VMC. Test-checked ULBs had not ensured zoning in their water supply 
network for ensuring equal distribution of water to all.     
There was no program for detecting visible and non -visible leakages in the ULBs due to 
which considerable expenditure was incurred on operation and maintenance works of 
distribution lines.   
In the three ULBs, 2.98 lakh (47.76 per cent) households were still out of water connection 
network. Water meters were not installed at the premises of consumers.  As such water tax 
was being recovered on flat rate basis which did not act as a deterrent against water wastage. 
Test-checked ULBs were supplying water obtained from borewells, to its inhabitants without 
testing. Supply of bore well water wi thout testing, could be hazardous for public health. 
Cases of use of coagulant without ascertaining the required dose by jar test, non -use of 
coagulant due to exhaust of stock , improper storage  in the test -checked ULBs were also 
observed. Samples of silt/ mud accumulated in the tanks to be collected for biological 
analysis, at the time of cleaning of OHTs to observe the presence of snails and worms, were 
not done by the ULBs. Independent water quality testing conducted by Audit revealed that 
out of 46 sampl es, reports of 21 samples showed presence of faecal coliform against the 
prescribed zero count.   
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

 
Audit Report on Local Bodies for the year ended 31 March 2021 
172 
 
5.3.11   Recommendations 
In view of above findings, the following recommendations are required to be implemented 
by the UAD: 
• Water audit may be conducted to ascertain the quantity of water filtered, water 
distributed, and losses both in physical and revenue terms.  
• A robust leakage control programme may be prepared and implemented to minimise 
the time that elapses between the occurrence of leaks and repairs thereof. 
• All households may be brought under the water connection network, water metering 
of all connections may be ensured, and water user charges levied accordingly.  
• Testing of samples of water extracted from borewell for s upply may be conducted 
invariably.

Appendix-2.1.8 
(Reference- paragraph 2.1.11 Page No. 45) 
Details of power and responsibilities assigned to officials of PRIs 
Sl. 
No. 
Name of officials Power and Responsibility assigned 
1 Chief Executive 
Officers of ZP/JP 
As per Madhya Pradesh Panchayat (Powers and Functions of Chief 
Executive Officer) Rules, 1995, 
• Supervision and control the officers and servant of the Panchayat; 
• Convene the meeting of Panchayat and Standing Committee and maintain 
proceeding thereof. 
• Custodian of all papers and documents connected with proceedings 
of meeting of Panchayat and its Standing Committees; 
• May call any documents information, return, statement or report from any 
officer holding office under t he Panchayat or from any other district office 
of any department. 
• shall supervise and control the execution of all activities of the Panchayat.  
• Initiate proceeding, civil or criminal on behalf of Panchayats. 
• prepare annual development plans and budget and ensure efficient execution 
of schemes included for all matters relating to the accounts of Panchayat. 
• The Chief Executive Officer shall also exercise all such powers and perform 
such other functions and duties as may be delegated to him by Panchayat or 
assigned by the Stale Government. 
2 Technical assistance 
and Execution 
• To prepare plans for execution of schemes/programme. 
•  Prepare estimates and issuance of technical sanctions. 
•  To execute the works as per sanctions by executing agencies i. e. GPs or 
other agencies.  
• Supervision and valuation of works.

No
.

Panchaya

Appendices 
211 
 
3 Accounting and record 
keeping 
• To prepare and maintained all relevant records as envisages in Accounting 
rule of PRIs, to prepare all accounts and produce to audit and present all the 
accounts, budget, development plans in executive body of PRIs. 
4 
 
Secretary of the 
Panchayats 
• Circular issued by PRD dated 28, April 2011 envisages the responsibilities 
of Panchayat Secretary. 
• For conducting Gram Sabha, to decide date, time and place, intimate to the 
members and try to fulfil the quorum, registered the proceeding and get it 
approved in next Gram Sabha. 
• To take appropriate action on the proposal approved in GS and copy of the 
same send to JP. 
• Intimate the CEO JP and Collector regarding action taken under sec. 39 of 
PRI Act against Panch/ Sarpanch. 
• To prepare agenda with the consent of Sarpanch and intimate to all Panch. 
• Work to perform all the functions assigned to the Gram Sabha. 
• To give permission for construction of houses. If found illegal take 
appropriate action as per provisions of the Act 
• Take action against encroachment and fined to such people.   
• To safe custody of amount of GP, open the account in Post office/ bank by 
joint signature of Sarpanch/ Secretory. 
• Before completion of financial year, arrange the annual meeting and present 
the income expenditure of fund received under central and state sector 
schemes and budget for next financial year. 
• To prepare budget related to various schemes and get it approved by GS.  
• To present requisite records for audit.  
• To prepare annual account of GP in time and send to JP before 5 th of next 
month. 
• To prepare list of all taxpayers and levy the tax as per the provisions of Act. 
• As per the provisions of the Act, to produce all records and information for 
conducting audit.  
• As per MoPR  requirement, accounts of the GPs will be maintained in 
formats of PRIA soft. 
(Source: PRI Act and Rules thereunder)
